Dependent prepositions: noun patterns#

The prepositions that a noun drags along behind it — a reason for, a solution to, an increase in — chosen not by the writer but by the noun itself.

← The grammar of prepositions · All categories

Everywhere else in this dictionary a preposition is a word with a meaning, and you pick it because of what you want to say. Here it is a piece of another word's spelling. Solution takes to, cause takes of, increase takes in, and no fact about solutions, causes, or increases explains why — the pairing is arbitrary, historical, and simply has to be learned with the noun, the way a French learner learns that table is feminine. Nothing in this file is a choice, which is why the entries below read as a list of facts rather than a set of distinctions, and why the rest of the dictionary — the core three, the core relational six, place, movement, time, and the categories after them — treats prepositions as words in their own right, with meanings you can reason about.

Its companions are dependent prepositions: verb patterns and dependent prepositions: adjective patterns. Take the three together: the same root often takes one preposition as a verb, a second as a noun, and a third as an adjective, and the shift between them is where fluent writers slip.

The noun-versus-verb shift#

The most productive error in this file. English routinely gives a noun a different preposition from the verb it comes from — and, worse, gives a preposition to the noun when the verb takes none at all. Discuss is a bare transitive verb (we discussed the budget, never ✗ we discussed about the budget), but the noun needs a preposition to attach anything (a discussion about the budget). The verb form is the one learners over-generalize from, so the ✗ forms below are the ones that survive into advanced writing.

Verb (what it takes)Noun (what it takes)The error to kill
discuss something — no prepositiona discussion about / of something✗ discuss about the budget
marry someone — no prepositionher marriage to someone✗ marry with him, ✗ married with her
lack something — no prepositiona lack of something✗ he lacks of experience
enter a room — no prepositionentry into a room✗ enter into the room (literal sense)
answer a question — no prepositionthe answer to a question✗ the answer of the question
approach the city — no prepositionan approach to the problem✗ approach to the city (as a verb)
emphasize the risk — no prepositionthe emphasis on the risk✗ emphasize on the risk
request a copy — no prepositiona request for a copy✗ request for a copy (as a verb)
attend the meeting — no prepositionattendance at the meeting✗ attend to the meeting (different sense)
reach the summit — no prepositionwithin reach of the summit✗ reach to the summit
resemble his father — no prepositiona resemblance to his father✗ resembles to his father
contact the office — no prepositioncontact with the office✗ contact with the office (as a verb)
protest the ruling — no preposition (American)a protest against the rulingprotest against the ruling is British, not an error

And the shift runs the other way just as often, where both forms take a preposition but not the same one:

VerbNounNote
believe in ita belief in itsame — the easy case
depend on itdependence on itsame
reply to ita reply to itsame — reply keeps to in both, unlike answer
compare X with Ya comparison with / to Yverb prefers with, noun takes either
substitute X for Ya substitute for Ythe verb reverses the roles — see substitute for
search for a cure / search a housethe search for a curesearch is transitive in one sense and prepositional in the other
benefit from itthe benefit of itfrom on the verb, of on the noun

The complete list of zero-preposition verbs has a section of its own at the end of this file.

Reason for, reason why, reason that#

Four frames, three of them fully standard in American English and one of them a genuine error.

  • the reason for + noun phrase or -ing — the reason for the delay, the reason for postponing the vote. The preposition frame. ✗ the reason of the delay.
  • the reason why + clause — the reason why she left. Old, standard, and used continuously in edited American prose since Middle English. The claim that it is redundant is a nineteenth-century invention; ignore it. It is heaviest in emphatic and rhetorical writing (that is the reason why).
  • the reason that + clause, or with nothing at all — the reason that she left, the reason ø she left. Both are lighter than why and slightly more formal in edited prose. The bare version is the commonest in speech.
  • the reason to + infinitive — a reason to leave early. Not the same thing: this is a justification for a future act, not an explanation of a past one. Compare the reason for leaving early (why it happened) with a reason to leave early (why you should).
  • ✗ the reason is because — the error. Because already means "for the reason that," so the reason is because he was sick says it twice. Write the reason is that he was sick, or drop the frame: he was sick. In speech nobody notices; in writing an editor will.

Full entry: reason for.

A way to do it against a way of doing it#

Both are correct. They are not identical, and neither one is the safe default.

  • way to + infinitive — a means of reaching a goal. There has to be a way to fix this. That's not the way to talk to a customer. It answers "how do I get the result I want?"
  • way of + -ing — a manner, habit, or characteristic style. He has a way of turning every conversation into an argument. It was her way of saying thank you. It answers "what is this person's or thing's manner?"
  • The overlap is real: a way to make bread and a way of making bread are both fine and mean nearly the same thing. The split shows up at the edges — you cannot say ✗ a way to turning every conversation into an argument, and her way to say thank you sounds wrong because it is a manner, not a method.
  • ✗ a way for doing it and ✗ a way of do it are both errors. After of the verb takes -ing; after to it takes the bare form.

Full entry: way of/to.

The to trap#

Half the errors in this file come from one ambiguity: the letters t-o spell two different words. They are told apart by one test — put a plain noun in the slot.

  • If a noun fits, to is a preposition, and a verb after it must be an -ing form: an objection to the fee → an objection to being asked, never ✗ an objection to be asked. Same for an alternative to driving, an obstacle to finishing, the key to solving it, opposition to raising taxes, resistance to being told, with a view to selling.
  • If a noun does not fit, to is the infinitive marker, and a bare verb follows: a willingness to compromise (✗ a willingness to the compromise, ✗ a willingness to compromising). Same for permission to leave, a chance to speak, a decision to resign, the right to remain silent, a tendency to overreact.
  • A few nouns take both, with different meanings. A chance of winning is a probability; a chance to win is an opportunity. An attempt at humor is a try at a thing; an attempt to be funny is a try at an act. Permission for the trip is a thing permitted; permission to travel is an act permitted. And one noun takes both spellings of to with no change of meaning at all: a commitment to reducing emissions and a commitment to reduce them are equally standard, which is exactly why the adjective committed to reducing — gerund only — catches people out.

How to read the entries#

Every entry names the pattern, not the bare noun, because the pattern is the unit you have to memorize. The Complement line is the one to read twice: it says what can follow the preposition and, more usefully, what cannot. The Contrast line is where the meaning-changing swaps live — concern about against concern for, interest in against interest on, taste for against taste in. Weak forms are given because the preposition in these patterns is almost never stressed: a solution to it comes out /ə səˈluʃən tə ɪt/ in any normal sentence, and stressing the to would sound like a correction.

A note on the American forms used throughout: toward and afterward without the -s; different from (with different than accepted before a clause and different to marked British); talk with alongside talk to; on the weekend, not at the weekend; write me, not only write to me; protest something transitively, where British English says protest against something.

The entries run alphabetically by headword. Where one noun takes two or three genuinely different prepositions, they share a single entry — advantage of/over, concern about/for, taste for/in — because the choice between them is the thing actually worth learning. The verbs that take no preposition at all are gathered in a section of their own at the end.

A#

access to#

Pronunciation
/ˈæksɛs tu/ · AK-ses too · weak /ˈæksɛs tə/
Русский
доступ к — право или возможность войти, дойти, воспользоваться
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (access to funding, access to editing the master file) — never a to-infinitive (✗ access to edit the file, unless to edit is a purpose clause after a complete phrase) and never a that-clause
Pattern
have / gain / get / deny / grant access to [noun phrase] · access to [someone] · give [someone] access to [noun phrase]
Contrast
Access for names the beneficiary, not the thing reached: access to the building is what you can enter, access for wheelchair users is who gets in. The verb access takes a direct object and no preposition — she accessed the file, never ✗ she accessed to the file. Note also the adjective split: accessible to people (they can use it) against accessible by road (that is the route).

The right or ability to reach, enter, or use something.

  1. Only three people have access to the payroll system.
  2. The new ramp gives access to the second floor.
  3. Rural counties still lack reliable access to broadband.
  4. Her lawyer was denied access to the case file for two weeks.
  5. Interns get access to the archive but not to the vault.
  6. Nothing in the settlement guarantees access to editing the original recordings.
See also
enter something, limit to/on, permission for, to

adherence to#

Pronunciation
/ədˈhɪrəns tu/ · ad-HEER-unss too · weak /ədˈhɪrəns tə/
Русский
приверженность чему-л., соблюдение чего-л. — верное следование правилу или режиму
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — policy, medicine, and compliance writing
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (adherence to logging every deviation) — never a to-infinitive (✗ adherence to log every deviation) and never a that-clause (✗ adherence that the rules be followed). The to here is a preposition, so a verb after it must end in -ing.
Pattern
adherence to [a rule / a standard / a schedule / a regimen] · strict / rigid / close / slavish adherence to [noun phrase] · in adherence to [noun phrase] (formal) · [someone's] adherence to [a party / a doctrine]
Contrast
Adherence is sticking to a rule; adhesion is sticking to a surface. Adherence to the protocol is following it; the adhesion of the coating to the steel is physical. In American medicine adherence to a drug regimen has largely displaced compliance with it, on the theory that patients cooperate rather than obey — compliance with survives in the regulatory sense. The verb is adhere to (✗ adhere with), and the adjective is adherent to. ✗ adherence of the rules and ✗ adherence with the policy are both errors.

Faithful, continued observance of a rule, a standard, or a course of treatment.

  1. Payment depends on strict adherence to the reporting schedule.
  2. Only about half the patients showed good adherence to the six-month regimen.
  3. The audit found no adherence to the written change-control policy at all.
  4. Her adherence to the party line surprised people who had known her in law school.
  5. Certification requires adherence to documenting every deviation, however small.
  6. Adherence to the posted speed limit on that stretch is essentially voluntary.
See also
compliance with, conformity with/to, deference to, comply with, in accordance with, to

advantage of/over#

Pronunciation
/ədˈvæntɪdʒ ʌv/, /ədˈvæntɪdʒ ˈoʊvɚ/ · ad-VAN-tij uv, ad-VAN-tij OH-ver · weak /ədˈvænɪdʒ əv/ → /ədˈvænɪdʒ ə/; over has no weak form
Русский
преимущество: of — выгода от чего-л., over — превосходство над соперником
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: noun phrase or gerund (the advantage of arriving early) — never a to-infinitive (✗ the advantage to arrive early). over: a noun phrase naming a rival or a competing option, never a gerund (✗ an advantage over arriving early).
Pattern
the advantage of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · an advantage over [competitor] · take advantage of [noun phrase] · have the advantage of [noun phrase] · to [someone's] advantage
Variants
the advantages to doing it this way — an accepted informal American variant of the advantage of, but of is the edited form
Contrast
The two prepositions are not interchangeable. The advantage of a hybrid is the good thing a hybrid gives you; an advantage over a hybrid is the way something beats a hybrid. Mixing them produces nonsense: ✗ the advantage over arriving early has no rival to compare with. Take advantage of is fixed and has a second, negative sense — exploiting a person — that take the advantage of does not carry. ✗ advantage on and ✗ advantage above are errors.

A benefit something gives you, or a way in which one thing beats another.

  1. The main advantage of leasing is that repairs aren't your problem.
  2. Solar panels have a clear advantage over a diesel generator in the summer.
  3. She had the advantage of having read the report the night before.
  4. Don't let the contractor take advantage of your father.
  5. The rule change works to the advantage of smaller schools.
  6. Our only advantage over the incumbent is that we can move faster.
See also
benefit of/to, disadvantage of, comparison with/to, of, over

advice on/about#

Pronunciation
/ədˈvaɪs ɑn/, /ədˈvaɪs əˈbaʊt/ · ad-VYSS on, ad-VYSS uh-BOWT · weak /ədˈvaɪs ən/; about clips to /ˈbaʊt/ in fast speech
Русский
совет по поводу чего-л. — в русском исчисляемо, в английском нет (✗ an advice)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (advice on choosing a plan), or a wh-clause (advice on what to do, advice on how to file) — never a that-clause (✗ advice that you should wait; write advice to wait). Advice to + infinitive is possible and names the content of the advice: his advice to sell was wrong.
Pattern
advice on / about [noun phrase / verb-ing / wh-clause] · advice to [person] · advice to [verb] · give / take / follow / ignore advice · a piece of advice
Variants
advice on — the default for a domain or procedure · advice about — slightly more conversational and more usual with a specific worry
Contrast
Advice is uncountable in American English: ✗ an advice, ✗ some advices, ✗ many advices. Count it with a piece of advice, two pieces of advice, or some advice. Do not confuse the noun advice /ədˈvaɪs/ with the verb advise /ədˈvaɪz/ — the c form is the noun. The verb takes a person as a direct object with no preposition (she advised me, ✗ she advised to me) and takes on for the topic (she advised us on the merger).

A recommendation about what someone should do.

  1. I need advice on whether to refinance now or wait.
  2. She gave me some good advice about handling difficult clients.
  3. The website has advice on choosing a health plan.
  4. His only advice to new teachers was to learn every name in the first week.
  5. Take my advice and get it in writing.
  6. That's the worst piece of advice I've heard all year.
See also
information about/on, opinion on/of/about, knowledge of/about, on, about

agreement with/on#

Pronunciation
/əˈɡrimənt wɪθ/, /əˈɡrimənt ɑn/ · uh-GREE-munt with, uh-GREE-munt on · weak /əˈɡrimənt wəθ/, /əˈɡrimənt ən/
Русский
соглашение с кем-л. / о чём-л.; также согласие с кем-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
with: a person, party, or institution. on / about: a topic — noun phrase, gerund (agreement on postponing the vote), or wh-clause (no agreement on who pays). Agreement also takes a bare that-clause with no preposition (an agreement that he would leave) and a to-infinitive (an agreement to split the costs).
Pattern
an agreement with [party] · an agreement on / about [issue] · an agreement to [verb] · an agreement that [clause] · reach / sign / break an agreement · in agreement with [someone]
Variants
agreement on — an issue settled · agreement about — looser and more conversational · agreement between [two parties] — from the outside, naming both sides
Contrast
Three frames with three jobs: an agreement with the union names the other side, an agreement on wages names the subject, and an agreement between the city and the union names both sides from outside. The related verb splits the same way and is a classic error zone: agree with someone (share their view), agree to a proposal (accept it), agree on a date (settle it jointly), agree that... — and ✗ agree something is not American English, though British writers use agree a deal.

A settlement between parties, or the state of holding the same view.

  1. The company signed a five-year agreement with the pilots' union.
  2. We never reached agreement on who would pay the deductible.
  3. There's broad agreement about the diagnosis and none at all about the treatment.
  4. They came to an agreement that neither side would talk to reporters.
  5. I'm in agreement with you on almost everything except the timeline.
  6. The agreement between the two districts expires in June.
See also
disagreement with/about, opposition to, difference between/in, with, agree with, agree to, agree on, on

alternative to#

Pronunciation
/ɔlˈtɜrnətɪv tu/ · awl-TUR-nuh-tiv too · weak /ɔlˈtɜrnətɪv tə/
Русский
альтернатива чему-л. — то, что можно выбрать вместо
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (an alternative to driving) — never a to-infinitive. ✗ an alternative to drive downtown is the single commonest error with this word, because the to looks like an infinitive marker and is not. Test it: a noun fits the slot (an alternative to the car), so to is a preposition and the verb takes -ing.
Pattern
an alternative to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · have no alternative but to [verb] · the only alternative is to [verb] · offer / propose / seek an alternative to [noun phrase]
Contrast
Alternative to names what you would be replacing; there is no ✗ alternative for, ✗ alternative of, or ✗ alternative from. Two other frames use a different to: in no alternative but to wait and the only alternative is to wait, the to really is an infinitive marker, which is why the bare verb is right there and wrong after an alternative to. Purists once insisted alternative could apply to exactly two options; American usage abandoned that long ago and three alternatives is unremarkable.

Something you can choose or use instead of something else.

  1. Biking is a serious alternative to driving in a town this flat.
  2. We're looking for an alternative to the current supplier.
  3. Is there any alternative to filing an appeal?
  4. Community college was the only affordable alternative to a four-year school.
  5. She had no alternative but to resign.
  6. The board rejected all three alternatives to the merger.
See also
substitute for, objection to, preference for, solution to, to

amendment to#

Pronunciation
/əˈmɛndmənt tu/ · uh-MEND-munt too · weak /əˈmɛndmənt tə/
Русский
поправка к — официальное изменение уже действующего документа
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — legislatures, contracts, bylaws, court filings
Complement
either a noun phrase naming the document amended (an amendment to the lease) or a bare infinitive naming what the amendment would do (an amendment to strike the residency requirement) — the two to's are different words and both are standard. Never a gerund (✗ an amendment to striking the requirement) and never a that-clause (✗ an amendment that the fee be capped; write an amendment capping the fee).
Pattern
an amendment to [a bill / a contract / the constitution] · an amendment to [verb] · propose / offer / attach / adopt / defeat an amendment · an amendment banning [noun phrase] · the First / Fifth / Fourteenth Amendment (usually cited bare)
Contrast
The two to's are the whole difficulty. An amendment to the lease attaches to a document, and a noun follows; an amendment to cap the fee says what it does, and a bare verb follows — put a plain noun in the slot to tell them apart. Amendment of is the act of amending and is stiffer and rarer: amendment of the bylaws requires a two-thirds vote, but the sheet of paper is an amendment to the bylaws. ✗ an amendment on the bill is an error. A numbered constitutional amendment is normally cited bare (the Fourth Amendment), with to the Constitution added only in full formal citation.

A formal change proposed or made to a document already in force.

  1. The committee attached an amendment to the highway bill at two in the morning.
  2. We signed a one-page amendment to the lease so the dog could stay.
  3. She offered an amendment to strike the residency requirement.
  4. The amendment to the bylaws needed a two-thirds vote of the full membership.
  5. The Twenty-Sixth Amendment to the Constitution lowered the voting age to eighteen.
  6. Every amendment to the settlement has to be filed with the court within ten days.
See also
appendix to, exhibit to/schedule to/annex to, waiver of, notice of/to, change in/of, to

analogy to/with#

Pronunciation
/əˈnælədʒi tu/, /əˈnælədʒi wɪθ/ · uh-NAL-uh-jee too, uh-NAL-uh-jee with · weak /əˈnælədʒi tə/, /əˈnælədʒi wəθ/
Русский
аналогия с — сходство, проводимое ради объяснения или довода
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; heaviest in law, philosophy, and science writing
Complement
to / with: a noun phrase or gerund (an analogy to boiling a frog slowly). between: two things joined by and. Never a to-infinitive (✗ an analogy to boil a frog) and never a that-clause (✗ an analogy that the market is a machine).
Pattern
an analogy to / with [noun phrase / verb-ing] · an analogy between X and Y · draw / make / press an analogy · by analogy to / with [noun phrase] · the analogy breaks down
Variants
analogy to and analogy with are both standard American English — to is commoner in legal and mathematical writing, with in general prose. Analogy between is not a third alternative but a different frame: it needs two named terms.
The adjective takes only one preposition
analogous to, and ✗ analogous with is not the edited form even though it is heard. Against similarity to/between: a similarity is a fact about two things, an analogy is an argument you build out of one — which is why an analogy can be good, strained, or false, and a similarity cannot. Watch the stress, which moves across the family: analogy /əˈnælədʒi/, analogous /əˈnæləɡəs/, but analog /ˈænəlɔɡ/ and analogical /ænəˈlɑdʒɪkəl/.

A likeness drawn between two things in order to explain or argue from one to the other.

  1. He drew an analogy to the way a fever burns off an infection.
  2. The analogy with the 1970s only goes so far, and it stops at the oil price.
  3. There's a rough analogy between a watershed and a circuit board.
  4. By analogy to the earlier case, the court found no duty of disclosure.
  5. The analogy to a household budget is the one every politician reaches for, and it is wrong.
  6. Her analogy with chess made the whole strategy suddenly obvious.
See also
comparison with/to, similarity to/between, contrast to/with, comparable to, similar to

answer to#

Pronunciation
/ˈænsɚ tu/ · AN-ser too · weak /ˈænsɚ tə/
Русский
ответ на (вопрос), решение (задачи) — рус. «на», не «к» и не «о»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund — usually a question, problem, or letter (the answer to my email), but a gerund is normal when the complement names a task rather than a question (the answer to reducing traffic is not more lanes, the answer to getting kids to read). That gerund is the proof that the to is a preposition, so it is never a to-infinitive: ✗ the answer to reduce traffic. The noun also takes an appositive that-clause naming its content, with no preposition: the answer, that nobody had checked the valve, took a week to surface.
Pattern
the answer to [question / problem / letter] · an answer to [someone] · in answer to [noun phrase] · have / know / give the answer to [noun phrase]
Contrast
The classic noun-versus-verb shift. The verb answer takes a direct object and no preposition — answer the question, answer the phone, answer my letter, never ✗ answer to the question. The noun requires to: the answer to the question, never ✗ the answer of the question. The one place answer to is a verb is the unrelated sense "report to, be accountable to": she answers to the regional director, he'll answer for this. Compare reply to, where the to survives in both the noun and the verb — which is exactly why answer trips people.

A response to a question, or the solution to a problem.

  1. Nobody could give me a straight answer to a simple question.
  2. The answer to the housing shortage isn't going to be one policy.
  3. I never got an answer to my email.
  4. In answer to your question, yes, the lease renews automatically.
  5. The answer to number twelve was on the back of the page.
  6. She answers to the board, not to the CEO.
See also
reply to, response to, solution to, answer something, to

apology for#

Pronunciation
/əˈpɑlədʒi fɔr/ · uh-POL-uh-jee for · weak /əˈpɑlədʒi fɚ/
Русский
извинение за — выражение сожаления о содеянном
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (an apology for being late) — never a to-infinitive (✗ an apology to be late). Apology to takes the person owed: an apology to the family. Both can appear at once: an apology to the family for the delay.
Pattern
an apology for [noun phrase / verb-ing] · an apology to [person] · an apology to [person] for [noun phrase] · owe / offer / demand / accept an apology · make no apology for [verb-ing]
Contrast
Two prepositions, two roles: for names the offense, to names the person wronged. Swapping them is ungrammatical, not just odd: ✗ an apology for the family. The verb follows the same split — apologize to her for the delay — and takes no direct object at all: ✗ I apologized her, ✗ I apologized the delay. Note the American spelling apologize, and that apologia is a different word meaning a formal defense, not a regret.

An expression of regret for something you did.

  1. The airline sent a form apology for the four-hour delay.
  2. You owe your sister an apology for what you said at dinner.
  3. He offered an apology to the family but never admitted fault.
  4. I make no apology for holding the vote in public.
  5. Her apology for missing the funeral came three months too late.
  6. The mayor's apology to the neighborhood was read aloud at the meeting.
See also
excuse for, complaint about, responsibility for, for, apologize to, apologize for, to

appendix to#

Pronunciation
/əˈpɛndɪks tu/ · uh-PEN-diks too · weak /əˈpɛndɪks tə/
Русский
приложение к — раздел с дополнительным материалом в конце документа
Type
noun + preposition
Register
formal — academic writing, agency reports, and legal instruments
Complement
a noun phrase naming the document the appendix hangs off — never a gerund, never an infinitive (✗ an appendix to show the raw data, unless to show is a purpose clause), and never a clause.
Pattern
an appendix to [a report / a book / an agreement] · Appendix A / Appendix 3 (cited bare, no preposition) · set out / attached / reproduced in the appendix · see the appendix to [document]
Variants
the plural is appendices /əˈpɛndəsiz/ for documents in scholarly and legal prose and appendixes for the organ; American general writing uses appendixes for both without anyone objecting
Contrast
In contract drafting the attached documents are exhibits, schedules, and annexes, not appendices — see exhibit to/schedule to/annex to. An addendum to adds material that did not exist before; an appendix to parks supporting material that did. ✗ an appendix of the report is wrong for the attachment sense, though an appendix of tables (what it contains) is fine. The organ takes no preposition at all: ✗ an appendix to the colon.

A section of supporting material attached at the end of a longer document.

  1. The full data set is in the appendix to the final report.
  2. She published the letters as an appendix to the second edition.
  3. Appendix C to the grant application lists every subcontractor by name.
  4. The methodology is buried in an appendix to a 1978 study nobody reads.
  5. Nothing in the appendix to the contract changes the payment terms.
  6. Reviewers rarely read past the first appendix to a submission this long.
See also
exhibit to/schedule to/annex to, amendment to, citation to, reference to, to

applicability to#

Pronunciation
/əˌplɪkəˈbɪləti tu/ · uh-plik-uh-BIL-uh-tee too · weak /əˌplɪkəˈbɪləti tə/
Русский
применимость к — насколько правило распространяется на данный случай
Type
noun + preposition
Register
formal — regulatory analyses, court opinions, and standards documents; an American meets it as the heading "Applicability" at the front of a federal rule, a building code, or an insurance policy
Complement
a noun phrase or gerund (its applicability to franchising the brand) — never a to-infinitive (✗ applicability to cover franchises) and never a clause.
Pattern
the applicability of X to Y · [possessive] applicability to [a case / a class of persons / a transaction] · question / dispute / concede / limit the applicability of [rule] to [facts]
Contrast
The frame has two halves and needs both. Standard is the applicability of the exemption to small carriers; the of phrase may shrink to a possessive (its applicability to small carriers, the statute's applicability to small carriers) but it cannot simply vanish. The adjective is applicable to, and ✗ applicable for is an error. Do not confuse it with application to, which is an everyday word for a form you file: her application to the program is paperwork, the rule's applicability to the program is a question of law. ✗ applicability for and ✗ applicability on are errors.

Whether and how far a rule, standard, or principle reaches a particular case.

  1. The brief spends four pages on the statute's applicability to out-of-state sellers.
  2. Counsel conceded the rule's applicability to the 2019 transactions.
  3. Section 1 sets out the code's applicability to existing buildings.
  4. Nobody disputed the standard's applicability to leased equipment.
  5. The agency limited the guidance's applicability to federally funded projects.
  6. Its applicability to volunteer fire departments is still unsettled in this circuit.
See also
relevance to, compliance with, exemption from, subject to, pursuant to

appointment with#

Pronunciation
/əˈpɔɪntmənt wɪθ/ · uh-POYNT-munt with · weak /əˈpɔɪntmənt wəθ/
Русский
приём у кого-л., назначенная встреча с; отдельно — назначение на должность
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
with: a person, an office, or a business — never a gerund (✗ an appointment with seeing the dentist) and never a clause. for: the purpose or the day (an appointment for a cleaning, an appointment for Tuesday). at: the place or the clock time (an appointment at the clinic, an appointment at four). to: an office or a body, in the wholly different sense of the word (her appointment to the bench).
Pattern
an appointment with [person] · an appointment for [purpose / day] · an appointment at [place / time] · make / keep / miss / cancel / reschedule an appointment · [someone's] appointment to [a position] · by appointment only
Contrast
With names the other party, for the reason, at the place, and to the job. ✗ an appointment to the dentist is an error in the meeting sense, though his appointment to the FCC is correct in the office sense — one spelling, two nouns. American English keeps appointment for professional slots and reservation for tables, rooms, and seats: you have an appointment with the dentist and a reservation at the restaurant, where British English would say booking for both.

A scheduled meeting with a professional or an official, or the act of placing someone in an office.

  1. I've got an appointment with the orthodontist at eight-fifteen.
  2. She made an appointment with the county assessor to contest the valuation.
  3. Can you move my appointment with Dr. Reyes to Thursday afternoon?
  4. His appointment to the appeals court took fourteen months and two hearings.
  5. The clinic is by appointment only after four.
  6. He missed a third appointment with the probation officer and a warrant issued.
See also
conversation with, interview with, encounter with, visit to, with

approach to#

Pronunciation
/əˈproʊtʃ tu/ · uh-PROHCH too · weak /əˈproʊtʃ tə/
Русский
подход к — способ решения; также приближение к месту
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (an approach to teaching writing) — never a to-infinitive (✗ an approach to teach writing). In the physical sense the complement is a place: the approach to the bridge. A wh-clause does not follow it (✗ an approach to how we hire; write an approach to hiring).
Pattern
an approach to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · take an approach to [noun phrase] · a [adjective] approach to [noun phrase] · the approach to [place]
Contrast
The verb takes no preposition at all. The plane approached the runway, he approached me after class — never ✗ approached to the runway. The noun requires to, and ✗ an approach of, ✗ an approach on, and ✗ an approach for are all wrong. Keep the -ing rule in view: because a noun fits the slot, the to is a preposition, so an approach to solving it, not ✗ an approach to solve it. The plural approaches is also the aviation term for the final descent path.

A way of dealing with something, or the final stretch leading to a place.

  1. Her approach to classroom discipline is quieter than mine.
  2. We need a completely different approach to funding rural clinics.
  3. The company took a cautious approach to the acquisition.
  4. Traffic backs up on the approach to the tunnel every weekday.
  5. There's no single right approach to teaching a child to read.
  6. His approach to negotiating is to say nothing for the first ten minutes.
See also
attitude to/toward, solution to, way of/to, approach something, to

argument with/about#

Pronunciation
/ˈɑrɡjəmənt wɪθ/, /ˈɑrɡjəmənt əˈbaʊt/ · AR-gyuh-munt with, AR-gyuh-munt uh-BOWT · weak /ˈɑrɡjəmənt wəθ/, /ˈɑrɡjəmənt ˈbaʊt/
Русский
спор с кем-л. / о чём-л.; отдельно — довод, аргумент в пользу позиции
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
with: a person. about / over: the subject — noun phrase, gerund (an argument about paying the deposit), or wh-clause (an argument about who was driving). for / against: a position, and here a that-clause is possible after the noun (the argument that the tax is regressive). ✗ an argument to do something.
Pattern
an argument with [person] · an argument about / over [issue] · an argument for / against [position] · an argument that [clause] · have / start / win / lose an argument · get into an argument with [person]
Variants
argument about — neutral · argument over — the same, with more heat and usually a scarce resource (an argument over the last seat)
Contrast
Four prepositions, four jobs. With is the opponent, about/over is the topic, for/against is the side taken — an argument for raising the cap is a reasoned case, while an argument about raising the cap is a quarrel. Because argument means both "quarrel" and "line of reasoning," the preposition is what tells the reader which one you mean. ✗ an argument to the manager and ✗ an argument on the topic are both wrong; use with and about. The verb keeps the same set: argue with someone about something, argue for a position, argue that.

A quarrel, or a reasoned case for a position.

  1. I had a stupid argument with my brother about a parking space.
  2. The argument over the property line has been going on since 1998.
  3. Her argument for raising the minimum wage runs eighty pages.
  4. There's a strong argument against rebuilding on the same site.
  5. They got into an argument about who forgot to lock the shed.
  6. The argument that the tax is regressive has never been answered.
See also
disagreement with/about, discussion about/of, opposition to, with, argue with, argue about, argue over, about

association with/between#

Pronunciation
/əˌsoʊsiˈeɪʃən wɪθ/, /əˌsoʊʃiˈeɪʃən bɪˈtwin/ · uh-soh-see-AY-shun with, uh-soh-shee-AY-shun bih-TWEEN · weak /əˌsoʊsiˈeɪʃən wəθ/, /əˌsoʊʃiˈeɪʃən bəˈtwin/ · both /-siˈeɪ-/ and /-ʃiˈeɪ-/ are standard American
Русский
связь с / между — принадлежность к кругу либо статистическая связь величин
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral; the between frame is technical
Complement
with: a person, group, or thing, or a gerund (his association with running the fund). between: two items joined by and. Never a to-infinitive (✗ an association to reduce risk) and never a that-clause.
Pattern
an association with [person / organization] · an association between X and Y · in association with [partner] · guilt by association · break off / sever / deny an association with [noun phrase] · a trade / homeowners' / alumni association (no preposition)
Contrast
With for affiliation, between for statistics. His association with the firm is who he worked for; an association between lead exposure and reading scores is a pattern in a data set — and a researcher will insist that an association between claims far less than a cause of. ✗ an association to is an error in both senses. Against correlation with/between: a correlation is a measured coefficient, an association need only be a tendency, which is why epidemiologists reach for the looser word first.

A tie between a person and a group, or an observed pattern linking two variables.

  1. He cut every association with the group the week after the indictment.
  2. The study reports an association between night shifts and elevated blood pressure.
  3. The exhibit was mounted in association with the state historical society.
  4. Her only association with the case was one memo written in 2011.
  5. Researchers found no association between the vaccine and the reported symptoms.
  6. Guilt by association cost him the nomination before a single vote was counted.
See also
connection with/to/between, correlation with/between, link between/to, relationship with/between, in association with, with

attack on#

Pronunciation
/əˈtæk ɑn/ · uh-TAK on · weak /əˈtæk ən/
Русский
нападение на, атака на — также резкая публичная критика кого-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun — a target, whether physical (an attack on the depot) or reputational (an attack on her record). It does not take a gerund or a clause: ✗ an attack on raising taxes; write an attack on the tax increase. Attack of takes an illness or a fit: an attack of nerves.
Pattern
an attack on [target] · an attack against [target] · an attack by [attacker] · an attack of [illness / emotion] · come / be under attack · launch an attack on [target]
Variants
an attack upon — formal and chiefly written · an attack against — normal with abstract or institutional targets (an attack against press freedom), less usual with physical ones
Contrast
Three different of / on / by jobs: on names the target, by names the attacker, of names an ailment or a fit — an attack of the flu, an attack of conscience, a heart attack. The verb takes a direct object and no preposition: they attacked the depot, never ✗ they attacked on the depot or ✗ attacked against the depot. Compare threat to, where the preposition names who is endangered rather than what is struck.

An act of violence, or a hostile public criticism.

  1. The attack on the substation knocked out power to nine counties.
  2. His column was a sustained attack on the mayor's housing record.
  3. The clinic has been under attack from state legislators all spring.
  4. She had an attack of vertigo halfway up the stairs.
  5. Two of the attacks on the pipeline were never claimed.
  6. The senator called the ruling an attack on the First Amendment.
See also
threat to, complaint about, opposition to, on, against

attempt at#

Pronunciation
/əˈtɛmpt æt/ · uh-TEMPT at · weak /əˈtɛmpt ət/
Русский
попытка чего-л. — рус. родительный, английское at предлогом не переводится
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (an attempt at reconciliation, an attempt at fixing the leak) — never a bare infinitive after at (✗ an attempt at fix the leak). The same noun takes a to-infinitive with no preposition at all: an attempt to fix the leak. Both are correct; see the contrast.
Pattern
an attempt at [noun phrase / verb-ing] · an attempt to [verb] · make an attempt at / to · an attempt on [someone's life / a record] · at the first attempt
Variants
an attempt at doing it and an attempt to do it — both standard; at + -ing stresses the trying, to + infinitive stresses the goal
Contrast
At and to divide the labor. An attempt at humor is a try at producing a thing; an attempt to be funny is a try at performing an act, so the gerund frame is normal with result nouns (an attempt at a soufflé) and the infinitive frame with action verbs (an attempt to reach him). A third preposition changes the meaning entirely: an attempt on his life is an attempted murder, and an attempt on the record is a try at breaking it. ✗ an attempt for and ✗ an attempt of are errors. The verb attempt takes a direct object or an infinitive and no preposition: she attempted the climb, she attempted to climb it, never ✗ attempted at the climb.

A try at doing or producing something.

  1. His attempt at an apology made everything worse.
  2. This is our third attempt at getting the permit approved.
  3. She made no attempt to hide her irritation.
  4. The bread was a first attempt at sourdough and it showed.
  5. There was an attempt on the governor's life in 1974.
  6. He passed the exam at the second attempt.
See also
willingness to, chance of, success in/at, at, to

attention to#

Pronunciation
/əˈtɛnʃən tu/ · uh-TEN-shun too · weak /əˈtɛnʃən tə/
Русский
внимание к — сосредоточенность и забота, направленные на что-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (attention to getting the details right) — never a to-infinitive (✗ attention to get the details right) and never a that-clause (✗ attention that the details were wrong). A wh-clause is fine, chiefly in the pay attention to frame: pay attention to what he says, little attention to how the work was funded.
Pattern
attention to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · pay attention to [noun phrase / wh-clause] · draw / call / bring attention to [noun phrase] · attention to detail · the attention of [person]
Contrast
The preposition never changes, but the near-synonyms do. Attention to is the only frame — ✗ attention on, ✗ attention about, ✗ attention for — yet the closely related focus takes on (focus on the details), which is where the mixing starts. The verb attend splits by sense: attend the meeting takes no preposition and means "be present at," while attend to the patient means "take care of." A speaker saying ✗ I attended to the conference has produced a different sentence from the one they meant.

Notice, care, or concentration directed at something.

  1. The renovation shows real attention to detail.
  2. Pay attention to the fine print on page nine.
  3. The report drew attention to three unreported spills.
  4. She gives more attention to her houseplants than to her email.
  5. Very little attention has been paid to rural transit funding.
  6. May I have your attention for one minute.
See also
focus on, emphasis on/stress on, interest in, respect for, to

attitude to/toward#

Pronunciation
/ˈætɪtud tu/, /ˈætɪtud təˈwɔrd/ · AT-ih-tood too, AT-ih-tood tuh-WORD · weak /ˈætɪtud tə/, /ˈætɪtud tɔrd/
Русский
отношение к — устойчивый взгляд или чувство по поводу чего-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (her attitude toward working weekends) — never a to-infinitive (✗ his attitude to work weekends). No clause: ✗ an attitude that we should wait means something different (attitude + that-clause exists only in the rare "stance" sense).
Pattern
an attitude to / toward [noun phrase / verb-ing] · an attitude about [noun phrase] · someone's attitude · have / take an attitude toward [noun phrase] · a change in attitude
Variants
toward — the American default, with no -s · towards — British · to — standard everywhere but distinctly more British in feel · about — colloquial American, common in speech (his attitude about money)
Contrast
Toward is the American form, and the -s on towards buys no formality — it just reads as British. All three of toward, to, and about are grammatical; ✗ attitude against, ✗ attitude on, and ✗ attitude for are not. Note that bare attitude with no complement has become an American noun in its own right meaning insolence (don't give me that attitude, she's got attitude), which is why he has an attitude and he has an attitude toward authority say almost opposite things about the speaker's judgment.

A settled way of thinking or feeling about something.

  1. Her attitude toward deadlines has improved since the reorganization.
  2. Public attitudes to nuclear power shifted sharply after 2011.
  3. I don't like his attitude about being asked to show ID.
  4. The whole department has a casual attitude toward recordkeeping.
  5. There's been a real change in attitude among younger farmers.
  6. You'll get further with a better attitude toward being coached.
See also
approach to, opinion on/of/about, view on/of, reaction to, toward

authority on/over#

Pronunciation
/əˈθɔrəti ɑn/, /əˈθɔrəti ˈoʊvɚ/ · uh-THOR-uh-tee on, uh-THOR-uh-tee OH-ver · weak /əˈθɔrəti ən/; over has no weak form
Русский
on — авторитет, специалист по чему-л.; over — власть над кем-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
on / in: a field of knowledge — noun phrase only (✗ an authority on how bridges fail; write an authority on bridge failures). over: a person, territory, or budget. The same noun takes a to-infinitive with no preposition: the authority to sign checks — and there the to is an infinitive marker, so ✗ the authority to signing checks.
Pattern
an authority on / in [field] · authority over [person / area / budget] · the authority to [verb] · on the authority of [source] · have / exercise / exceed one's authority
Variants
an authority on — the usual for a subject · an authority in — a field or discipline (an authority in pediatric oncology), slightly more institutional
Contrast
Two nouns wearing one spelling. An authority on medieval coins is an expert — countable, and you can be one of several. Authority over the budget is power — uncountable, and there is only so much of it. The prepositions keep them apart, so ✗ an authority over medieval coins claims a strange dominion and ✗ authority on the budget claims expertise where you meant control. A third frame, the authority to approve it, is a granted right, and it takes the infinitive, not a preposition.

An expert on a subject, or power exercised over people or resources.

  1. She's the leading authority on nineteenth-century Shaker furniture.
  2. The commission has no authority over privately owned wells.
  3. He exceeded his authority when he signed the settlement.
  4. The department has authority to issue permits but not to revoke them.
  5. I have that on the authority of the county clerk herself.
  6. Two authorities in the field reviewed the manuscript.
See also
expert on/in, control over/of, influence on/over, responsibility for, over

awareness of#

Pronunciation
/əˈwɛrnəs ʌv/ · uh-WAIR-nus uv · weak /əˈwɛrnəs əv/ → /əˈwɛrnəs ə/
Русский
осведомлённость о чём-л., осознание чего-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (awareness of being watched), or a wh-clause (an awareness of what the job actually pays) — never a to-infinitive. Unusually for a noun in this file, it also takes a bare that-clause with no preposition: an awareness that the money would run out.
Pattern
awareness of [noun phrase / verb-ing / wh-clause] · awareness that [clause] · raise / increase awareness of [noun phrase] · a growing awareness of [noun phrase] · awareness among [group]
Variants
awareness about — increasingly common in public-health and campaign writing (awareness about the symptoms), but of remains the edited form
Contrast
Of is the preposition; about is a marketing habit. Both are heard, but of is what edited American prose uses, and ✗ awareness on, ✗ awareness to, and ✗ awareness for are simply wrong. Watch the two frames after the noun: awareness of the risk takes a noun, awareness that there was a risk takes a clause with no preposition at all. Dropping the of before a clause (✗ awareness of that there was a risk) is the error that follows from mixing them. The adjective aware takes the same two: aware of the risk, aware that there was a risk.

Knowledge or perception that something exists or is the case.

  1. There's a growing awareness of how much water the plant actually uses.
  2. The campaign raised awareness of early stroke symptoms.
  3. She had a vague awareness of being followed down the block.
  4. Public awareness of the recall was almost nonexistent.
  5. An awareness that the fund would be exhausted by June changed the vote.
  6. Safety awareness among the night crew has improved since the training.
See also
knowledge of/about, understanding of, insight into, belief in, aware of, of
