basis for#

Pronunciation
/ˈbeɪsɪs fɔr/ · BAY-siss for · weak /ˈbeɪsɪs fɚ/
Русский
основание для — то, на чём что-л. держится или может быть построено
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (a basis for believing him) — the edited frame. A that-clause is fine after the noun (no basis for the claim that the fund was solvent). A to-infinitive after basis is common in legal and journalistic prose (no basis to conclude that) but is still the marked choice; for + -ing is the form no editor will touch.
Pattern
a basis for [noun phrase / verb-ing] · the basis of [something] · on the basis of [evidence] · have / provide / lay / lack a basis for [noun phrase] · on a first-come basis, on a monthly basis (adverbial, no complement)
Variants
the basis of and the basis for split by direction: the basis of his argument is what the argument rests on, a basis for an argument is material you could build one out of. Both are standard; they are not interchangeable in every sentence.
Contrast
The plural is bases /ˈbeɪsiz/, spelled like the plural of base /ˈbeɪsɪz/ and pronounced differently — a written trap in on these two bases. ✗ a basis to the claim is an error. Note the frozen adverbial on a ... basis, which takes no preposition of its own and which editors compress on sight: on a weekly basis is usually just weekly.

The evidence, principle, or foundation that something rests on or could be built on.

  1. There's no basis for the claim that the well was tested in June.
  2. The affidavit gave the judge a basis for issuing the warrant.
  3. His whole case rests on the basis of one unsigned memo.
  4. Attendance is not a basis for dismissal under this contract.
  5. She had no basis for believing the transfer had cleared.
  6. The board meets on a quarterly basis, which is to say four times a year.
See also
grounds for, evidence of/for, justification for, rationale for, on the basis of, based on

bearing on#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɛrɪŋ ɑn/ · BAIR-ing on · weak /ˈbɛrɪŋ ən/
Русский
отношение к делу — have a bearing on = иметь значение для чего-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; common in courtrooms, editorials, and committee reports
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (no bearing on choosing a successor) — never a to-infinitive (✗ no bearing to choose a successor) and never a that-clause. The noun almost always appears inside have a bearing on or have no bearing on; a bare the bearing on is not idiomatic.
Pattern
have a bearing on [noun phrase] · have no bearing on [noun phrase] · a direct / material / significant bearing on [noun phrase] · bear on [noun phrase] (the verb) · get / find one's bearings (a different noun)
Contrast
Two unrelated nouns share the spelling. A bearing on something is relevance; one's bearings are orientation (get your bearings), and a bearing in a machine is a part — only the first takes on. ✗ a bearing to the case and ✗ a bearing with the case are errors. Against relevance to: the two say the same thing with different prepositions, and swapping the prepositions is the standard slip — ✗ no relevance on the case, ✗ no bearing to the case. The verb keeps on: the memo bears on the timeline.

Relevance — the quality of having something to do with the matter at hand.

  1. His military record has no bearing on the zoning question.
  2. What the neighbors saw has a direct bearing on the timeline.
  3. The rate cut will have some bearing on next quarter's numbers.
  4. Whether she liked him has no bearing on whether he was negligent.
  5. The witness's memory has a real bearing on deciding which account to credit.
  6. Anything with a bearing on the merger goes to outside counsel first.
See also
relevance to, effect on, impact on, influence on/over, relevant to, on

belief in#

Pronunciation
/bɪˈlif ɪn/ · bih-LEEF in · weak /bəˈlif ən/
Русский
вера в — убеждённость, что нечто существует, работает или стоит того
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (a belief in taking the long view) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a belief in take the long view, ✗ a belief to take the long view). Like awareness, it also takes a bare that-clause with no preposition: a belief that the market will correct.
Pattern
a belief in [noun phrase / verb-ing] · a belief that [clause] · in the belief that [clause] · beliefs about [topic] · contrary to popular belief
Variants
beliefs about — the plural, for opinions on a topic (beliefs about diet), where singular belief in is about faith or confidence
Contrast
In for faith, that for content, and no preposition before the clause. A belief in vaccines is confidence in them; a belief that vaccines work is a specific proposition held — and ✗ a belief in that vaccines work mashes the two together. ✗ belief on and ✗ belief to are errors. The verb splits the same way and produces the file's most useful minimal pair: I believe you (I accept what you just said) against I believe in you (I have confidence in your ability), and ✗ I believe in that you can do it is wrong for the same reason.

Confidence that something exists, works, or is worth holding to.

  1. Her belief in the program survived two rounds of budget cuts.
  2. He lost his belief in the possibility of a negotiated settlement.
  3. A belief in rewarding effort rather than results runs through the whole handbook.
  4. The belief that housing prices only go up died in 2008.
  5. They pressed on in the belief that the weather would clear.
  6. Contrary to popular belief, the bridge was never named after him.
See also
confidence in, faith in, dependence on/reliance on, doubt about, believe in, in

beneficiary of/under#

Pronunciation
/ˌbɛnəˈfɪʃieri ʌv/, /ˌbɛnəˈfɪʃieri ˈʌndɚ/ · ben-uh-FISH-ee-air-ee uv, ben-uh-FISH-ee-air-ee UN-der · weak /ˌbɛnəˈfɪʃieri əv/ → /ˌbɛnəˈfɪʃieri ə/; under has no weak form
Русский
юр. выгодоприобретатель, бенефициар — по завещанию, полису, договору
Type
noun + preposition
Register
of is neutral; under is legal drafting — a will, a trust instrument, a life-insurance form, an ERISA plan document. An American meets beneficiary under on the day a parent's estate is probated, or on the form the HR office hands out in the first week of a job.
Complement
of: a noun phrase naming the instrument, the person, or the windfall (the beneficiary of the trust, the beneficiary of a clerical error). under: a noun phrase naming the legal instrument only — a will, a policy, a plan, a section (a beneficiary under the policy). Never a gerund after under, and never an infinitive or a clause after either.
Pattern
the beneficiary of [an instrument / an act / a mistake] · a beneficiary under [a will / a policy / a plan] · name / designate [someone] as beneficiary · the primary / contingent beneficiary · a third-party beneficiary of [a contract]
Variants
beneficiary under the will and beneficiary of the will are both correct in a probate file; under is the drafter's form because it matches the other under phrases in the instrument (rights under, obligations under, hereunder)
Contrast
Of works everywhere; under only where there is a document. You may be the beneficiary of a lucky mistake, but ✗ a beneficiary under a lucky mistake is nonsense — under needs an instrument to sit beneath. Do not confuse the noun with benefit of/to: the benefit of the doubt is an advantage, the beneficiary of the estate is a person. In American insurance the fixed phrases are primary beneficiary and contingent beneficiary, both without a preposition.

The person or entity legally entitled to receive something, or anyone who gains from an event.

  1. She is the sole beneficiary of her aunt's estate.
  2. Every beneficiary under the policy must be notified within thirty days.
  3. The contractor was the unexpected beneficiary of a drafting error in section 9.
  4. He named his daughter as the primary beneficiary and his brother as contingent.
  5. Farmers in three counties were the real beneficiaries of the rate freeze.
  6. A beneficiary under the trust cannot also serve as its sole trustee here.
See also
benefit of/to, entitlement to, party to, signatory to, title to, hereunder

benefit of/to#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɛnəfɪt ʌv/, /ˈbɛnəfɪt tu/ · BEN-uh-fit uv, BEN-uh-fit too · weak /ˈbɛnəfɪt əv/ → /ˈbɛnəfɪt ə/; /ˈbɛnəfɪt tə/
Русский
of — польза от чего-л.; to — выгода для кого-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: noun phrase or gerund (the benefit of starting early) — never a to-infinitive (✗ the benefit of to start early). to: a person or group who gains. A to-infinitive does attach directly to the noun in the informal the benefits to doing it this way, which is standard American speech but avoided in edited prose.
Pattern
the benefit of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · a benefit to [person / group] · of benefit to [person] · for the benefit of [person] · give [someone] the benefit of the doubt · a benefit for [cause]
Variants
the benefits to doing X — accepted informal American variant of the benefits of doing X
Contrast
Three prepositions, three meanings. The benefit of insulation is the advantage insulation gives; a benefit to homeowners is who receives it; and a benefit for the animal shelter is a fundraising event, an entirely separate noun. The verb takes yet another preposition: benefit from — homeowners benefit from the rebate, never ✗ benefit of the rebate, and this verb-to-noun shift (from → of) is one of the commonest in the file.

An advantage gained, or the person or group that gains it.

  1. The main benefit of the new schedule is that nobody drives home in the dark.
  2. The tax credit is of real benefit to first-time buyers.
  3. She had the benefit of eight years' experience in the same building.
  4. I'll give him the benefit of the doubt one more time.
  5. The concert was a benefit for the county food bank.
  6. There's no obvious benefit to switching carriers midyear.
See also
advantage of/over, disadvantage of, importance of/to, price of/for, of

breach of#

Pronunciation
/britʃ ʌv/ · breech uv · weak /britʃ əv/ → /britʃ ə/
Русский
нарушение чего-л. — договора, обязанности, стандарта; юр.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; the core term of American contract and fiduciary law, and an everyday word for a security failure
Complement
a noun phrase naming the duty, agreement, or standard broken — never a gerund (✗ a breach of failing to disclose; write a breach of the duty to disclose), never an infinitive, and never a that-clause.
Pattern
a breach of [contract / duty / trust / the peace / etiquette] · in breach of [an agreement] · a material / minor / anticipatory breach · sue for breach of contract · a data breach (compound, no preposition)
Contrast
Breach and breech are homophones and neither one is the other. A breach is a break; a breech is the back end of a gun or a baby delivered feet first, and breeches are trousers. ✗ a breech of contract is the commonest spelling error in small-claims filings. Note that breach of contract takes no article inside the fixed phrase — sued for breach of contract, not ✗ for a breach of the contract, unless a particular breach is meant. The verb takes a bare object: they breached the agreement, ✗ they breached of the agreement. In American practice a material breach excuses the other side's performance; a minor one only supports damages.

The violation of a duty, an agreement, a standard, or a barrier.

  1. Missing two deliveries is a breach of the supply agreement.
  2. The suit alleges a breach of fiduciary duty by three former officers.
  3. Reading her mail was a plain breach of trust.
  4. The company disclosed the data breach eleven months after it happened.
  5. Showing up in shorts was a breach of etiquette, not of the dress code.
  6. He was arrested for breach of the peace outside the arena.
See also
compliance with, default on/under, damages for, liability for/to, obligation to/under, remedy for/against, in violation of

C#

case for/against#

Pronunciation
/keɪs fɔr/, /keɪs əˈɡɛnst/ · kayss for, kayss uh-GENST · weak /keɪs fɚ/, /keɪs əˈɡɛnst/
Русский
доводы за / против; отдельно — судебное дело и отдельный случай
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (the case for raising rates) — never a to-infinitive (✗ the case to raise rates). A that-clause attaches to the verb frame instead: she made the case that rates should rise. The legal noun takes different prepositions: a case against a defendant, a case for the plaintiff, the case of Ruiz v. Kemper.
Pattern
the case for / against [noun phrase / verb-ing] · make / build / press / state the case for [noun phrase] · a case against [a person] (legal) · in case of [emergency] · a case of [flu / wine / mistaken identity]
Contrast
For and against are the argument; of is the instance. The case for a bond issue is the argument in favor; a case of the flu is one occurrence; a case against Ruiz is a prosecution. ✗ the case of raising rates means nothing. Note that in case of (a fixed compound preposition — see in case of) and in the case of (= "as for") are different phrases doing different jobs. ✗ a case to raise rates is the infinitive error, and it is common in memos.

The argument in favor of or opposed to a course of action — and, separately, a legal action or a single instance.

  1. Nobody has made a serious case for keeping the old boiler another winter.
  2. The case against expanding the runway is mostly noise and mostly persuasive.
  3. She built the case for consolidating the two departments over six months.
  4. The state's case against him rested on a single unreliable witness.
  5. There's a strong case for waiting until the audit closes.
  6. It was a case of mistaken identity, and it cost him two nights in county.
See also
argument with/about, grounds for, justification for, rationale for, in case of, against

cause of#

Pronunciation
/kɔz ʌv/ · kawz uv · weak /kɔz əv/ → /kɔz ə/
Русский
причина чего-л. — рус. родительный падеж, не «для»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (the cause of the flooding, the cause of so many people leaving) — never a to-infinitive (✗ the cause of to flood) and never a that-clause (✗ the cause that the pipe burst; write the cause of the burst pipe).
Pattern
the cause of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · a cause for [concern / alarm / celebration] · the cause of [death / the fire / the delay] · a lost cause · the leading cause of [outcome]
Contrast
Cause of against cause for, and the difference is total. The cause of names what produced a result — the cause of the fire was faulty wiring. Cause for appears only in a short fixed list of abstract nouns — cause for concern, cause for alarm, cause for celebration, cause for complaint — and means "grounds, justification." ✗ the cause for the fire is an error; a cause of concern is not wrong but says something different (what caused the concern, not what justifies it). Note also the unrelated third noun in a good cause, a lost cause, meaning a movement or aim.

The thing that makes something happen.

  1. Investigators never determined the cause of the fire.
  2. Heart disease is still the leading cause of death in the county.
  3. The cause of the outage turned out to be a squirrel.
  4. The lab results are no cause for alarm.
  5. Falling enrollment is the cause of the budget gap, not the result of it.
  6. There's cause for cautious optimism in the third-quarter numbers.
See also
reason for, result of, consequence of, effect on, of

chance of#

Pronunciation
/tʃæns ʌv/ · chanss uv · weak /tʃæns əv/ → /tʃæns ə/
Русский
вероятность чего-л. (of + -ing); ср. chance to — возможность что-л. сделать
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: noun phrase or gerund (a chance of rain, a chance of winning) — never a to-infinitive after of (✗ a chance of to win, ✗ a chance of win). The same noun takes a to-infinitive with no preposition: a chance to speak. Both are correct and they do not mean the same thing.
Pattern
a chance of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · a chance to [verb] · the chances are that [clause] · stand / have a chance of [verb-ing] · by chance · give [someone] a chance to [verb]
Contrast
Chance of is probability; chance to is opportunity. There's a chance of speaking to her means it might happen; there's a chance to speak to her means the opening exists and you could take it. Weather forecasts and odds always use of (a forty percent chance of rain, ✗ a chance to rain), and offers always use to (give me a chance to explain, ✗ a chance of explaining in that sense). ✗ chance for doing is an error, though a chance for the kids (an opportunity for a person) is fine. Compare possibility of, which has only the of frame; opportunity behaves like chance but prefers the infinitive (an opportunity to speak) and takes for rather than of with a noun (an opportunity for growth).

The likelihood that something will happen, or an opportunity to do something.

  1. There's a sixty percent chance of thunderstorms after four.
  2. She has a real chance of making the Olympic team.
  3. Give me a chance to explain before you decide.
  4. What are the chances of the ferry running on a day like this?
  5. We met entirely by chance at a gas station in Nebraska.
  6. He never got the chance to say goodbye.
See also
possibility of, risk of, danger of/to, hope for/of, of

change in/of#

Pronunciation
/tʃeɪndʒ ɪn/, /tʃeɪndʒ ʌv/ · chaynj in, chaynj uv · weak /tʃeɪndʒ ən/, /tʃeɪndʒ əv/ → /tʃeɪndʒ ə/
Русский
in — изменение в чём-л.; of — смена, замена одного другим
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
in: a noun naming what is being altered (a change in the weather, a change in how we bill). of: a noun naming what is being swapped out (a change of address, a change of clothes). to: the modification made to a document or plan (a change to the schedule). Gerunds follow in freely (a change in staffing); no to-infinitive and no that-clause.
Pattern
a change in [thing that alters] · a change of [thing replaced] · a change to [document / plan] · a change from X to Y · make a change to [noun phrase] · for a change
Variants
a change of — swap · a change in — internal alteration · a change to — an edit · changes for — never; use changes to
Contrast
Three prepositions and three different events. A change in the schedule means the schedule itself is now different; a change of schedule means one schedule has replaced another; a change to the schedule is a specific edit made to it. Errors cluster around of: ✗ a change of the weather (the weather alters, it is not swapped) and ✗ a change in address (an address is swapped, not internally altered). Change from X to Y names both endpoints. The verb takes a direct object with no preposition — change the tire, ✗ change to the tire — except in change into (transform) and change from X to Y.

An alteration, a replacement, or an edit.

  1. There's been a change in the weather since Thursday.
  2. You'd better pack a change of clothes.
  3. I've made two small changes to the contract — see the redline.
  4. The most striking change in the neighborhood is the number of children.
  5. We're looking at a change from fee-for-service to a flat rate.
  6. Let's eat outside for a change.
See also
difference between/in, improvement in/on, increase in/decrease in, effect on, in

citation to#

Pronunciation
/saɪˈteɪʃən tu/ · sy-TAY-shun too · weak /saɪˈteɪʃən tə/
Русский
ссылка на источник; отдельно — уведомление о нарушении (штраф) или благодарность
Type
noun + preposition
Register
to is legal and academic — briefs, law-review footnotes, style manuals; for is everyday American in two unrelated senses, a traffic ticket and a military commendation
Complement
to: a noun phrase naming a source — a case, a statute, a page, an authority. for: a noun phrase or gerund naming the offense or the deed (a citation for speeding, a citation for running the light, a citation for valor). of: what is quoted (the citation of three district-court opinions). Never an infinitive or a clause after any of them.
Pattern
a citation to [a case / a statute / page 14] · a citation for [an offense / an act of bravery] · the citation of [authorities] · issue / write / fight a citation · a pin citation
Contrast
To points at a source, for at a reason. A citation to Miranda sends the reader to a case; a citation for speeding is a ticket a Michigan trooper hands you; a citation for valor is a medal. ✗ a citation of Miranda is not wrong but means the act of citing rather than the reference itself. The verb splits the same way: cite to the record is legal usage, cite the record is general usage, and cite someone for a violation is the ticket. Do not confuse it with sight or site.

A pointer to a source in a text, or an official notice of an offense or a commendation.

  1. The brief has forty pages of argument and one citation to the record.
  2. He got a citation for an expired tag on the way to the hearing.
  3. Every citation to an unpublished opinion has to be flagged.
  4. Her citation for valor is framed in the hallway outside the chief's office.
  5. The footnote gives a citation to the 1976 committee report.
  6. Two paragraphs in, the memo drops a citation to a statute that was repealed.
See also
reference to, appendix to, penalty for, refer to, to

claim against/for/to#

Pronunciation
/kleɪm əˈɡɛnst/, /kleɪm fɔr/, /kleɪm tu/ · klaym uh-GENST, klaym for, klaym too · weak /kleɪm fɚ/, /kleɪm tə/; against has no weak form
Русский
юр. претензия к кому-л., требование о чём-л., притязание на что-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; the against and to frames are legal and insurance usage, met on a proof-of-claim form in probate or a homeowner's policy
Complement
against: the party or fund you are proceeding against. for: what you want — damages, benefits, a refund. to: the thing you assert a right in — a title, a throne, an inheritance. on: a demand on a resource (a claim on her time). The noun also takes a bare that-clause (the claim that the meter was broken) but never a to-infinitive (✗ a claim to be paid, in the entitlement sense — that reading is only the verb, he claims to be paid).
Pattern
a claim against [a person / an estate / an insurer] · a claim for [damages / benefits] · a claim to [property / a title] · a claim on [resources / attention] · file / press / settle / deny a claim · lay claim to [noun phrase]
Contrast
Four prepositions and four different sentences. A claim against the estate is who owes; a claim for $40,000 is what is owed; a claim to the mineral rights is what you say is yours; a claim on my afternoon is a demand for time. Swap them and the meaning breaks: ✗ a claim to the estate for the mineral rights is word salad. Note the to trap: a claim to the land takes a noun, and a claim to own the land is not the entitlement noun at all. The verb claim takes a bare object (she claimed the deduction) and a that-clause (he claims that he paid), never ✗ claim for the deduction in American English.

An assertion of a right, a demand for payment, or a right asserted in something.

  1. Three contractors filed a claim against the estate before the deadline.
  2. Her claim for lost wages was denied twice before it was paid.
  3. The family still has a claim to forty acres along the river.
  4. Grandchildren have no claim on his time and he tells them so.
  5. The insurer disputes the claim for water damage but not the one for wind.
  6. He laid claim to the discovery in a letter nobody kept.
See also
damages for, entitlement to, liability for/to, right to/of/over, title to, against

comment on#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑmɛnt ɑn/ · KOM-ent on · weak /ˈkɑmɛnt ən/
Русский
комментарий к чему-л., замечание по поводу чего-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (a comment on being passed over), or a wh-clause (no comment on what was said) — never a to-infinitive. The noun takes a bare that-clause as its content: his comment that the numbers were soft.
Pattern
a comment on / about [noun phrase] · a comment to [person / the press] · a comment that [clause] · make a comment on [noun phrase] · no comment · without comment
Variants
comment on — the default, and the only form in no comment on · comment about — conversational, common in speech
Contrast
On for the subject, to for the audience. A comment on the draft is what the comment is about; a comment to reporters is who heard it. ✗ a comment for the draft and ✗ a comment of the draft are errors. The verb keeps on and cannot take a direct object: she commented on the delay, never ✗ she commented the delay — a frequent error because the parallel verb criticize does take a bare object (criticized the delay). Commentary on behaves identically to the noun.

A spoken or written remark about something.

  1. The professor left three comments on my second paragraph.
  2. Her comment about the parking situation got a bigger laugh than it deserved.
  3. The agency declined to comment on the pending lawsuit.
  4. His comment that the numbers were "directionally fine" did not reassure anyone.
  5. The report was released without comment.
  6. A single comment to the wrong reporter cost him the job.
See also
opinion on/of/about, complaint about, view on/of, reference to, comment on, on

commitment to#

Pronunciation
/kəˈmɪtmənt tu/ · kuh-MIT-munt too · weak /kəˈmɪtmənt tə/
Русский
обязательство, приверженность чему-л. — обещание или преданность делу
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (a commitment to the plan, a commitment to reducing emissions) — a noun fits the slot, so that to is a preposition and the verb after it takes -ing. The same noun also licenses a to-infinitive in the undertaking sense (a commitment to reduce emissions by half, a commitment to appear at the hearing), where to is the infinitive marker; both frames are standard American English. A that-clause is possible in formal use (a commitment that no jobs would be cut). ✗ a commitment of reducing and ✗ a commitment for reducing are errors.
Pattern
a commitment to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · make / honor / break / renew a commitment · a commitment from [party] · a prior commitment · commitment to [a person or a cause]
Variants
a commitment toward — occasional and slightly off; to is the edited form · a commitment on — bureaucratic, as in a commitment on funding levels
Contrast
The noun forgives what the adjective does not. A commitment to reducing emissions and a commitment to reduce emissions are both good English, because commitment licenses a prepositional complement and an infinitive alike. The adjective allows only the gerund: committed to raising standards, ✗ committed to raise standards — see committed to. A writer moving from the noun to the adjective therefore has to switch from "either" to "-ing only," and that is where the mistake gets made. ✗ a commitment for and ✗ a commitment with are errors: the party who gave the promise takes from (a commitment from the vendor), while commitment to plus a person names devotion rather than a promise (her commitment to the students).

A promise to do something, or a settled devotion to a person or a cause.

  1. The city made a public commitment to cutting emissions in half by 2035.
  2. Her commitment to the students never wavered, even in the worst years.
  3. We need a firm commitment from the vendor before anyone signs.
  4. I have a prior commitment on Thursday, so count me out.
  5. Nothing in the current draft is a commitment to hiring locally.
  6. He broke every commitment he had made to the union that spring.
See also
contribution to, objection to, responsibility for, dependence on/reliance on, to

comparison with/to#

Pronunciation
/kəmˈpɛrɪsən wɪθ/, /kəmˈpɛrɪsən tu/ · kum-PAIR-ih-sun with, kum-PAIR-ih-sun too · weak /kəmˈpɛrɪsən wəθ/, /kəmˈpɛrɪsən tə/
Русский
сравнение с чем-л., сопоставление — рус. «с», не «к»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (no comparison with running a marathon) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a comparison to run a marathon) and never a clause. Comparison between takes two items joined by and: a comparison between the two designs.
Pattern
a comparison with / to [noun phrase] · a comparison between X and Y · a comparison of X with / to Y · in comparison with / to [noun phrase] · by comparison · there's no comparison
Variants
comparison with — the analytical default, and the usual form in academic prose · comparison to — commoner in speech and in the figurative sense · both are standard American English
Contrast
The verb splits where the noun does not. Compare X with Y means measure them against each other point by point; compare X to Y means liken them, assert a resemblance — he compared the mayor to Nero is an insult, he compared the mayor with Nero is a study. The noun tolerates either preposition for both jobs, which is why the distinction survives mainly in careful writing. ✗ comparison than is an error (than belongs with the adjective different, see difference between/in), and by comparison takes no object at all — ✗ by comparison with the others, by comparison is a doubling.

The act of setting two things side by side, or the relation that results.

  1. The report makes a careful comparison with three neighboring districts.
  2. Any comparison to the 1970s energy crisis is going to be inexact.
  3. There's simply no comparison between the two engines.
  4. In comparison with last winter, this one has been mild.
  5. The second draft is, by comparison, a model of clarity.
  6. A side-by-side comparison of the two policies is on page four.
See also
contrast to/with, difference between/in, similarity to/between, relationship with/between, with

complaint about#

Pronunciation
/kəmˈpleɪnt əˈbaʊt/ · kum-PLAYNT uh-BOWT · weak /kəmˈpleɪnt ˈbaʊt/
Русский
жалоба на — рус. «на», не «о» (✗ жалоба о качестве)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
about: noun phrase, gerund (a complaint about being kept waiting), or wh-clause (complaints about how the tests are graded) — never a to-infinitive. against: a person or institution, in the formal sense of a filed grievance. The noun also takes a bare that-clause: a complaint that the heat was never turned on.
Pattern
a complaint about [noun phrase / verb-ing] · a complaint against [person / company] · a complaint to [authority] · a complaint that [clause] · file / lodge / make a complaint · a complaint of [symptom]
Variants
complain about — the everyday verb · complain of — used for symptoms and formal grievances (he complained of chest pain)
Contrast
Four prepositions, four roles: about is the subject, against is the party accused, to is the authority receiving it, and of is reserved for symptoms and legal pleading — she complained of dizziness. Choosing against for a subject produces ✗ a complaint against the noise, which reads as though the noise had been sued. The verb takes no direct object: ✗ he complained the delay, ✗ I complained the manager; it must be complained about the delay, complained to the manager.

An expression of dissatisfaction, formal or otherwise.

  1. We've had four complaints about the noise from the loading dock.
  2. She filed a formal complaint against her supervisor.
  3. A complaint to the state board takes about ninety days to process.
  4. His only complaint about the apartment was the water pressure.
  5. The patient came in complaining of numbness in her left hand.
  6. There were complaints about being asked to work through lunch.
See also
objection to, doubt about, problem with, argument with/about, complain to, complain about, about

compliance with#

Pronunciation
/kəmˈplaɪəns wɪθ/ · kum-PLY-unss with · weak /kəmˈplaɪəns wəθ/
Русский
соблюдение чего-л., выполнение требований — рус. родительный, не «с»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — regulatory, corporate, and clinical writing; in American companies Compliance is also the name of a department
Complement
a noun phrase naming a rule, order, standard, or request — never a gerund (✗ compliance with filing on time; write compliance with the filing deadline), never an infinitive, and never a clause.
Pattern
compliance with [a rule / an order / a standard / a subpoena] · in compliance with [noun phrase] · full / substantial / voluntary compliance · bring [something] into compliance with [noun phrase] · a compliance officer (compound)
Contrast
✗ Compliance to is the error, and it is everywhere — in vendor emails, in ISO paperwork, in job listings. The noun takes with and only with, matching the verb comply with and the adjective compliant with. Against adherence to, which takes to and only to: the two nouns mean nearly the same thing and take opposite prepositions, which is exactly why each attracts the other's. American medicine has largely swapped patient compliance with a regimen for adherence to it. Note that in compliance with (the complex preposition) is stiffer than the plain noun and belongs in filings, not in email.

The act or state of doing what a rule, order, or standard requires.

  1. The plant was cited for failure of compliance with the emissions limit.
  2. We are in compliance with every condition of the permit as of Friday.
  3. Compliance with the subpoena took four paralegals and a weekend.
  4. Voluntary compliance with the guideline has been about sixty percent.
  5. Bringing the stairwell into compliance with the code cost more than the roof.
  6. She runs compliance for a mid-sized bank in Columbus.
See also
adherence to, conformity with/to, breach of, exemption from, comply with, in compliance with

concern about/for#

Pronunciation
/kənˈsɜrn əˈbaʊt/, /kənˈsɜrn fɔr/ · kun-SURN uh-BOWT, kun-SURN for · weak /kənˈsɜrn ˈbaʊt/, /kənˈsɜrn fɚ/
Русский
about — беспокойство из-за чего-л.; for — забота о ком-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
about / over: what worries you — noun phrase, gerund (concern about losing the contract), or wh-clause. for: a person or a thing whose welfare is at stake. with: what someone is occupied with, an entirely different sense. The noun also takes a bare that-clause: concern that the levee would not hold. Never a to-infinitive.
Pattern
concern about / over [issue] · concern for [person / welfare] · concern with [subject matter] · concern that [clause] · of concern to [person] · express / raise / voice concern about [issue]
Variants
concern about — neutral · concern over — the same, slightly more journalistic · concerns regarding — bureaucratic
Contrast
The three-way split is the whole entry. Concern about the bridge is worry that something is wrong with it; concern for the residents is care about their welfare; concern with the bridge is having the bridge as your subject or business — the report's concern with rural roads. Saying ✗ concern for the delay misfires, because delays do not have welfare. ✗ concern on is wrong. Note the third life of this word: the modern adjective concerning meaning "worrying" (a concerning trend) is separate from the preposition concerning meaning "about," for which see concerning.

Worry about something, care for someone, or the subject someone is occupied with.

  1. There's growing concern about the condition of the levee.
  2. Her concern for the older residents kept the shelter open another week.
  3. The book's central concern with labor history is clear from the first chapter.
  4. The board raised concerns over the auditor's independence.
  5. Water quality is of real concern to everyone downstream.
  6. Concern that the vote had been miscounted led to a manual recount.
See also
worry about, doubt about, interest in, sympathy for/with, concerned about, for

confidence in#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑnfɪdəns ɪn/ · KON-fih-dunss in · weak /ˈkɑnfɪdəns ən/
Русский
уверенность в ком-л./чём-л., доверие к чьим-л. способностям
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (confidence in getting it done on time) — never a to-infinitive after in (✗ confidence in to finish). The noun separately takes a to-infinitive with no preposition, meaning self-assurance: the confidence to ask for a raise. It also takes a bare that-clause: confidence that the funding will come through.
Pattern
confidence in [person / thing] · the confidence to [verb] · confidence that [clause] · have / lose / restore confidence in [noun phrase] · a vote of confidence in [person] · in confidence
Variants
confidence about — heard in speech about a future outcome (confident about the exam), but in is the noun's fixed preposition
Contrast
In is the only preposition for the object of your trust: ✗ confidence on the team, ✗ confidence to the team, ✗ confidence of the team — that last one means the team's own confidence, not yours in them. Distinguish the two frames: confidence in speaking is trust placed in the activity, while the confidence to speak is the nerve to do it. Note also in confidence (privately) and a confidence (a secret told), both unrelated senses. The adjective is confident in a person and confident about an outcome.

Trust that someone or something will perform.

  1. I have complete confidence in the surgeon who did my mother's knee.
  2. The board's confidence in the CEO has visibly eroded.
  3. She lacked the confidence to speak up in the first three meetings.
  4. Investors lost confidence in the currency within a week.
  5. He told me in confidence, so I can't repeat it.
  6. There's little confidence that the deadline will hold.
See also
belief in, faith in, dependence on/reliance on, doubt about, confident of, in

conformity with/to#

Pronunciation
/kənˈfɔrməti wɪθ/, /kənˈfɔrməti tu/ · kun-FOR-muh-tee with, kun-FOR-muh-tee too · weak /kənˈfɔrməti wəθ/, /kənˈfɔrməti tə/
Русский
соответствие чему-л. — согласованность со стандартом или образцом
Type
noun + preposition
Register
formal — specifications, audits, court opinions, and theology; the social sense (conformity without a complement) is neutral
Complement
a noun phrase naming the standard, rule, or model — never a gerund (✗ conformity with reporting quarterly) and never a clause.
Pattern
conformity with / to [a standard / a rule / a type] · in conformity with [noun phrase] · bring [something] into conformity with [noun phrase] · a certificate of conformity · conformity (bare, the social pressure sense)
Variants
conformity with and conformity to are both standard American English and interchangeable in most sentences; with is commoner in legal and regulatory prose, to in engineering and in the sense "matching a model." Manufacturing also uses conformance to, a narrower technical variant.
Contrast
Against compliance with: compliance is obedience to something binding, conformity is resemblance to a pattern — which is why goods conform to a specification but a firm complies with an order. ✗ conformity of the rules is an error for conformity with. The verb keeps both (conform to the spec, conform with the requirements), and the adjective is conformable to, now rare. Note the bare social noun, which takes no preposition at all: the pressure toward conformity in a high school.

Agreement with a standard, rule, or model, or the act of matching one.

  1. The inspector certified conformity with the 2021 electrical code.
  2. Each casting is checked for conformity to the drawing before it ships.
  3. The order was entered in conformity with the parties' stipulation.
  4. Nothing in the design is in conformity with what the client approved.
  5. Conformity to type is what breeders are paid to judge.
  6. The whole essay is about conformity and none of it is about conforming.
See also
compliance with, adherence to, consistent with, in accordance with, with

connection with/to/between#

Pronunciation
/kəˈnɛkʃən wɪθ/ · kuh-NEK-shun with · weak /kəˈnɛkʃən wəθ/, /kəˈnɛkʃən tə/
Русский
связь с чем-л. / между чем-л. — соединение или отношение
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
with / to: a single noun phrase. between: two or more items joined by and (a connection between the leak and the bad valve). Gerunds are possible (no connection with smuggling arms); never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause (✗ a connection that the two are related).
Pattern
a connection with / to [noun phrase] · a connection between X and Y · in connection with [noun phrase] · make / see / deny a connection · a connection to [network / flight]
Variants
connection with — association, involvement · connection to — a physical or network link, and increasingly the general form in American speech
Contrast
Between needs two things and the others need one. ✗ a connection between the fire is incomplete; ✗ a connection with the fire and the wiring should be between. Connection to is the everyday choice for a physical or digital link (a connection to the water main, a connection to the airport) and for a personal tie (she has a connection to the family), while connection with carries the sense of involvement, which is why questioned in connection with the robbery means suspected of it. The fixed phrase is in connection with, not ✗ in connection to.

A link, relationship, or association between things or people.

  1. Police questioned two men in connection with the break-in.
  2. There's no proven connection between the additive and the symptoms.
  3. The house still has a connection to the old well.
  4. She has a family connection with the shipyard going back four generations.
  5. I missed my connection in Denver by nine minutes.
  6. Investigators see a connection between the two fires.
See also
link between/to, relationship with/between, reference to, similarity to/between, with

consensus on/about#

Pronunciation
/kənˈsɛnsəs ɑn/, /kənˈsɛnsəs əˈbaʊt/ · kun-SEN-sus on, kun-SEN-sus uh-BOWT · weak /kənˈsɛnsəs ən/; about clips to /ˈbaʊt/ in fast speech
Русский
консенсус по чему-л., общее согласие в группе
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
on / about: a noun phrase or gerund (consensus on raising the levy) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a consensus to raise the levy). among / within: the group that holds it. The noun also takes a bare that-clause: the consensus that the levee will hold.
Pattern
a consensus on / about [topic] · consensus among [a group] · the consensus that [clause] · reach / build / break a consensus · by consensus · the scientific consensus
Contrast
Two usage fights ride on this word. Consensus of opinion is criticized as redundant, since a consensus is already an agreement of opinion, and general consensus draws the same complaint; both are old, both are common, and both are worth cutting in edited prose. ✗ concensus is a misspelling that survives spellcheck in nobody's favor. ✗ a consensus to postpone is the infinitive error — write a consensus on postponing or an agreement to postpone, since agreement with/on does take the infinitive and consensus does not.

General agreement within a group, or the position that group holds.

  1. There's no consensus on whether to rebuild the pier or remove it.
  2. The committee reached a consensus about the timeline in one afternoon.
  3. Consensus among the county's engineers has shifted twice since March.
  4. The scientific consensus that the warming is human-caused is not in serious dispute.
  5. They never got to a consensus on funding the second position.
  6. The board decided by consensus rather than by a recorded vote.
See also
agreement with/on, disagreement with/about, opinion on/of/about, view on/of, on

consequence of#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑnsəkwɛns ʌv/ · KON-suh-kwenss uv · weak /ˈkɑnsəkwəns əv/ → /ˈkɑnsəkwəns ə/
Русский
следствие, последствие чего-л. — обычно нежелательное
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (the consequence of ignoring the warning) — never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause. In the separate sense "importance," it takes to: a matter of no consequence to us.
Pattern
a consequence of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · as a consequence of [noun phrase] · the consequences for [person / group] · of no consequence to [person] · face / suffer the consequences
Contrast
Of names the cause; for names who bears it. The consequences of the flood are what the flood produced; the consequences for homeowners are what homeowners now face. Both can appear together: the consequences of the flood for homeowners. ✗ a consequence to the flood is an error, though of no consequence to me is correct in the unrelated "importance" sense. Compare result of, which is neutral, where consequence usually implies something unwelcome, and note the fixed connector as a consequence of, treated in full at as a consequence of.

Something that follows from an earlier action, usually an unwelcome one.

  1. Higher premiums are a direct consequence of two bad fire seasons.
  2. As a consequence of the recount, certification slipped by a week.
  3. Nobody modeled the consequences for small landlords.
  4. The consequence of ignoring the warning light was a $2,000 repair.
  5. He's old enough to face the consequences of his own decisions.
  6. The typo was, fortunately, of no consequence.
See also
result of, effect on, cause of, impact on, of

consideration for#

Pronunciation
/kənˌsɪdəˈreɪʃən fɔr/ · kun-sid-uh-RAY-shun for · weak /kənˌsɪdəˈreɪʃən fɚ/
Русский
внимание, уважение к кому-л.; юр. встречное предоставление по договору
Type
noun + preposition
Register
senses 1 and 2 neutral; sense 3 is legal — the word that decides whether a promise is enforceable, met on the first page of almost every American contract and in the first month of law school
Complement
a noun phrase or gerund (consideration for agreeing not to compete) — never a to-infinitive (✗ consideration for be promoted, ✗ consideration to promote her) and never a clause. In the courtesy sense the complement is a person or their interests; in the contract sense it is the promise being paid for.
Pattern
consideration for [a person / a position / a promise] · out of consideration for [someone] · under consideration (no complement) · take [something] into consideration · in consideration of [a promise] (the contract formula) · good and valuable consideration
Contrast
Three unrelated nouns, one spelling. Consideration for the neighbors is courtesy; consideration for the job is being weighed as a candidate; consideration for the promise is the dollar or the peppercorn that makes a contract binding. Only the first two are interchangeable in tone; the third is a term of art, and in a contract in consideration of is a fixed formula — see in consideration of. ✗ consideration to the neighbors is an error, and considerate of is the adjective (considerate of her neighbors, not ✗ considerate for). Note under consideration, which takes no complement at all.

A courtesy shown to others, the weighing of a candidate, or — in contract law — the price paid for a promise.

1. Thoughtfulness toward other people. Regard for someone else's comfort or feelings. The complement is a person or a group, and the phrase often appears as out of consideration for.

  1. Out of consideration for the neighbors, they moved the band inside at ten.
  2. He shows no consideration for anyone who has to drive behind him.

2. Being weighed as a candidate. The complement is a position, an award, or a slot, and the noun almost always follows for.

  1. Her name is under consideration for the deputy's job.
  2. Submitting after Friday means no consideration for this year's fellowship.

3. In contract law, the thing of value exchanged for a promise. Without it a promise is a gift and unenforceable. American drafters open with the formula in consideration of the mutual covenants herein, and the amount can be nominal — the classic recital is ten dollars and other good and valuable consideration.

  1. The court found no consideration for the modification and refused to enforce it.
  2. A promise to do what you already owe is not consideration for anything.
See also
concern about/for, respect for, eligibility for, in consideration of, for

contrast to/with#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑntræst tu/, /ˈkɑntræst wɪθ/ · KON-trast too, KON-trast with · weak /ˈkɑntræst tə/, /ˈkɑntræst wəθ/
Русский
контраст с чем-л., противопоставление — рус. «с», не «к»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (in contrast to spending a fortune on consultants) — never a to-infinitive (✗ in contrast to spend) and never a clause (✗ in contrast to that he left; write in contrast to his departure). A contrast between takes two items joined by and.
Pattern
in contrast to / with [noun phrase] · a contrast between X and Y · by contrast (no object) · a contrast to [noun phrase] · stand in stark contrast to [noun phrase]
Variants
in contrast to and in contrast with — both standard American English; to is commoner and slightly lighter
Contrast
Watch the -ing rule in in contrast to. The to is a preposition, so in contrast to hiring outside counsel, never ✗ in contrast to hire outside counsel — the same trap as alternative to and objection to. ✗ in contrast of is an error. By contrast takes no object at all and is a sentence adverb: By contrast, the second quarter was flat — ✗ by contrast to the second quarter is a blend of two constructions. Against comparison with/to, which looks for likeness, contrast insists on difference. The verb stresses differently from the noun: CON-trast the noun, con-TRAST the verb.

A striking difference, or the act of setting differences side by side.

  1. In contrast to last year's turnout, this election was quiet.
  2. There's a sharp contrast between the two neighborhoods on either side of the highway.
  3. Her calm stood in stark contrast to the panic in the hallway.
  4. The southern route, by contrast, adds four hours and no scenery.
  5. The essay draws a contrast between what the law says and what the clerks do.
  6. In contrast with the federal rule, the state standard has no exemption.
See also
comparison with/to, difference between/in, similarity to/between, opposition to, with

contribution to#

Pronunciation
/ˌkɑntrɪˈbjuʃən tu/ · kon-trih-BYOO-shun too · weak /ˌkɑntrɪˈbjuʃən tə/
Русский
вклад в — рус. «в», не «к» (вклад в науку)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (a contribution to reducing emissions) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a contribution to reduce emissions). This is one of the highest-frequency -ing traps in the file, because the sentence reads perfectly well as an infinitive and is wrong.
Pattern
a contribution to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · a contribution toward [goal / cost] · a contribution of [amount / item] · make a contribution to [noun phrase] · contributions from [donors]
Variants
contribution toward — a partial payment against a total (a contribution toward the deductible) · contribution to — everything else
Contrast
The to is a preposition, so the verb after it takes -ing: a contribution to solving the shortage, never ✗ a contribution to solve the shortage. Test it with a noun — a contribution to the solution — and the trap is obvious. ✗ a contribution for the fund is wrong in the donation sense (use to), though a contribution for the holiday party names a purpose and is fine. The verb keeps the same preposition and takes no direct object: she contributed to the fund, ✗ she contributed the fund, though contribute money to the fund is correct.

Something given to help a cause, or a part played in a result.

  1. Her contribution to the field was mostly methodological.
  2. The company makes a monthly contribution to each employee's retirement account.
  3. Poor drainage made a real contribution to the flooding.
  4. We're asking for a small contribution toward the cost of the bus.
  5. His biggest contribution to getting the bill passed was staying quiet.
  6. Contributions from alumni were down eleven percent.
See also
supply of, commitment to, share in/of, responsibility for, attention to, contribute to, to

control over/of#

Pronunciation
/kənˈtroʊl ˈoʊvɚ/, /kənˈtroʊl ʌv/ · kun-TROHL OH-ver, kun-TROHL uv · weak over has no weak form; of → /əv/ → /ə/
Русский
контроль над, власть над чем-л.; также владение чем-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun. A gerund is possible but rare (control over what gets published is the natural form, using a wh-clause). Never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause. Controls on takes a noun naming what is being restricted: controls on spending.
Pattern
control over / of [noun phrase] · in control of [noun phrase] · under the control of [person] · out of control · lose / take / regain control of [noun phrase] · controls on [activity]
Variants
control over — power to direct, especially over people and processes · control of — possession of the thing itself, and the usual form in take control of, lose control of
Contrast
The plural is a different word. Control over the budget is authority; controls on the budget are the rules restricting it, and ✗ control on the budget is an error in the authority sense. Of and over overlap heavily — control of the company and control over the company are both fine — but only of works in the fixed phrases in control of, out of control, lose control of the car. ✗ control to and ✗ control for are wrong, though control for is a real technical verb in statistics (controlled for income).

Power to direct something, or possession of it.

  1. The trustees have no control over how the money is spent day to day.
  2. She lost control of the car on black ice near the county line.
  3. The family retained control of the newspaper until 1997.
  4. The fire was out of control within twenty minutes.
  5. New controls on short-term rentals take effect in January.
  6. He's not in control of his own calendar anymore.
See also
authority on/over, influence on/over, limit to/on, responsibility for, over

conversation with#

Pronunciation
/ˌkɑnvɚˈseɪʃən wɪθ/ · kon-ver-SAY-shun with · weak /ˌkɑnvɚˈseɪʃən wəθ/
Русский
разговор, беседа с кем-л. — тема вводится через about
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
with: a person or group — never a gerund (✗ a conversation with explaining it) and never a clause. about / on: the topic, as a noun phrase or gerund (a conversation about selling the house). Never a to-infinitive after either (✗ a conversation to sell the house, unless to sell is a purpose clause).
Pattern
a conversation with [person] · a conversation about / on [topic] · have / start / end / overhear a conversation · in conversation with [person] · make conversation (small talk) · a conversation between [two people]
Contrast
With names the other party and never to: ✗ a conversation to my landlord is an error, even though the verb talk allows both talk to and talk with — see talk to, talk with, talk about. A conversation between us views the two speakers from outside; a conversation with you views it from my side. American usage note: since about 2010 a conversation about has become office-speak for a difficult topic (we need to have a conversation about attendance), which is a euphemism, not a new grammar.

An exchange of talk between people, and what it is about.

  1. I had a long conversation with the county clerk about the easement.
  2. Her conversation with the loan officer lasted four minutes.
  3. He overheard a conversation between two of the contractors in the stairwell.
  4. That's a conversation for after the audit, not before it.
  5. We should have a conversation about who covers weekends.
  6. She is in conversation with two publishers and will not say which.

appointment with, interview with, discussion about/of, argument with/about, talk to, talk with, talk about, with

correlation with/between#

Pronunciation
/ˌkɔrəˈleɪʃən wɪθ/, /ˌkɔrəˈleɪʃən bɪˈtwin/ · kor-uh-LAY-shun with, kor-uh-LAY-shun bih-TWEEN · weak /ˌkɔrəˈleɪʃən wəθ/, /ˌkɔrəˈleɪʃən bəˈtwin/
Русский
корреляция с / между — согласованное изменение двух величин
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; a technical term in statistics and a loose one in journalism
Complement
between: two variables joined by and. with: a single second variable. Both take noun phrases; a gerund is possible but stiff (a correlation with starting school later). Never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause.
Pattern
a correlation between X and Y · a correlation with [variable] · a strong / weak / positive / negative / spurious correlation · correlate with [variable] (the verb) · the correlation coefficient
Contrast
✗ Correlation to is the error that gets past most editors; the noun takes between or with, and so does the verb — X correlates with Y, never ✗ correlates to Y. Between needs two named terms: ✗ a correlation between income is incomplete. And the sentence every statistics course exists to teach: correlation is not causation — a correlation between ice cream sales and drownings is real and explains nothing, which is why cause of claims more than association with/between, and association claims more than a mere correlation coefficient.

A measured tendency for two variables to move together.

  1. There's a strong correlation between household income and third-grade reading scores.
  2. Sales show almost no correlation with the weather in this market.
  3. The correlation between the two indexes broke down in 2020.
  4. Any correlation with the treatment disappears once you control for age.
  5. She warned the board that a correlation between the two lines proves nothing.
  6. The reported correlation between screen time and sleep is weaker than the headline.
See also
association with/between, link between/to, relationship with/between, ratio of ... to, cause of, between

criterion for#

Pronunciation
/kraɪˈtɪriən fɔr/ · kry-TEER-ee-un for · weak /kraɪˈtɪriən fɚ/; the plural is /kraɪˈtɪriə/ · kry-TEER-ee-uh
Русский
критерий чего-л. — мерило отбора или оценки (мн. ч. criteria)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
a noun phrase or gerund (a criterion for awarding the contract) — never a to-infinitive (✗ the criteria to award the contract) and never a that-clause. The frame the criterion by which + clause is the formal alternative.
Pattern
a criterion for [selection / eligibility / admission] · the criteria for [noun phrase / verb-ing] · meet / satisfy / fail the criteria · the criterion by which [clause] · on what criteria [clause]
Contrast
The countability rule is the trap. Criterion is singular and criteria is plural: ✗ a criteria, ✗ this criteria is, ✗ criterias. Write one criterion, three criteria, these criteria are. The error is now so common in job listings and grant guidelines that it is drifting toward acceptance, but it is still marked in edited prose. Against grounds for: a criterion is a test you apply in advance, grounds are reasons you already have. ✗ criterion of eligibility is not wrong but is stiffer than for.

A standard or test by which something is judged or selected.

  1. Cost is only one criterion for awarding the contract.
  2. The criteria for admission changed the year after she applied.
  3. Two applicants met every criterion for the fellowship and one got it.
  4. What criteria are you using to rank these?
  5. Residency is not a criterion for jury service in this county.
  6. The single criterion for replacement is whether the part has cracked.
See also
grounds for, basis for, eligibility for, threshold for, rule against/for, for

cure for#

Pronunciation
/kjʊr fɔr/ · kyoor for · weak /kjʊr fɚ/
Русский
лекарство от, средство от — рус. «от», не «для»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase naming a disease or a problem (a cure for hiccups). A gerund is unusual but possible (a cure for waking up at three a.m.). Never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
a cure for [disease / problem] · find / seek a cure for [noun phrase] · beyond cure · the cure is worse than the disease
Contrast
For names the ailment; of belongs to the verb and names the patient's affliction. A cure for insomnia is the remedy; cure someone of insomnia is what a doctor does — ✗ cure someone for insomnia and ✗ a cure of insomnia are both wrong. ✗ cure to and ✗ cure against are errors; the near-synonyms diverge, with remedy for, treatment for, vaccine against or for, and antidote to or for. Note the unrelated culinary and industrial verb (cured ham, cured concrete), which takes no preposition at all.

A remedy that ends a disease or a persistent problem.

  1. There's still no cure for the common cold.
  2. Everyone in the family has a different cure for hiccups.
  3. The trial is testing a possible cure for sickle cell disease.
  4. Sunlight is not a cure for a badly designed office.
  5. The drug cured him of the infection but wrecked his hearing.
  6. By then the rot was beyond cure.
See also
solution to, protection from/against, damage to, danger of/to, for

custody of#

Pronunciation
/ˈkʌstədi ʌv/ · KUSS-tuh-dee uv · weak /ˈkʌstədi əv/ → /ˈkʌstədi ə/
Русский
опека над кем-л., попечение; хранение чего-л.; юр. также содержание под стражей
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — family court, criminal procedure, records management; an ordinary American word because of divorce and arrest
Complement
a noun phrase naming a person, a thing, or a record — never a gerund (✗ custody of keeping the files), never an infinitive, and never a clause.
Pattern
custody of [a child / the records / the evidence] · in the custody of [an authority] · take [someone] into custody · have / share / lose custody · joint / sole / physical / legal custody · the chain of custody
Contrast
Of
is the only preposition the noun takes
✗ custody over the children and ✗ custody on the evidence are errors, even though jurisdiction and control do take over — see jurisdiction over and control over/of. Two senses live side by side and neither uses a different preposition: custody of a child is a parental right, and in custody alone means under arrest. In American family law legal custody is decision-making and physical custody is where the child sleeps; in criminal cases the chain of custody is the paper trail on a piece of evidence, and breaking it can lose the case.

The right or duty of keeping and caring for someone or something.

  1. She has legal custody of both children and he has alternate weekends.
  2. The suspect has been in the custody of the marshals since Tuesday.
  3. Custody of the original tapes passed to the university in 1998.
  4. The defense attacked the chain of custody of the blood sample.
  5. Two deputies took him into custody outside the courthouse.
  6. The clerk has custody of every filing in the case.
See also
possession of, control over/of, jurisdiction over, responsibility for, title to, of
