damage to#

Pronunciation
/ˈdæmɪdʒ tu/ · DAM-ij too · weak /ˈdæmɪdʒ tə/
Русский
ущерб, вред, повреждение чему-л. — рус. дательный падеж
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun naming the thing harmed (damage to the roof, damage to her reputation). Never a gerund, never a to-infinitive, never a clause. Damage from names the cause: damage from the storm.
Pattern
damage to [thing harmed] · damage from / by [cause] · do / cause damage to [noun phrase] · the damage was done · damages (plural) for [loss]
Contrast
To for the victim, from for the cause — damage to the siding from hail. ✗ damage of the roof is an error, and so is ✗ damage on the roof. The plural is a legal term, not a plural: damages means money awarded by a court, so he sued for damages is compensation and he inspected the damage is the harm itself — ✗ the damages to my car were extensive is a common and conspicuous slip. The verb takes a direct object and no preposition: the hail damaged the siding, ✗ damaged to the siding.

Physical or reputational harm done to something.

  1. There's serious water damage to the subfloor under the sink.
  2. Damage to the levee will take two seasons to repair.
  3. The leak did more damage to the building's reputation than to the building.
  4. Most of the damage from the storm was in the eastern half of the county.
  5. By the time anyone noticed, the damage was done.
  6. The jury awarded damages of $1.2 million.
See also
threat to, responsibility for, effect on, protection from/against, to

damages for#

Pronunciation
/ˈdæmɪdʒɪz fɔr/ · DAM-ij-iz for · weak /ˈdæmɪdʒɪz fɚ/
Русский
юр. убытки, возмещение за — присуждённая судом компенсация, всегда мн. ч.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
legal — complaints, jury instructions, settlement letters; an American meets it in a demand letter or on a small-claims form
Complement
for: the wrong or loss being compensated, as a noun phrase or gerund (damages for failing to warn). of: an amount (damages of $2.4 million). against: the party ordered to pay. Never a to-infinitive (✗ damages to compensate her as a complement of the noun) and never a clause.
Pattern
damages for [breach / injury / lost wages] · damages of [an amount] · damages against [a defendant] · sue / recover / award damages · compensatory / punitive / liquidated / nominal damages · treble damages
Contrast
Damage is harm and takes to; damages is money and takes for. Damage to the roof is the hole; damages for the damage to the roof is the check. ✗ damages to the roof is one of the commonest errors in insurance correspondence, and it converts a physical fact into a legal claim. The plural has no singular in this sense: ✗ a damage of $500. Note the stacking, exactly as with tax on: damages of $40,000 for lost profits against the general contractor.

Money awarded by a court to compensate for a loss or wrong.

  1. She sued for damages for lost wages and emotional distress.
  2. The jury awarded damages of $1.2 million against the hospital.
  3. Damages for breach of contract are limited to what the parties could foresee.
  4. Punitive damages are rare in this state and capped when they happen.
  5. The clause fixes liquidated damages for each day the work runs late.
  6. He recovered nothing in damages for the delay because he could not prove it.
See also
damage to, claim against/for/to, liability for/to, remedy for/against, judgment against/for, penalty for

danger of/to#

Pronunciation
/ˈdeɪndʒɚ ʌv/, /ˈdeɪndʒɚ tu/ · DAYN-jer uv, DAYN-jer too · weak /ˈdeɪndʒɚ əv/ → /ˈdeɪndʒɚ ə/; /ˈdeɪndʒɚ tə/
Русский
of — опасность чего-л. (самого исхода); to — угроза для кого-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: noun phrase or gerund naming the bad outcome (the danger of infection, in danger of losing the house) — never a to-infinitive (✗ in danger to lose the house). to: a person or thing at risk. The noun also takes a bare that-clause: the danger that the fund runs dry.
Pattern
the danger of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · a danger to [person / thing at risk] · in danger of [verb-ing] · out of danger · the danger that [clause]
Contrast
Of is what might happen; to is who it happens to. The danger of fire is the outcome feared; a danger to firefighters is who is exposed. Swapping them gives ✗ the danger to fire. In danger of is a fixed frame that requires -ing: in danger of collapsing, ✗ in danger to collapse — the single commonest error with this word. ✗ danger for is wrong in American English, though it is a natural transfer from French and Spanish. Compare risk of, which behaves identically and is commoner in technical prose.

Exposure to harm, or the harmful outcome itself.

  1. There's a real danger of the ceiling coming down.
  2. Loose gravel on that curve is a danger to cyclists.
  3. Two of the buildings are in danger of collapsing.
  4. She was out of danger by Tuesday morning.
  5. The danger of signing before an inspection is that you own the problem.
  6. Untreated, the infection poses a danger to the whole ward.
See also
risk of, threat to, chance of, protection from/against, of

default on/under#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈfɔlt ɑn/, /dɪˈfɔlt ˈʌndɚ/ · dih-FAWLT on, dih-FAWLT UN-der · weak /dɪˈfɔlt ən/; under has no weak form
Русский
юр. неисполнение обязательства, дефолт по кредиту — просрочка в срок платежа
Type
noun + preposition
Register
on is financial and neutral — mortgages, student loans, sovereign debt; under is legal drafting, met in the "Events of Default" section of a loan agreement or a commercial lease
Complement
on: the obligation not met — a loan, a payment, a bond. under: the instrument the default arises beneath — an agreement, a lease, a section number. Never a gerund (✗ default on paying the note; write default on the note), never an infinitive, and never a clause.
Pattern
a default on [a loan / a payment] · a default under [an agreement / section 8] · in default of [something owed] (formal) · by default · cure a default · an event of default · a default judgment
Variants
in default of survives in formal and legal prose meaning "for want of" (in default of any other bidder); it is a different frame from default on and is not interchangeable with it
Contrast
On points at the debt, under at the paper. You default on the note and are in default under the credit agreement — the same event described from the borrower's side and the drafter's side. ✗ default of the loan is an error in American usage. Keep the litigation sense separate: a default judgment is entered because a party failed to appear, and win by default means the other side did not show up. In computing the noun means a preset value and takes to on the verb (it defaults to Eastern time), a fourth sense with a fourth preposition.

A failure to meet an obligation when it comes due.

  1. Two missed payments put him in default on the second mortgage.
  2. Any transfer of the shares is an event of default under section 8.
  3. The country's default on its dollar bonds was the first since 2001.
  4. The tenant cured the default within the ten-day window.
  5. She won the seat by default when no one else filed.
  6. A default under the lease also triggers a default on the equipment loan.
See also
breach of, liability for/to, obligation to/under, lien on/against, judgment against/for, hereunder

deference to#

Pronunciation
/ˈdɛfɚəns tu/ · DEF-ur-unss too · weak /ˈdɛfɚəns tə/
Русский
книжн. почтительное подчинение чьему-л. мнению или власти
Type
noun + preposition
Register
formal; a term of art in American administrative law, where courts owe agencies varying degrees of it, and an ordinary courtesy word in out of deference to
Complement
a noun phrase naming the person, body, or judgment yielded to — never a gerund (✗ deference to reading it his way), never an infinitive, and never a clause.
Pattern
deference to [a person / a court / an agency / someone's judgment] · in / out of deference to [noun phrase] · show / owe / accord deference to [noun phrase] · undue / excessive deference · with all due deference (a hedge before disagreeing)
Contrast
Three near neighbors, three different words. Deference is yielding to someone's judgment; deferment and deferral are postponements (a student-loan deferment); difference is another word entirely, and the near-rhyme catches typists. ✗ deference of and ✗ deference toward are errors. The verb splits by sense and preposition: defer to the chair is yielding, defer the payment is postponing — one takes a preposition and one takes a bare object. In American law, deference to an agency is measured (Chevron deference, Skidmore deference) and with all due deference is the formula that introduces a refusal to defer.

Respectful submission to another's judgment, authority, or wishes.

  1. Out of deference to his widow, the department held the announcement a week.
  2. The panel showed unusual deference to the trial judge's findings.
  3. Courts owe some deference to an agency's reading of its own rule.
  4. Her deference to the older partners was strategic and did not last.
  5. In deference to the family, no photographs were taken inside the church.
  6. The report criticizes the board's deference to management on every close question.
See also
adherence to, respect for, attitude to/toward, authority on/over, to

degree in#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈɡri ɪn/ · dih-GREE in · weak /dɪˈɡri ən/
Русский
диплом, учёная степень по специальности; отдельно — степень, градус
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
in: the field of study, as a noun phrase — never a gerund (✗ a degree in nursing patients, though a degree in nursing is a field and fine) and never a clause. from: the institution. of: an amount or extent, in the wholly different sense (a degree of certainty), where a gerund is possible (some degree of guessing).
Pattern
a degree in [field] · a degree from [institution] · a bachelor's / master's / law degree in [field] · a degree of [certainty / difficulty] · to a degree · by degrees · ninety degrees
Contrast
In for the subject, from for the school, and never the reverse: ✗ a degree of economics and ✗ a degree in Michigan are both errors, though a degree from Michigan in economics stacks correctly. The measurement sense takes no preposition at all (it's ninety degrees), and to a degree means "somewhat" with no complement. American usage note: the possessive is a bachelor's degree and a master's degree, with the apostrophe, but an associate degree usually without one, and a doctorate replaces a doctor's degree entirely.

An academic qualification, named by the subject it was earned in — and, separately, an extent or a unit of measure.

  1. She has a degree in environmental engineering from a state school.
  2. He finished a master's degree in library science at night.
  3. A degree in philosophy is not a bad way into law school.
  4. There's always some degree of guessing in a first estimate.
  5. The thermostat was set to seventy-two degrees and nobody believed it.
  6. I agree with him to a degree, and the degree is small.
See also
experience of/in/with, expert on/in, knowledge of/about, success in/at, in, from

delay in#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈleɪ ɪn/ · dih-LAY in · weak /dəˈleɪ ən/
Русский
задержка с чем-л., промедление — рус. чаще «с», а не «в»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
in: a gerund or a noun of process (a delay in processing, a delay in the delivery) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a delay to process the claims). of: a length of time (a delay of two hours). to: what is being held up, chiefly in transport and construction writing (delays to the northbound lanes).
Pattern
a delay in [verb-ing / noun of process] · a delay of [time span] · delays to [service / project] · without delay · there was a delay before [clause]
Variants
delays to — normal in British transport English and in American engineering prose, less usual in general American writing, where delays in or delays on is commoner
Contrast
In for the activity held up, of for how long. A delay in shipping is the holdup itself; a delay of three days is its size; ✗ a delay of shipping mixes them. The gerund is obligatory after in — ✗ a delay in to notify the family, ✗ a delay in notify the family. The verb takes a direct object with no preposition (the storm delayed the flight, ✗ delayed to the flight) and a gerund when it is the subject's own action (he delayed telling her, ✗ he delayed to tell her).

A holdup, and the length of one.

  1. A delay in processing the claims pushed everything into the next quarter.
  2. There was a delay of almost four hours at the border.
  3. Any delay in seeking treatment makes the outcome worse.
  4. The bridge work is causing delays to the northbound lanes.
  5. Report the loss without delay.
  6. The delay in notifying the families became the center of the inquiry.
See also
difficulty with/in, problem with, limit to/on, progress in/on, in

demand for#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈmænd fɔr/ · dih-MAND for · weak /dəˈmænd fɚ/
Русский
спрос на что-л.; настойчивое требование чего-л. — рус. «на», не «для»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase naming what is wanted (demand for housing). A gerund is unusual after the economic sense; the "insistent request" sense takes a to-infinitive with no preposition (a demand to see the warrant) and a that-clause with the subjunctive (a demand that he resign, not ✗ that he resigns).
Pattern
demand for [goods / services] · a demand to [verb] · a demand that [clause with subjunctive] · in demand · meet / satisfy the demand for [noun phrase] · the demands of [job / role]
Contrast
Two nouns, two grammars. The economic noun is uncountable and takes for: demand for rentals is up. The "insistent request" noun is countable and takes an infinitive or a subjunctive clause: a demand to see the manager, a demand that the fee be waived. A third pattern, the demands of the job, means the requirements it imposes — ✗ the demands for the job means something else entirely. ✗ demand of housing is an error. Note that the American subjunctive after demand that keeps the bare verb: that he resign, not that he resigns.

The wish or need for something, or an insistent request.

  1. Demand for one-bedroom apartments has outrun supply for six years.
  2. There's growing demand for bilingual paralegals.
  3. The union's demand for a cost-of-living clause held up the whole contract.
  4. She made a formal demand to inspect the books.
  5. Welders are very much in demand right now.
  6. The demands of the job finally got to him.
See also
need for, supply of, request for, order for, for

departure from#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈpɑrtʃɚ frʌm/ · dih-PAR-cher frum · weak /dəˈpɑrtʃɚ frəm/
Русский
отъезд, отправление из; также отход от заведённой практики
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
from: a place left, or a norm broken (a departure from tradition, a departure from the airport). A gerund is possible in the abstract sense (a departure from doing it by hand). for: the destination (departure for Lisbon). Never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
a departure from [place / norm] · departure for [destination] · departure time · a radical / sharp departure from [precedent] · on / upon departure
Contrast
From is where you left; for is where you are headed — departure from Newark for Lisbon. ✗ departure to Lisbon is a widespread error, and arrival runs the other way (arrival in Lisbon from Newark, ✗ arrival to Lisbon). The abstract sense is the commoner one in writing: a departure from precedent means a break with it, and ✗ a departure of precedent is wrong. The verb takes from likewise (depart from the script) and is transitive only in travel announcements (flights departing Gate 14).

A leaving, or a break with an established practice.

  1. The ruling is a sharp departure from thirty years of precedent.
  2. Departure from the north terminal is now through Gate 44.
  3. Her cover of the song is a real departure from the original.
  4. Any departure from the written protocol has to be logged.
  5. Departure for the base camp is at four in the morning.
  6. The chairman's abrupt departure from the board surprised everybody.
See also
escape from, change in/of, exception to, difference between/in, from

dependence on/reliance on#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈpɛndəns ɑn/, /rɪˈlaɪəns ɑn/ · dih-PEN-dunss on, rih-LY-unss on · weak /dəˈpɛndəns ən/, /rəˈlaɪəns ən/
Русский
зависимость от чего-л., опора на что-л. — рус. «от», хотя англ. on
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (dependence on borrowing, reliance on being bailed out) — never a to-infinitive (✗ reliance on be bailed out) and never a that-clause.
Pattern
dependence / reliance on [noun phrase / verb-ing] · dependence of X on Y · reduce / end dependence on [noun phrase] · in reliance on [noun phrase] · dependent on [noun phrase]
Variants
dependence upon / reliance upon — identical in meaning, more formal and commoner in legal and academic writing · dependency on — the same noun in bureaucratic and clinical registers (chemical dependency)
Contrast
On is fixed and there is no alternative: ✗ dependence of foreign oil, ✗ dependence to foreign oil, ✗ reliance in the report. The one place of is right is the two-place technical frame the dependence of yield on temperature, where of names the variable and on still names what it depends on. The related adjective and verb keep on as well — dependent on the weather, it depends on the weather — and ✗ depend of, ✗ depend from are the classic transfer errors from French and Italian. Bare It depends. with no complement is fine; ✗ It depends of. is not.

The state of needing something or someone in order to function.

  1. The plan is meant to reduce our dependence on imported fertilizer.
  2. Her whole argument rests on the town's reliance on a single employer.
  3. Dependence on borrowing to cover payroll is what sank the company.
  4. The bank acted in reliance on the appraisal it was given.
  5. There's a documented dependence of crop yield on soil temperature.
  6. Long-term reliance on the medication carries its own risks.
See also

desire for#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈzaɪr fɔr/ · dih-ZYRE for · weak /dəˈzaɪr fɚ/
Русский
желание чего-л., стремление к чему-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
formal to neutral
Complement
for: a noun phrase naming what is wanted (a desire for privacy). The same noun takes a to-infinitive with no preposition: a desire to be left alone. Never of in modern American English (✗ a desire of privacy), and no gerund after for in edited prose (a desire for privacy, not a desire for being private).
Pattern
a desire for [noun phrase] · a desire to [verb] · a desire that [clause with subjunctive] · express / feel a desire for [noun phrase] · out of a desire to [verb]
Contrast
For takes a thing; to takes an act. A desire for change names the object wanted; a desire to change names the action wanted — and mixing them yields ✗ a desire for change the schedule. The word is markedly more formal than wish for or want, which is why out of a desire to help reads well in a report and stiffly in conversation. The verb is likewise formal and takes an object or an infinitive with no preposition: she desired an explanation, she desired to know, ✗ she desired for an explanation.

A wish or longing for something.

  1. There's a widespread desire for some kind of independent review.
  2. His desire for privacy is not the same as having something to hide.
  3. The changes came out of a desire to simplify the paperwork.
  4. She has no desire to relive any of it.
  5. The survey found a strong desire for more frequent bus service.
  6. A desire for approval runs through every page of the memoir.

deviation from#

Pronunciation
/ˌdiviˈeɪʃən frʌm/ · dee-vee-AY-shun frum · weak /ˌdiviˈeɪʃən frəm/
Русский
отклонение от — отступление от нормы, плана или ожидаемого хода
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — engineering, statistics, auditing, and aviation
Complement
from: the norm, plan, standard, or course departed from, as a noun phrase — never a gerund (✗ a deviation from following the plan), never an infinitive, and never a clause. in: where the deviation shows up (a deviation in the readings). The statistical compound takes of: the standard deviation of the sample.
Pattern
a deviation from [the norm / the plan / the standard / course] · a deviation in [a measurement] · the standard deviation of [a data set] · any deviation from [protocol] · two standard deviations above the mean
Contrast
✗ Deviation of the norm is the error — the noun takes from, exactly as the verb does (deviate from the script, never ✗ deviate of). Careful with the one compound that flips it: the standard deviation of the sample takes of, because there deviation is a measured quantity and not a departure. Against departure from, which is the everyday synonym: a deviation implies a rule that was supposed to be followed, a departure need only imply a previous practice.

A departure from a standard, plan, or expected course.

  1. Any deviation from the written protocol has to be logged and initialed.
  2. The audit found three deviations from the approved change process.
  3. A small deviation from course put them twelve miles off by morning.
  4. There's a noticeable deviation in the readings after the sensor was moved.
  5. The standard deviation of the sample is larger than the effect they claim.
  6. His answer was a polite deviation from the truth.
See also
departure from, discrepancy between, disparity between/in, difference between/in, conformity with/to, from

difference between/in#

Pronunciation
/ˈdɪfrəns bɪˈtwin/, /ˈdɪfrəns ɪn/ · DIF-runss bih-TWEEN, DIF-runss in · weak /ˈdɪfrəns bəˈtwin/, /ˈdɪfrəns ən/
Русский
различие между чем-л. / разница в чём-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
between: two or more items joined by and, or a plural (the difference between the two engines, differences between states). in: the respect in which they differ (a difference in tone). Gerunds follow both (the difference between buying and leasing). Never a to-infinitive; a wh-clause follows only the between frame indirectly (the difference between what he said and what he did).
Pattern
the difference between X and Y · a difference in [respect] · make a difference to / in [noun phrase] · tell the difference between X and Y · a difference of [amount / opinion]
Variants
make a difference to and make a difference in — both American; to is commoner with people (it made a difference to her), in with situations (it made a difference in the outcome)
Contrast
Between needs two things. The difference between the two engines and the older model is fine because both sides are named, but ✗ the difference between the engine is incomplete, and ✗ the difference of the two engines is wrong. Do not carry the adjective's preposition over to the noun: the adjective is different from (American standard), and ✗ the difference from the two is an error — though a difference from last year is correct when you are naming a single baseline. On different than: American English accepts it before a clause (different than I expected) and prefers different from before a noun; different to is British. A difference of opinion and a difference of four points are fixed quantity frames.

The way in which two things are not the same.

  1. The difference between the two engines is mostly noise, not power.
  2. There's a real difference in tone between her first book and this one.
  3. Can you tell the difference between margarine and butter in a cookie?
  4. One good sous chef makes all the difference to a small kitchen.
  5. We had a difference of opinion about how to handle it.
  6. The difference between buying and leasing comes down to how long you keep the car.
See also
similarity to/between, comparison with/to, contrast to/with, change in/of, different from, between

difficulty with/in#

Pronunciation
/ˈdɪfɪkəlti wɪθ/, /ˈdɪfɪkəlti ɪn/ · DIF-ih-kul-tee with, DIF-ih-kul-tee in · weak /ˈdɪfɪkəlti wəθ/, /ˈdɪfɪkəlti ən/
Русский
трудность с чем-л., затруднение в чём-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
with: a noun phrase naming the troublesome thing (difficulty with the software). in + gerund, or a bare gerund with the preposition dropped (difficulty in finding a job, difficulty finding a job) — never a to-infinitive (✗ difficulty to find a job). ✗ difficulty with to find is likewise impossible.
Pattern
difficulty with [noun phrase] · difficulty (in) [verb-ing] · have difficulty [verb-ing] · with difficulty · the difficulty of [verb-ing / noun phrase] · run into difficulties
Variants
difficulty in doing and difficulty doing — both standard American English; dropping in is commoner in speech and increasingly in writing
Contrast
The infinitive is the error, and it is everywhere: ✗ she has difficulty to concentrate, ✗ no difficulty to understand him. Write difficulty concentrating, no difficulty understanding him. With takes a thing, in takes an activity, and of takes the activity when difficulty is the head of a definite description (the difficulty of proving intent). ✗ difficulty for is wrong. The same -ing rule governs the near-synonyms trouble and a hard time: trouble sleeping, a hard time sleeping, never ✗ trouble to sleep.

Trouble doing something, or the thing that causes it.

  1. He's having difficulty with the new billing software.
  2. She had no difficulty finding parking at that hour.
  3. The difficulty in proving intent is what makes these cases rare.
  4. Patients report difficulty swallowing in the first week.
  5. The team ran into difficulties with the foundation almost immediately.
  6. He got up the stairs, but with obvious difficulty.
See also
trouble with, problem with, delay in, success in/at, with

disadvantage of#

Pronunciation
/ˌdɪsədˈvæntɪdʒ ʌv/ · dis-ad-VAN-tij uv · weak /ˌdɪsədˈvænɪdʒ əv/ → /ˌdɪsədˈvænɪdʒ ə/
Русский
недостаток, минус чего-л.; также невыгодное положение
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (the disadvantage of living downtown) — never a to-infinitive (✗ the disadvantage to live downtown). In at a disadvantage, the complement is dropped entirely or introduced by to/compared with.
Pattern
the disadvantage of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · at a disadvantage · put [someone] at a disadvantage · to [someone's] disadvantage · the disadvantages for [group]
Variants
the disadvantages to doing it that way — an accepted informal American variant, parallel to the advantages to
Contrast
Mirror image of advantage of/over, but with one frame missing: there is no ✗ disadvantage over. Where an advantage over a rival is idiomatic, the negative version is at a disadvantage compared with a rival or simply at a disadvantage. A disadvantage for the students is not wrong but names who suffers rather than what the drawback is; keep of for the drawback itself. The adjective disadvantaged is a social term (disadvantaged neighborhoods) and takes no preposition.

A drawback, or a position of relative weakness.

  1. The main disadvantage of the older model is that parts are hard to find.
  2. There's one real disadvantage of living this close to the tracks.
  3. Smaller districts are at a serious disadvantage when they bid for grants.
  4. The rule change works to the disadvantage of first-time applicants.
  5. The disadvantage of paying cash is that you have no record of it.
  6. Arriving late put her at a disadvantage for the rest of the interview.
See also
advantage of/over, benefit of/to, problem with, limit to/on, of

disagreement with/about#

Pronunciation
/ˌdɪsəˈɡrimənt wɪθ/, /ˌdɪsəˈɡrimənt əˈbaʊt/ · dis-uh-GREE-munt with, dis-uh-GREE-munt uh-BOWT · weak /ˌdɪsəˈɡrimənt wəθ/, /ˌdɪsəˈɡrimənt ˈbaʊt/
Русский
разногласие с кем-л. / по поводу чего-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
with: a person or party. about / over / on: the issue — noun phrase, gerund (disagreement about hiring outside counsel), or wh-clause (disagreement about who should sign). The noun also takes a bare that-clause. Never a to-infinitive.
Pattern
a disagreement with [person] · a disagreement about / over / on [issue] · a disagreement between X and Y · in disagreement with [person] · a sharp disagreement as to whether [clause]
Variants
disagreement over — implies more heat · disagreement on — an issue formally at stake · disagreement about — the neutral default
Contrast
Same three-way frame as agreement with/on: with the person, about/over/on the issue, between both sides named from outside. ✗ a disagreement to the proposal is an error — for opposition to a specific proposal use objection to. The verb takes the same set (disagree with her about the timeline) and, unlike its British cousin, ✗ disagree something is never American English. Note the mild-to-strong scale: disagreement is milder than argument with/about, which in turn is milder than dispute.

A difference of opinion, and the people or issues involved.

  1. I had a sharp disagreement with my accountant about the deduction.
  2. The disagreement over the property line has been to court twice.
  3. There's honest disagreement about what the data show.
  4. A disagreement between the two departments delayed the report by a month.
  5. She's in open disagreement with the rest of the committee.
  6. The disagreement about who signs off on refunds is still unresolved.
See also
agreement with/on, argument with/about, objection to, opposition to, with

discrepancy between#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈskrɛpənsi bɪˈtwin/ · dih-SKREP-un-see bih-TWEEN · weak /dɪˈskrɛpənsi bəˈtwin/
Русский
расхождение между — несовпадение данных, которые должны были сойтись
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — accounting, auditing, and reporting
Complement
between: two things joined by and, or a plural (a discrepancy between the two counts, discrepancies between the invoices). in: where the mismatch shows up (a discrepancy in the ledger). Never a gerund, never an infinitive, never a clause.
Pattern
a discrepancy between X and Y · a discrepancy in [a record / an account] · account for / explain / reconcile a discrepancy · a minor / material / unexplained discrepancy
Contrast
Between needs two terms and in
needs one location
✗ a discrepancy between the ledger is incomplete, and ✗ a discrepancy of the two counts is an error. Against difference between/in: a difference is neutral, a discrepancy implies that the two figures were supposed to match and did not — which is why an auditor writes discrepancy and a shopper writes difference. Against disparity between/in: a disparity is a gap that raises a question of fairness, not of arithmetic.

A mismatch between two accounts, figures, or records that ought to agree.

  1. There's a $4,000 discrepancy between the invoice and the purchase order.
  2. Nobody could explain the discrepancy between the two witnesses' timelines.
  3. Auditors flagged three discrepancies in the payroll ledger.
  4. The discrepancy between what he told the bank and what he told the IRS is the case.
  5. A small discrepancy between the counts turned into a two-week recount.
  6. She reconciled every discrepancy before the quarter closed.
See also
difference between/in, disparity between/in, deviation from, distinction between, between

discussion about/of#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈskʌʃən əˈbaʊt/, /dɪˈskʌʃən ʌv/ · dih-SKUSH-un uh-BOWT, dih-SKUSH-un uv · weak /dəˈskʌʃən ˈbaʊt/, /dəˈskʌʃən əv/ → /dəˈskʌʃən ə/
Русский
обсуждение чего-л. — рус. родительный (а глагол discuss идёт без предлога)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (a discussion about closing the branch), or wh-clause (a discussion about what to do next) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a discussion to close the branch) and never a that-clause (✗ a discussion that we should close it).
Pattern
a discussion about / of / on [topic] · a discussion with [person] · a discussion between X and Y · under discussion · have / open / close a discussion · in discussion with [party]
Variants
discussion about — conversational and the American default · discussion of — more formal, and the usual choice when the noun heads a definite description (a thorough discussion of the evidence) · discussion on — a scheduled or published treatment (a panel discussion on water rights)
Contrast
The flagship noun-versus-verb shift, and the source of the single most persistent error in advanced English: the verb discuss takes a direct object and no preposition — we discussed the budget — so ✗ we discussed about the budget is wrong, while the noun cannot attach anything without one — a discussion about the budget, ✗ a discussion the budget. Both halves have to be learned together, because dropping the preposition on the noun and adding it to the verb are two faces of the same confusion. ✗ a discussion for the budget is likewise an error; with names the other party, between names both from outside.

A conversation in which a subject is talked through.

  1. We had a long discussion about whether to close the Elm Street branch.
  2. Chapter six is a careful discussion of the evidence for the early date.
  3. There was a panel discussion on water rights at the county fair.
  4. I had a useful discussion with the inspector about the setback rules.
  5. Three proposals are still under discussion.
  6. The discussion about how to pay for it lasted longer than the vote.
See also
argument with/about, comment on, agreement with/on, discuss something, discuss, about

disparity between/in#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈspɛrəti bɪˈtwin/, /dɪˈspɛrəti ɪn/ · dih-SPAIR-uh-tee bih-TWEEN, dih-SPAIR-uh-tee in · weak /dɪˈspɛrəti bəˈtwin/, /dɪˈspɛrəti ən/
Русский
неравенство между, разрыв в — большой и обычно тревожный разрыв
Type
noun + preposition
Register
formal — policy writing, public health, civil-rights litigation, and journalism about them
Complement
between: two groups or figures joined by and. in: the dimension along which the gap runs (a disparity in pay, disparities in outcomes). among: three or more groups. Never a gerund, never an infinitive, never a clause.
Pattern
a disparity between X and Y · a disparity in [pay / sentencing / access] · disparities among [groups] · a wide / growing / persistent / racial disparity · the disparity between [rich and poor]
Contrast
Between names the parties, in names the measure, and the two stack: a disparity in sentencing between federal and state courts. ✗ a disparity of pay is an error, and ✗ a disparity with is another. Against difference between/in: every disparity is a difference, but the word carries a charge — it says the gap is large and probably unjust, which is why pay disparity and pay difference are not the same claim. Against discrepancy between: a discrepancy is a bookkeeping mismatch, a disparity is a social gap.

A wide and usually troubling gap between groups or measures.

  1. The report documents a persistent disparity between rural and urban response times.
  2. Disparities in maternal mortality have widened in three of the five counties.
  3. There's a real disparity in pay between the two campuses for the same job.
  4. Sentencing disparities among the district's judges drew the appeal.
  5. The disparity between what was budgeted and what was spent is the whole story.
  6. Nothing in the plan addresses the disparity in broadband access.
See also
difference between/in, discrepancy between, distinction between, ratio of ... to, in proportion to, between

distinction between#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈstɪŋkʃən bɪˈtwin/ · dih-STINK-shun bih-TWEEN · weak /dɪˈstɪŋkʃən bəˈtwin/
Русский
различие между — сознательно проводимое; отдельно — почётное отличие
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
between: two things joined by and. of: an honor, in the other sense (the distinction of being the oldest bank in the state), where a gerund is required — of + -ing, never ✗ the distinction to be the oldest. Never a that-clause after between.
Pattern
a distinction between X and Y · draw / make / blur a distinction between X and Y · without distinction · a distinction without a difference · the distinction of [verb-ing] · with distinction (an honor)
Contrast
A difference is a fact; a distinction is drawn. The difference between copper and aluminum wire exists whether or not anyone notices; the distinction between negligence and recklessness is one the law draws on purpose — which is why you can accuse someone of making a distinction without a difference and cannot make the same complaint about a difference. ✗ a distinction of X and Y is an error in this sense. The second noun, the honor, takes of plus a gerund and has nothing to do with the first: she graduated with distinction.

A difference someone deliberately marks between two things — and, separately, an honor.

  1. The opinion turns on a fine distinction between possession and control.
  2. He draws no distinction between a friend and a client, which is a problem.
  3. The distinction between weather and climate is the one every argument ignores.
  4. That's a distinction without a difference and the judge said so.
  5. She has the distinction of being the first woman elected from the district.
  6. The old rules applied to everyone without distinction.
See also
difference between/in, contrast to/with, discrepancy between, comparison with/to, as distinct from, between

doubt about#

Pronunciation
/daʊt əˈbaʊt/ · dowt uh-BOWT · weak /daʊt ˈbaʊt/
Русский
сомнение в чём-л., сомнения насчёт — рус. «в», не «о»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
about / as to: noun phrase, gerund (doubts about signing anything), or wh-clause (doubt about whether it will hold). The noun also takes a bare that-clause, almost always under a negative (no doubt that he meant it). Never a to-infinitive (✗ doubt to sign).
Pattern
doubt about / as to [noun phrase / verb-ing / wh-clause] · no doubt that [clause] · in doubt · beyond a reasonable doubt · cast doubt on [noun phrase] · have one's doubts about [noun phrase]
Variants
doubt about — the everyday form · doubt as to — formal and legal · doubt over — journalistic (doubts over the funding) · doubt of — archaic outside the fixed no doubt of it
Contrast
Three frames that look alike and are not. Doubts about the plan is uncertainty; cast doubt on the plan is the fixed collocation for undermining it — ✗ cast doubt about is wrong; and no doubt that he meant it takes a clause with no preposition — ✗ no doubt about that he meant it. The verb behaves differently again: I doubt it, I doubt that he'll come, I doubt whether it will hold — all with no preposition, so ✗ I doubt about his story is an error where I have doubts about his story is correct.

Uncertainty about whether something is true or will happen.

  1. I have serious doubts about the contractor's estimate.
  2. There's some doubt about whether the roof will last another winter.
  3. The new testimony casts doubt on the timeline.
  4. There's no doubt that she knew before the meeting.
  5. The outcome was in doubt until the last precinct reported.
  6. Her doubts about signing anything without a lawyer were well founded.
See also
confidence in, belief in, concern about/for, worry about, about

duty to/of#

Pronunciation
/ˈduti tu/, /ˈduti ʌv/ · DOO-tee too, DOO-tee uv · weak /ˈduti tə/, /ˈduti əv/ → /ˈduti ə/ · American /ˈduti/ or /ˈdjuti/; the flapped /d/ makes duty and doody homophones for most speakers
Русский
обязанность перед кем-л., долг; отдельно — пошлина на ввозимый товар
Type
noun + preposition
Register
sense 1 neutral to formal, and a term of art in tort and corporate law; sense 2 is trade and customs usage
Complement
to: either a person or body owed the duty (a duty to his clients) or a bare infinitive naming the act required (a duty to disclose) — the two to's are different words, and both are standard. Never a gerund after to (✗ a duty to disclosing). of: the kind of duty, as a noun (a duty of care, a duty of loyalty), or the person who bears it (the duty of every officer). on: what a customs duty falls on.
Pattern
a duty to [person] · a duty to [verb] · a duty of [care / loyalty / candor] · the duty of [a person] to [verb] · on / off duty · do one's duty · a duty on [imported goods] · duty-free
Contrast
Put a plain noun in the to slot to see which word it is. A duty to the shareholders takes a noun; a duty to disclose takes a bare verb; ✗ a duty to disclosing is impossible either way. Against obligation to/under: the two behave alike, but obligations come from agreements and duties from roles — a director has a duty of loyalty and an obligation under the credit agreement. And keep the second noun clear of the first: the duty on imported steel is a tax and belongs with tariff on/duty on/levy on, not with anything you owe your employer.

An obligation attached to a role or a relationship — and, separately, a tax on imported goods.

1. What a person or office is bound to do. The to frame names either the party owed or the act required; the of frame names the kind of duty. In American law the recurring pairs are duty of care, duty of loyalty, duty to warn, and duty to mitigate.

  1. A landlord has a duty to warn tenants about a known hazard.
  2. Directors owe a duty of loyalty to the corporation, not to the majority holder.
  3. He was off duty and two blocks away when the call came in.
  4. Jury duty pays fourteen dollars a day in this county.

2. A government charge on imported goods. This noun takes on, is countable, and sits beside tariff and levy. The compound duty-free is fixed.

  1. The duty on imported olive oil went up in the spring.
  2. She bought the perfume duty-free in Detroit on the way back.
See also
obligation to/under, responsibility for, liability for/to, onus on, right to/of/over, tariff on/duty on/levy on

E#

easement over/across#

Pronunciation
/ˈizmənt ˈoʊvɚ/, /ˈizmənt əˈkrɔs/ · EEZ-munt OH-ver, EEZ-munt uh-KRAWSS · weak /ˈizmənt əˈkrɔs/; over has no weak form
Русский
юр. сервитут — право ограниченного пользования чужим участком
Type
noun + preposition
Register
legal — deeds, title reports, surveys, and zoning disputes. An American meets the word exactly once, at a real-estate closing, when the title company lists the utility easement running along the back of the lot.
Complement
over / across / on: the burdened land, as a noun phrase. for: the purpose (an easement for ingress and egress, an easement for a sewer line), where a gerund is possible (an easement for maintaining the line). Never a to-infinitive (✗ an easement to cross the field, as a complement of the noun) and never a clause.
Pattern
an easement over / across [land] · an easement for [a purpose] · grant / reserve / extinguish an easement · an easement appurtenant / in gross · a utility / drainage / access easement · the dominant and servient estates
Variants
easement over, easement across, and easement on are all used of the same right; over is the drafter's default, across is picked when the strip physically crosses the parcel, and on is commoner in surveys
Contrast
The land is the object, not the beneficiary: an easement over the Harmons' pasture burdens the Harmons and benefits somebody else, so ✗ an easement over the power company is nonsense. Note the near neighbors: a right of way is the commonest kind of easement, a license is revocable where an easement is not, and a covenant restricts what you may do rather than letting someone else in. ✗ an easement of the pasture is an error.

A legal right to use a strip of someone else's land for a limited purpose.

  1. The deed reserves a ten-foot easement over the north edge of the lot.
  2. There's a utility easement across the back of every parcel on this street.
  3. They bought the cabin without noticing the logging easement over the access road.
  4. The county holds a drainage easement across both fields.
  5. An easement for ingress and egress runs with the land and binds the next owner.
  6. Her neighbor sued to extinguish the easement over the driveway.
See also
title to, right to/of/over, lien on/against, jurisdiction over, access to, over

effect on#

Pronunciation
/ɪˈfɛkt ɑn/ · ih-FEKT on · weak /əˈfɛkt ən/
Русский
воздействие на, влияние на — рус. «на», не «в»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund naming what is affected (an effect on morale, an effect on recruiting new staff) — never a to-infinitive (✗ an effect to lower morale) and never a that-clause (✗ an effect that morale dropped). A wh-clause follows on freely (an effect on how the crew is scheduled). The full frame names both sides: the effect of X on Y.
Pattern
an effect on [thing affected] · the effect of X on Y · have / produce an effect on [noun phrase] · in effect · take effect · side effects of [drug]
Variants
an effect upon — formal, identical in meaning · an impact on — the journalistic near-synonym; see impact on
Contrast
Of names the cause, on names the target: the effect of the drug on blood pressure. Reversing them is nonsense. ✗ an effect to morale and ✗ an effect for morale are errors.
The homophone problem is the real one here
effect is the noun (the effect on sales) and affect is the verb (it affected sales), so ✗ the affect on sales and ✗ it effected sales are the two directions of the same mistake — with the rare exception of the verb effect meaning "bring about" (effect a change) and the psychological noun affect meaning observable emotion (flat affect). And note that affect the verb takes no preposition: ✗ it affects on sales.

The change produced in something by something else.

  1. The tariff had almost no effect on retail prices.
  2. Nobody studied the effect of the closure on the surrounding businesses.
  3. Two cups of coffee have a noticeable effect on my sleep.
  4. The rule takes effect on the first of the month.
  5. Side effects of the medication include dizziness and dry mouth.
  6. Her testimony had a visible effect on the jury.
See also
impact on, influence on/over, result of, consequence of, on

eligibility for#

Pronunciation
/ˌɛlədʒəˈbɪləti fɔr/ · el-uh-juh-BIL-uh-tee for · weak /ˌɛlədʒəˈbɪləti fɚ/
Русский
право на что-л., соответствие условиям для получения или участия
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — benefits administration, college admissions, insurance, athletics
Complement
for: a noun phrase or gerund (eligibility for enrolling mid-year). to: a bare infinitive (eligibility to vote, eligibility to receive benefits). Both are standard, and the two to's test cleanly: after for a noun fits, after to only a verb does. Never a that-clause.
Pattern
eligibility for [benefits / a program / a scholarship] · eligibility to [verb] · determine / verify / establish / lose eligibility · eligibility requirements (compound) · eligible for / eligible to (the adjective, same split)
Contrast
For takes the thing, to
takes the act
eligibility for parole and eligibility to be paroled say the same thing with different grammar, and ✗ eligibility for be paroled and ✗ eligibility to parole are both errors. The adjective behaves identically — eligible for the draft, eligible to enlist — and ✗ eligible on is never right. In American college sports eligibility stands alone as a mass noun: he has one year of eligibility left.

Qualification to receive something or to take part.

  1. Her eligibility for the subsidy ended when the second job started.
  2. The clerk verifies eligibility to vote against the county roll.
  3. A felony conviction does not affect eligibility for federal student aid the way it used to.
  4. He has one year of eligibility left and a bad knee.
  5. Eligibility for enrolling mid-year depends on why the family moved.
  6. The form asks nine questions and settles eligibility for none of them.
See also
entitlement to, criterion for, threshold for, exemption from, qualify for, for

embargo on#

Pronunciation
/ɛmˈbɑrɡoʊ ɑn/ · em-BAR-goh on · weak /ɛmˈbɑrɡoʊ ən/
Русский
эмбарго на — запрет торговли либо запрет публиковать раньше срока
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — trade policy, diplomacy, and journalism, where it also names an agreed hold on publishing a story
Complement
a noun phrase or gerund (an embargo on selling spare parts) — never a to-infinitive (✗ an embargo to sell spare parts) and never a clause.
Pattern
an embargo on [goods / trade / news] · an embargo against [a country] · impose / lift / defy / break an embargo · under embargo · an arms / oil / news embargo
Variants
an embargo on names what is blocked and an embargo against names whom it is aimed at; on is far commoner in American writing, and both appear in the same paragraph without objection. The plural adds -es: embargoes.
Contrast
Against sanction against/sanctions on: an embargo is a total ban on trade in something, sanctions are a package of measures that may include one. Against moratorium on: a moratorium is a pause, an embargo is a prohibition with a political point. ✗ an embargo of oil is an error for the ban, though an embargo of 1973 dates it. Note the newsroom sense, which surprises people: a press release under embargo until Tuesday may be read now and printed then.

An official ban on trade in something, or on publishing something before a set time.

  1. The embargo on Cuban goods outlasted every president who inherited it.
  2. Congress imposed an embargo on arms sales to the region in March.
  3. The study is under embargo until nine Eastern on Thursday.
  4. An embargo on exporting the ore would close two mines in a week.
  5. Three outlets broke the embargo and published early.
  6. The 1973 oil embargo is why the speed limit was fifty-five.
See also
sanction against/sanctions on, moratorium on, prohibition on/against, tariff on/duty on/levy on, limit to/on, on

emphasis on/stress on#

Pronunciation
/ˈɛmfəsɪs ɑn/, /strɛs ɑn/ · EM-fuh-sis on, stress on · weak /ˈɛmfəsɪs ən/, /strɛs ən/
Русский
акцент на чём-л., упор на — особая важность, придаваемая чему-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (emphasis on getting the paperwork right) — never a to-infinitive (✗ emphasis on get it right, ✗ emphasis to improve safety) and never a clause (✗ emphasis that safety comes first; write emphasis on the fact that...).
Pattern
emphasis / stress on [noun phrase / verb-ing] · place / put / lay emphasis on [noun phrase] · with (an) emphasis on [noun phrase] · the emphasis of [work / program]
Variants
emphasis on — the standard noun frame · stress on — identical in meaning, plainer, and also the phonetic term (the stress on the second syllable)
Contrast
Both nouns take on; neither related verb takes anything. Emphasize and stress are bare transitive verbs — she emphasized the deadline, he stressed the risk — so ✗ she emphasized on the deadline and ✗ he stressed on the risk are errors, and they are among the most persistent in academic writing by non-native speakers. The noun cannot go without the preposition: ✗ emphasis the deadline. Note the unrelated stressed about (anxious) and stressed out, which have nothing to do with this pattern.

Special importance or attention given to something.

  1. The program puts heavy emphasis on early literacy.
  2. There's new emphasis on documenting every call.
  3. Her training placed unusual stress on footwork.
  4. The syllabus is history with an emphasis on primary sources.
  5. The emphasis on test scores has crowded out everything else.
  6. Note the stress on the second syllable: com-PA-ra-ble is a variant.
See also
attention to, focus on, importance of/to, emphasize something, emphasize, on

encounter with#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈkaʊntɚ wɪθ/ · in-KOWN-ter with · weak /ənˈkaʊntɚ wəθ/
Русский
встреча, столкновение с — обычно краткая, случайная или неприятная
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun naming a person, animal, or force met (an encounter with a bear). No gerund, no infinitive, no clause — ✗ an encounter with being arrested; write an encounter with the police.
Pattern
an encounter with [person / animal / force] · a chance / brief / violent encounter with [noun phrase] · have an encounter with [noun phrase] · a close encounter
Contrast
With is the only preposition — ✗ an encounter to, ✗ an encounter of, ✗ an encounter against. The verb, by contrast, takes a direct object and no preposition at all: we encountered heavy traffic, never ✗ we encountered with heavy traffic. This is a textbook noun-versus-verb shift and worth pairing in memory with meeting with and contact with, both of which behave the same way. Note that the verb also covers abstract obstacles (encountered resistance) where the noun usually does not.

A meeting, usually brief, unplanned, or unwelcome.

  1. Her first encounter with the county assessor did not go well.
  2. We had a brief encounter with a black bear on the switchbacks.
  3. The report documents six encounters with armed patrols.
  4. A chance encounter with an old professor turned into a job.
  5. His only encounter with the criminal justice system was a parking ticket in 1988.
  6. The film opens with an encounter with a stranger on a train.
See also
interview with, relationship with/between, contact someone, argument with/about, with

entitlement to#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈtaɪtəlmənt tu/ · in-TY-tul-munt too · weak /ɪnˈtaɪtəlmənt tə/
Русский
право на что-л. — юридически обеспеченное притязание на получение
Type
noun + preposition
Register
formal — benefits law, employment contracts, pensions; in American politics entitlements is also the standing name for Social Security, Medicare, and Medicaid, and the bare noun is an insult
Complement
to + noun phrase (an entitlement to severance) or to + bare infinitive (an entitlement to receive severance) — both occur in legal writing, and the two to's are again different words. Never a gerund (✗ an entitlement to receiving severance is not the edited form) and never a clause.
Pattern
an entitlement to [a benefit / a hearing / notice] · an entitlement to [verb] · establish / deny / forfeit an entitlement · a statutory entitlement · entitlement programs (American policy usage) · a sense of entitlement
Contrast
Against eligibility for, which is the distinction that decides cases: eligibility means you may apply and might qualify; an entitlement means the thing is already yours and can be enforced. A benefit you are eligible for can be denied on the merits; a benefit you have an entitlement to can only be taken away with process. ✗ entitlement of and ✗ entitlement for are errors. The adjective is entitled to with the same two complements (entitled to a refund, entitled to know), and entitled alone, as a description of a person, is purely pejorative in American English.

An enforceable right to receive something.

  1. Nothing in the handbook creates an entitlement to severance.
  2. The statute gives every applicant an entitlement to a written decision.
  3. Her entitlement to the survivor benefit vested after ten years.
  4. He has an entitlement to be heard before the license is pulled.
  5. Half the federal budget goes to entitlement programs and the argument never ends.
  6. The kid's sense of entitlement did not come from nowhere.
See also
eligibility for, right to/of/over, claim against/for/to, beneficiary of/under, obligation to/under, to

escape from#

Pronunciation
/ɪˈskeɪp frʌm/ · ih-SKAYP frum · weak /əˈskeɪp frəm/
Русский
побег из, бегство от — из заточения, от опасности или от рутины
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
from: a place, condition, or pursuer (an escape from the county jail, an escape from routine). A gerund is possible (no escape from having to decide). to: a destination or refuge. Never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
an escape from [place / condition] · an escape to [refuge] · a narrow escape · make one's escape · there is no escape from [noun phrase] · a fire escape
Contrast
From for what you got out of; to for where you went. ✗ an escape of the jail is an error; an escape of gas is a different noun entirely, meaning a leak. The verb takes both a prepositional and a bare-object frame with a real difference in meaning: escape from prison is getting out of the building, while escape prison means avoiding a sentence, and escape someone's notice, escape injury, escape punishment are all bare and cannot take from. So ✗ the detail escaped from my notice is wrong: it escaped my notice.

A getting away from confinement, danger, or routine.

  1. The escape from the county jail was captured on four cameras.
  2. Gardening is her escape from a job she can't talk about.
  3. There's no escape from the paperwork, only from doing it yourself.
  4. They had a narrow escape on the icy stretch outside Laramie.
  5. The book is an escape to a version of Maine that never quite existed.
  6. An escape of natural gas forced the block to be evacuated.
See also
departure from, protection from/against, limit to/on, danger of/to, escape from, from

evidence of/for#

Pronunciation
/ˈɛvɪdəns ʌv/, /ˈɛvɪdəns fɔr/ · EV-ih-dunss uv, EV-ih-dunss for · weak /ˈɛvɪdəns əv/ → /ˈɛvɪdəns ə/; /ˈɛvɪdəns fɚ/
Русский
свидетельство чего-л., доказательства в пользу — в англ. неисчисляемо
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: noun phrase or gerund (evidence of tampering, evidence of having been moved). for: a claim, theory, or proposition supported. The noun also takes a bare that-clause: evidence that the seal was broken. Never a to-infinitive (✗ evidence to prove is a purpose clause, a different structure).
Pattern
evidence of [thing shown to exist] · evidence for [claim / theory] · evidence that [clause] · evidence against [person / theory] · in evidence · give / present / suppress evidence
Contrast
Of is a trace; for is support for an argument. Evidence of tampering means signs that tampering happened; evidence for the early date means material that argues for a conclusion; evidence against him is material that incriminates.
The countability rule is the trap
evidence is uncountable in American English, so ✗ an evidence, ✗ evidences, ✗ many evidences. Say a piece of evidence, two pieces of evidence, some evidence, much evidence. Compare proof of, which is also uncountable in this sense but countable in mathematics (two proofs of the theorem).

Facts or signs that support a conclusion.

  1. There's clear evidence of water intrusion behind the drywall.
  2. The evidence for an earlier eruption is mostly in the ash layers.
  3. No evidence that the driver had been drinking was ever produced.
  4. The strongest evidence against him was his own text messages.
  5. We found no evidence of forced entry.
  6. That's one piece of evidence, not a case.
See also
proof of, example of, reason for, grounds for, of

example of#

Pronunciation
/ɪɡˈzæmpəl ʌv/ · ig-ZAM-pul uv · weak /əɡˈzæmpəl əv/ → /əɡˈzæmpəl ə/
Русский
пример чего-л. — рус. родительный, не «для»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (an example of overreaching) — never a to-infinitive (✗ an example to overreach) and never a that-clause. In set an example, the complement is a person introduced by for or to.
Pattern
an example of [category / behavior] · for example · set an example for / to [person] · make an example of [person] · give / cite an example of [noun phrase] · by way of example
Contrast
Of for the category, for/to for the audience, and the two must not be swapped. An example of good design is an instance; an example for the younger staff is a model to imitate. ✗ an example about is an error, and ✗ for example of is a doubling. Note make an example of someone, which means to punish them publicly — the opposite of set an example for someone, though the words are nearly identical. The Latin abbreviation e.g. means for example and takes a comma after it in American style; i.e. means that is and is not interchangeable.

An instance that illustrates a general point.

  1. This is a textbook example of how not to run a hearing.
  2. Can you give me an example of what you mean?
  3. The clock tower is a good example of Richardsonian Romanesque.
  4. She set an example for every apprentice who came after her.
  5. Some cities — Portland, for example — never adopted the rule.
  6. It was an example of overreaching that the court declined to endorse.
See also
evidence of/for, proof of, reference to, exception to, of

exception to#

Pronunciation
/ɪkˈsɛpʃən tu/ · ik-SEP-shun too · weak /əkˈsɛpʃən tə/
Русский
исключение из — рус. «из», не «к» (исключение из правила)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase naming a rule, generalization, or pattern (an exception to the rule). A gerund is possible in the take exception to frame (took exception to being interrupted) — and note that this proves the to is a preposition, so never a bare infinitive (✗ took exception to be interrupted).
Pattern
an exception to [rule] · with the exception of [noun phrase] · make an exception for [person] · take exception to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · without exception · the exception that proves the rule
Contrast
Three prepositions doing three jobs. An exception to the rule is a case the rule does not cover; with the exception of Tuesday means "except Tuesday" and is a fixed phrase, so ✗ with the exception to Tuesday is wrong; and make an exception for Maria means bending the rule in her favor, so ✗ make an exception to Maria misfires. The idiom take exception to means to object, not to exempt — she took exception to his tone is a complaint. ✗ an exception of the rule is an error.

A case that a rule or generalization does not cover.

  1. Every rule in this handbook has at least one exception to it.
  2. With the exception of two holdouts, the block voted the same way.
  3. The zoning board made an exception for the corner lot.
  4. She took exception to being told to smile more.
  5. The policy applies to all employees without exception.
  6. Weekends are the one exception to the parking ban.
See also
rule against/for, limit to/on, objection to, alternative to, to

excuse for#

Pronunciation
/ɪkˈskjus fɔr/ · ik-SKYOOSS for · weak /əkˈskjus fɚ/
Русский
оправдание за что-л.; также предлог для того, чтобы сделать что-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for: noun phrase or gerund naming what is being excused (an excuse for being late) — never a bare infinitive after for (✗ an excuse for be late). The same noun takes a to-infinitive with no preposition, meaning a pretext: an excuse to leave early. Both are correct and mean different things.
Pattern
an excuse for [noun phrase / verb-ing] · an excuse to [verb] · make excuses for [person / verb-ing] · there's no excuse for [verb-ing] · a poor excuse for a [noun]
Contrast
For + -ing looks backward; to + infinitive looks forward. An excuse for leaving early justifies a departure that happened; an excuse to leave early is a pretext for one you want. ✗ an excuse of being late and ✗ an excuse about being late are errors. Note the sarcastic frame a poor excuse for a lawyer, where for means "in the role of." The noun is /ɪkˈskjus/ with an s sound; the verb excuse is /ɪkˈskjuz/ with a z, and it takes a person as its object with for on the offense: excuse me for interrupting, ✗ excuse for me.

A reason offered to justify a fault, or a pretext for doing something.

  1. There's no excuse for leaving the gate open in bear country.
  2. He gave the same excuse for missing three meetings in a row.
  3. The rain was just an excuse to stay in and read.
  4. Stop making excuses for him.
  5. What's your excuse for not registering by the deadline?
  6. That radio is a poor excuse for a public address system.
See also
apology for, reason for, grounds for, responsibility for, for

exemption from#

Pronunciation
/ɪɡˈzɛmpʃən frʌm/ · ig-ZEMP-shun frum · weak /ɪɡˈzɛmpʃən frəm/
Русский
освобождение от — от обязанности, налога или требования
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — tax, regulatory, and school paperwork; an American meets it on a W-4, a property-tax form, or a vaccination waiver
Complement
from: the requirement escaped, as a noun phrase or gerund (an exemption from filing quarterly) — never a to-infinitive (✗ an exemption to file quarterly) and never a clause. for: who or what gets the break (an exemption for veterans). of: whoever is exempted, in formal drafting (the exemption of nonprofits from the tax).
Pattern
an exemption from [a tax / a rule / a requirement] · an exemption for [a group / a category] · claim / grant / seek / lose an exemption · a personal / homestead / religious / medical exemption · exempt from [noun phrase] (the adjective)
Contrast
From is what you escape, for is who escapes it. An exemption from the noise ordinance is the relief; an exemption for houses of worship is the class that gets it; ✗ an exemption of the noise ordinance reverses the sense. ✗ exemption to is an error, and it is common because exception to does take to — but an exception is a case the rule does not cover and an exemption is a release granted to somebody, so the two nouns are not synonyms and do not share a preposition. The adjective is exempt from, never ✗ exempt of.

Official release from a duty, tax, or requirement that otherwise applies.

  1. The church claimed an exemption from the county's assembly permit.
  2. Small carriers got a two-year exemption from the new logging rule.
  3. She filed for a medical exemption from the requirement.
  4. There's a homestead exemption for owner-occupied property in this state.
  5. No exemption from filing quarterly is available once revenue crosses the line.
  6. Losing the exemption cost the foundation about $80,000 a year.
See also
exception to, immunity from, waiver of, eligibility for, compliance with, from

exhibit to/schedule to/annex to#

Pronunciation
/ɪɡˈzɪbɪt tu/, /ˈskɛdʒul tu/, /ˈænɛks tu/ · ig-ZIB-it too, SKED-jool too, AN-eks too · weak /ɪɡˈzɪbɪt tə/, /ˈskɛdʒul tə/, /ˈænɛks tə/ · the noun is AN-eks, the verb uh-NEKS /əˈnɛks/
Русский
юр. приложение к договору — документ, ставший его частью
Type
noun + preposition
Register
legal — contract drafting, SEC filings, treaties. An American meets these on the signature-page side of a purchase agreement, a commercial lease, or an employment contract with a schedule of stock options.
Complement
a noun phrase naming the instrument the document is attached to — never a gerund, never an infinitive, never a clause. In court, exhibit takes no preposition at all: Exhibit 14, plaintiff's Exhibit A.
Pattern
Exhibit A to [the Agreement] · Schedule 2 to [the Purchase Agreement] · the Annex to [the Treaty] · attached as Exhibit B · set forth on Schedule 3.1 · incorporated by reference
Variants
the three are drafting conventions, not synonyms with fixed meanings, and firms differ: an exhibit is usually a stand-alone document (a form of note, a bill of sale); a schedule is usually a list keyed to a representation (the Disclosure Schedules); an annex is commoner in treaties and in agreements drafted outside the United States. An appendix belongs to reports and books — see appendix to — and an addendum adds new terms.
Contrast
All three take to and none takes of: ✗ Exhibit A of the Agreement is a drafting error a partner will circle, though it is everywhere in email. Note the case convention: capitalized and unhyphenated when they name a specific attachment (as set forth in Schedule 4.2), lowercase when generic (the schedules to the agreement). In litigation the word switches meaning entirely — Exhibit 6 is a thing handed to a witness, and it hangs off nothing.

A document attached to and made part of an agreement.

  1. The form of promissory note is attached as Exhibit B to the loan agreement.
  2. Every open lawsuit is listed on the disclosure Schedule to the merger agreement.
  3. The Annex to the treaty runs longer than the treaty.
  4. Counsel added a schedule to the lease listing the fixtures that stay.
  5. Nothing in the exhibits to the agreement changes the closing date.
  6. She marked the letter as plaintiff's Exhibit 14 and handed it to the witness.
See also
appendix to, amendment to, party to, signatory to, warranty as to, to

experience of/in/with#

Pronunciation
/ɪkˈspɪriəns ʌv/ · ik-SPEER-ee-unss uv · weak /əkˈspɪriəns əv/ → /ə/; /ən/; /wəθ/
Русский
опыт в чём-л., опыт работы с чем-л.; также пережитое
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
in: a field or activity — noun phrase or gerund (experience in managing volunteers) — never a to-infinitive (✗ experience to manage volunteers). with: a tool, system, or population dealt with. of: something undergone. The noun also takes a bare to-infinitive in the frame the experience to handle it, meaning enough experience.
Pattern
experience in [field / verb-ing] · experience with [tool / people] · the experience of [thing undergone] · the experience to [verb] · have / gain / lack experience · from experience
Variants
experience in — a field or discipline · experience with — hands-on dealings, the American preference on résumés · experience of — undergoing something, commoner in British English and in academic prose about lived experience
Contrast
Three prepositions, three relationships to the thing. Experience in nursing is a career; experience with ventilators is having handled them; the experience of being a patient is having lived through it. American job postings overwhelmingly want experience with a technology and experience in a field, so ✗ experience of Excel reads as British or as a translation.
Countability matters
experience is uncountable when it means accumulated know-how (she has ten years' experience, ✗ ten years' experiences) and countable when it means an episode (a strange experience, two bad experiences).

Accumulated know-how, hands-on dealings, or something lived through.

  1. She has eight years' experience in pediatric nursing.
  2. Do you have any experience with payroll software?
  3. The whole experience of moving cross-country aged me a decade.
  4. He lacks the experience to run a crew that size.
  5. I know from experience that the second week is the hard one.
  6. Two bad experiences with the same contractor were enough.
See also
knowledge of/about, expert on/in, understanding of, success in/at, with

expert on/in#

Pronunciation
/ˈɛkspɜrt ɑn/, /ˈɛkspɜrt ɪn/ · EK-spurt on, EK-spurt in · weak /ˈɛkspɜrt ən/, /ˈɛkspɜrt ən/
Русский
эксперт по чему-л., специалист в чём-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
on: a subject known about — noun phrase only (✗ an expert on how engines fail; write an expert on engine failures). in: a field practiced. at: a skill, and here a gerund is normal (an expert at getting funding) — never a to-infinitive (✗ an expert at get funding).
Pattern
an expert on [subject] · an expert in [field] · an expert at [verb-ing / skill] · expert testimony on [subject] · consult an expert on [noun phrase]
Variants
expert on — knows about it · expert in — works in it · expert at — is good at doing it; all three are standard American English and the choice is real, not stylistic
Contrast
Choose by what kind of thing follows. An expert on wolves studies them; an expert in wildlife biology practices the discipline; an expert at tracking wolves is skilled at the activity. ✗ an expert of wolves is an error in all three senses. The parallel adjective expert takes the same at and in (expert at splicing rope, expert in tax law) but not on: ✗ she is expert on wolves wants the noun, she is an expert on wolves. Compare authority on/over, which shares the on frame but adds a power sense that expert does not have.

Someone with deep knowledge of a subject or skill.

  1. She's the state's leading expert on groundwater contamination.
  2. We brought in an expert in forensic accounting.
  3. He's an expert at getting extensions out of the county.
  4. The defense called two experts on eyewitness reliability.
  5. You don't have to be an expert in nutrition to read a label.
  6. My uncle is an expert at losing car keys.
See also
authority on/over, knowledge of/about, experience of/in/with, grasp of, expert at, on

exposure to#

Pronunciation
/ɪkˈspoʊʒɚ tu/ · ik-SPOH-zher too · weak /ɪkˈspoʊʒɚ tə/
Русский
подверженность чему-л., контакт с чем-л.; также разоблачение скрытого
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral; technical in medicine, industrial hygiene, and finance
Complement
a noun phrase or gerund (exposure to being outdoors all day) — never a to-infinitive (✗ exposure to breathe the fumes) and never a clause. The revealing sense takes of instead: the exposure of the scheme.
Pattern
exposure to [a substance / a risk / a language / the elements] · the exposure of [a secret / a fraud] · limit / reduce / document exposure to [noun phrase] · occupational exposure · exposure to [a currency / a sector] (finance) · die of exposure
Contrast
To for what reaches you, of for what gets revealed. Her exposure to asbestos is contact; the exposure of the cover-up is disclosure — and ✗ the exposure to the cover-up would mean the cover-up was reaching her. ✗ exposure with and ✗ exposure on are errors. Note the American senses that take no complement: die of exposure is hypothermia, an exposure in photography is one frame, and good exposure for a business is publicity. In finance, exposure to the euro is how much you stand to lose if it moves, which is the risk sense, not the contact sense.

Contact with something that can affect you — a substance, a risk, an idea — or the act of revealing something hidden.

  1. Years of exposure to solvents in the shop finally showed up on a scan.
  2. Early exposure to a second language does more than later drilling does.
  3. The fund cut its exposure to commercial real estate by half.
  4. Two hikers died of exposure on the ridge that November.
  5. The paper's exposure of the billing scheme took eighteen months.
  6. OSHA limits exposure to silica dust to fifty micrograms per cubic meter.
See also
risk of, danger of/to, protection from/against, immunity from, vulnerable to, to

faith in#

Pronunciation
/feɪθ ɪn/ · fayth in · weak /feɪθ ən/
Русский
вера в кого-л./что-л. — полное доверие, не требующее доказательств
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (faith in doing things the slow way) — never a to-infinitive (✗ faith to do things the slow way). The noun does take a bare that-clause: faith that it would work out.
Pattern
faith in [person / thing] · faith that [clause] · have / lose / keep faith in [noun phrase] · in good faith · an article of faith · bad faith
Contrast
In is fixed — ✗ faith on, ✗ faith to, ✗ faith about. The distinction from confidence in is one of evidence: confidence implies grounds, faith implies going beyond them, which is why blind confidence is odd and blind faith is idiomatic. Note the fixed legal phrases in good faith and in bad faith, which take no complement at all, and keep faith with someone (honor a commitment to them), where the preposition changes with the sense.

Complete trust in someone or something, held without proof.

  1. I've lost what faith I had in the appeals process.
  2. She kept her faith in the students long after the district gave up.
  3. His faith in the machine survived three breakdowns in one week.
  4. They negotiated in good faith and got nothing for it.
  5. The board's faith that enrollment would rebound was misplaced.
  6. Faith in institutions is measurably lower than it was in 1975.
See also
confidence in, belief in, dependence on/reliance on, hope for/of, in

fear of#

Pronunciation
/fɪr ʌv/ · feer uv · weak /fɪr əv/ → /fɪr ə/
Русский
страх перед чем-л., боязнь чего-л. — рус. родительный или «перед»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: noun phrase or gerund (a fear of flying, fear of losing the house) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a fear to fly). for: a person or thing whose safety is at stake (fear for her safety). The noun also takes a bare that-clause: a fear that the levee would fail.
Pattern
a fear of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · fear for [someone / something at risk] · a fear that [clause] · for fear of [verb-ing] · for fear that [clause] · out of fear of [noun phrase]
Contrast
Of is what frightens you; for is who you are frightened about. Fear of her father means he frightens you; fear for her father means you are worried about him — a total reversal from one preposition. The fixed adverbial for fear of requires an -ing form (he said nothing for fear of making it worse, ✗ for fear to make it worse), while for fear that takes a clause. The verb allows the bare infinitive frame that the noun forbids — I fear to think what it cost is archaic but grammatical, whereas ✗ a fear to think is not.

Being afraid of something, or being afraid on someone's behalf.

  1. Her fear of flying kept her off airplanes for twenty years.
  2. There's a real fear of losing the farm if the drought holds.
  3. The family expressed fear for his safety in the county lockup.
  4. He never reported it, for fear of being blamed.
  5. A fear that the levee would fail emptied the whole subdivision.
  6. Out of fear of the paperwork, she let the claim lapse.
See also
risk of, danger of/to, worry about, concern about/for, afraid of, of

focus on#

Pronunciation
/ˈfoʊkəs ɑn/ · FOH-kus on · weak /ˈfoʊkəs ən/
Русский
сосредоточенность на чём-л., фокус на; главный предмет внимания
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (a focus on reducing waste) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a focus to reduce waste) and never a clause (✗ a focus that we reduce waste).
Pattern
a focus on [noun phrase / verb-ing] · the focus of [study / attention] · place / put the focus on [noun phrase] · in focus · out of focus · shift focus from X to Y
Variants
focus in on — an informal American intensifier of the verb (let's focus in on page three); harmless in speech, redundant in writing
Contrast
On for the target, of for whose focus it is: the focus of the study is what the study concentrates on, and a focus on cost is the thing concentrated on — both can appear together, the focus of the study on cost. ✗ a focus to, ✗ a focus about, ✗ a focus in are errors. Unlike emphasis on/stress on, the verb here keeps the preposition: focus on the numbers, ✗ focus the numbers — except in the optical and transitive sense (focus the lens, focus your attention on it).

Concentrated attention, or the thing it is directed at.

  1. The district's new focus on attendance has already moved the numbers.
  2. The focus of the audit is the 2023 fiscal year.
  3. There's too much focus on the logo and not enough on the product.
  4. The whole slide was out of focus.
  5. Her focus on reducing packaging waste started as a side project.
  6. The committee shifted its focus from prevention to treatment.
