fondness for#

Pronunciation
/ˈfɑndnəs fɔr/ · FOND-nus for · weak /ˈfɑndnəs fɚ/
Русский
любовь, слабость к чему-л. — мягкая, чуть снисходительная симпатия
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral, mildly literary
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (a fondness for taking the long way home) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a fondness to take the long way) and never a clause.
Pattern
a fondness for [noun phrase / verb-ing] · develop / have a fondness for [noun phrase] · look back with fondness on [noun phrase]
Contrast
For is fixed — ✗ fondness of, ✗ fondness to, ✗ fondness toward. Do not carry over the adjective's preposition: the adjective is fond of (fond of jazz, ✗ fond for jazz), and the noun is fondness for, so the pair is a small but reliable trap. Note the gentle irony the word usually carries in American prose: a fondness for expensive shoes is nearly always a mild accusation. Compare taste for/in, which is close in meaning but splits by preposition, and preference for, which is neutral.

A liking for something, usually mild and slightly indulgent.

  1. He has a well-known fondness for diner coffee.
  2. Her fondness for nineteenth-century sermons is hard to explain.
  3. The judge showed no particular fondness for long opening statements.
  4. I've developed a fondness for taking the long way home.
  5. A fondness for the underdog runs in that family.
  6. His fondness for the sound of his own voice was the whole problem.

G#

grasp of#

Pronunciation
/ɡræsp ʌv/ · grasp uv · weak /ɡræsp əv/ → /ɡræsp ə/
Русский
понимание чего-л., владение предметом — книжнее, чем handle on
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or a wh-clause (a firm grasp of what the job involves) — never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause. A gerund is possible but unusual (a grasp of doing the math in your head reads oddly; prefer a grasp of the arithmetic).
Pattern
a grasp of [subject / situation] · have / get / lose one's grasp of [noun phrase] · beyond / within someone's grasp · a firm / shaky / tenuous grasp of [noun phrase]
Variants
a grasp on — heard in American speech (a good grasp on the material), but of is the edited form; a grip on is the idiomatic alternative with on
Contrast
Of for understanding, within/beyond for the physical or figurative reach. A grasp of the material is comprehension; within his grasp is attainability — ✗ a grasp to the material is an error. The near-synonym grip prefers on (a grip on reality, get a grip on the situation), which is where grasp on comes from; both are acceptable in speech, but grasp of is what edited prose uses. The verb takes a direct object with no preposition: he grasped the point, ✗ he grasped at the point (which means something else — a desperate attempt, as in grasping at straws).

An understanding of a subject or situation.

  1. She has a firm grasp of the underlying statistics.
  2. His grasp of what the job actually involves is shaky at best.
  3. You can't teach this without a solid grasp of the chemistry.
  4. A tenure-track job seemed, for one week, within her grasp.
  5. The report shows almost no grasp of how the permitting process works.
  6. The concept is not beyond the grasp of a bright ninth grader.
See also
understanding of, knowledge of/about, insight into, awareness of, of

grounds for#

Pronunciation
/ɡraʊndz fɔr/ · growndz for · weak /ɡraʊndz fɚ/
Русский
основания для — достаточная юридически или логически причина; обычно мн. ч.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (grounds for firing him) — and separately a to-infinitive with no preposition (grounds to believe he lied). Also a that-clause in the fixed frame on the grounds that. ✗ grounds for to fire him mixes the two.
Pattern
grounds for [noun phrase / verb-ing] · grounds to [verb] · on the grounds that [clause] · on the grounds of [noun phrase] · have grounds for [noun phrase] · no grounds whatsoever
Variants
grounds (plural) — the normal form in this sense, even with a singular determiner (a single ground for appeal is possible but stiff) · ground for — occurs in legal writing (ground for reversal)
Two fixed phrases that get blended
on the grounds that the notice was late takes a clause, and on the grounds of procedural error takes a noun — ✗ on the grounds of that the notice was late is the blend. ✗ grounds of appeal is British legal usage; American practice says grounds for appeal. The unrelated singular ground meaning earth, and the plural grounds meaning the land around a building or the sediment in coffee, take no preposition at all.

A legally or logically sufficient reason for doing something.

  1. Repeated absence is grounds for dismissal under the contract.
  2. The tenant has grounds for withholding rent until the heat is fixed.
  3. We have grounds to believe the meter was misread.
  4. The appeal was denied on the grounds that it was filed nine days late.
  5. There are no grounds for reopening the investigation.
  6. The permit was refused on the grounds of inadequate parking.
See also
reason for, evidence of/for, excuse for, cause of, for

guarantee of#

Pronunciation
/ˌɡɛrənˈti ʌv/ · gair-un-TEE uv · weak /ˌɡɛrənˈti əv/ → /ˌɡɛrənˈti ə/
Русский
гарантия чего-л. — твёрдое обещание, что нечто произойдёт или выдержит
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: noun phrase or gerund (no guarantee of getting a refund) — never a to-infinitive after of (✗ a guarantee of to get a refund). The noun separately takes a that-clause (a guarantee that the work will hold) and, in commercial use, against (a guarantee against defects).
Pattern
a guarantee of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · a guarantee that [clause] · a guarantee against [defect / loss] · a guarantee on [product] · under guarantee · no guarantee
Variants
guaranty — the same word in the narrow financial sense of a surety obligation; in ordinary American writing use guarantee for everything
Contrast
Of for the outcome promised, against for the risk covered, on for the product. A guarantee of delivery by Friday, a guarantee against rust, a two-year guarantee on the compressor — swapping these produces ✗ a guarantee for rust, which reads as though rust were promised. ✗ a guarantee to the buyer is wrong in the promise sense, though a guarantee can of course be given to a buyer. The verb takes a direct object, an indirect object, or a that-clause and no preposition: we guarantee the work, I guarantee you it will hold, ✗ we guarantee for the work.

A firm promise that something will happen or hold up.

  1. A high LSAT score is no guarantee of admission anywhere.
  2. The dealer offers a three-year guarantee on the transmission.
  3. There's a written guarantee against corrosion for the first decade.
  4. We got a guarantee that the crew would be out by the fifteenth.
  5. The compressor is still under guarantee.
  6. Paying early is no guarantee of getting your deposit back.
See also
proof of, responsibility for, protection from/against, confidence in, of

H#

habit of#

Pronunciation
/ˈhæbɪt ʌv/ · HAB-it uv · weak /ˈhæbɪt əv/ → /ˈhæbɪt ə/
Русский
привычка делать что-л. — устойчивое повторяющееся поведение
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of + gerund, almost always (the habit of checking twice) — never a to-infinitive (✗ the habit to check twice). A plain noun after of is rare and reads as a compound instead (a coffee habit, not ✗ a habit of coffee).
Pattern
the habit of [verb-ing] · in the habit of [verb-ing] · get into / out of the habit of [verb-ing] · make a habit of [verb-ing] · break the habit · out of habit · a habit of mind
The infinitive is the error and it is nearly universal among learners
✗ he has the habit to interrupt, ✗ I'm in the habit to walk after dinner. Every frame here requires -ing — the habit of interrupting, in the habit of walking. ✗ a habit to has no standing in American English. Compare the adjectives, which do take the infinitive and thereby reinforce the confusion: he is accustomed to walking (preposition, -ing) but he tends to walk (infinitive). Note out of habit (mechanically) against out of the habit of (no longer doing it) — one word apart, opposite meanings.

A settled, repeated behavior.

  1. She has the habit of rereading every email before sending it.
  2. I'm not in the habit of lending tools to people I just met.
  3. He got into the habit of leaving the radio on all night.
  4. Don't make a habit of parking there.
  5. I checked the mailbox out of habit, though nothing comes on Sundays.
  6. Breaking the habit of checking his phone at dinner took a year.
See also
tendency to/toward, taste for/in, fondness for, difficulty with/in, of

handle on#

Pronunciation
/ˈhændəl ɑn/ · HAN-dul on · weak /ˈhændəl ən/
Русский
разг. представление о проблеме — «ухватить суть» настолько, чтобы справиться
Type
noun + preposition
Register
informal American English — spoken, and fine in email and reporting, out of place in a brief or a scientific paper
Complement
a noun phrase or gerund (a handle on managing the backlog) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a handle to manage the backlog) and never a clause. The noun is nearly always inside get or have a handle on; a bare the handle on the problem is not idiomatic in this sense.
Pattern
get / have a handle on [a problem / the numbers / a situation] · no handle on [noun phrase] · a good / firm / rough handle on [noun phrase] · a handle (a nickname or a screen name) · the handle on [a door] (the literal object)
Contrast
On for understanding, of
for the physical grip's owner
a handle on the budget is comprehension, the handle of the pitcher is the part you hold, and the handle on the door is also literal — context, not the preposition, separates the last two. Against grasp of, which is the same idea one register up: a grasp of the material is what you write in a recommendation letter, a handle on the material is what you say out loud. Compare understanding of, the neutral form. In American slang a handle with no complement is a name (what's your handle?) or, in basketball, ball-handling ability.

An informal grip on a problem — enough understanding to manage it.

  1. Nobody has a handle on how much the repairs will actually run.
  2. Give me a week and I'll have a handle on the backlog.
  3. She's got a good handle on the county's permitting mess.
  4. We have no handle on why the returns spiked in March.
  5. He's finally getting a handle on managing three sites at once.
  6. His Twitter handle is the same one he used on CB radio in 1979.
See also
grasp of, understanding of, insight into, knowledge of/about, control over/of, on

hope for/of#

Pronunciation
/hoʊp fɔr/, /hoʊp ʌv/ · hohp for, hohp uv · weak /hoʊp fɚ/, /hoʊp əv/ → /hoʊp ə/
Русский
надежда на что-л. — рус. «на», не «для»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for: a noun phrase naming what is wanted (hope for peace). of + gerund: the outcome hoped for (hope of finding survivors) — never a to-infinitive after either preposition (✗ no hope to survive, ✗ hope of to survive). The noun also takes a bare that-clause: the hope that it would blow over.
Pattern
hope for [noun phrase] · hope of [verb-ing] · the hope that [clause] · in the hope of [verb-ing] · in the hope that [clause] · give up hope of [verb-ing] · hopes for [the future]
Contrast
For takes a thing; of takes an outcome, always in the -ing form. ✗ no hope to find survivors is the classic error; write no hope of finding survivors. The verb behaves in the opposite direction and that is precisely why the noun trips people: hope to find survivors is perfectly good English with an infinitive, as are hope for the best and hope that it holds. So the same idea splits by word class — verb hope to do, noun hope of doing. ✗ hope in survives only in religious registers (hope in the Lord).

A wish for a good outcome, and the expectation that it might come.

  1. There's still hope for a negotiated settlement.
  2. Crews gave up hope of finding anyone alive on the fourth day.
  3. She applied in the hope of getting in-state tuition.
  4. The hope that the market would recover by spring did not survive February.
  5. My hopes for the season died in the first quarter.
  6. He has no realistic hope of making the payments.
See also
desire for, wish for, chance of, chance of, hope for, for

I#

idea of/for#

Pronunciation
/aɪˈdiə ʌv/, /aɪˈdiə fɔr/ · eye-DEE-uh uv, eye-DEE-uh for · weak /aɪˈdiə əv/ → /aɪˈdiə ə/; /aɪˈdiə fɚ/
Русский
of — представление о чём-л.; for — идея, замысел чего-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: a concept — noun phrase or gerund (the idea of moving) — never a to-infinitive after of (✗ the idea of to move). for: a proposal (an idea for a documentary). The noun also takes a bare that-clause (the idea that markets self-correct) and a wh-clause after no idea (no idea what he meant).
Pattern
the idea of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · an idea for [project] · the idea that [clause] · have no idea [wh-clause] · get the idea · ideas about [topic] · the idea behind [thing]
Variants
ideas about — opinions on a topic (ideas about parenting), distinct from both of and for
Contrast
Of is the concept, for is the proposal, and the two are not close. The idea of a second bridge is the notion itself; an idea for a second bridge is somebody's plan. ✗ the idea to move is an error in careful writing, though it is common in speech; write the idea of moving. The clause frame drops the preposition entirely — the idea that markets self-correct, ✗ the idea of that markets self-correct — and no idea takes a wh-clause with nothing in between: I have no idea what he meant, where no idea of what he meant is heavier but not wrong.

A concept, a proposal, or an opinion.

  1. The idea of selling the house had never occurred to either of them.
  2. She had a good idea for a documentary about the last drive-in.
  3. The idea that the deed was lost in the 1911 fire is family legend.
  4. I have no idea what the second key opens.
  5. His ideas about discipline are twenty years out of date.
  6. The whole idea behind the redesign was to cut one page from the form.
See also
plan for, opinion on/of/about, view on/of, insight into, of

immunity from#

Pronunciation
/ɪˈmjunəti frʌm/ · ih-MYOO-nuh-tee frum · weak /ɪˈmjunəti frəm/
Русский
юр. иммунитет от ответственности; мед. невосприимчивость к болезни
Type
noun + preposition
Register
from is legal and formal — prosecutors' letters, diplomatic notes, civil-rights suits; to is medical and everyday
Complement
from: the legal process or consequence escaped, as a noun phrase or gerund (immunity from being sued) — never an infinitive. to: a disease or agent (immunity to measles). against: an agent, in immunology (immunity against the variant). Never a clause after any of them.
Pattern
immunity from [prosecution / suit / liability] · immunity to [a disease] · immunity against [a pathogen] · grant / claim / waive immunity · diplomatic / sovereign / qualified / transactional immunity · herd immunity (no complement)
Contrast
From for the law, to for the body, and swapping them is the error: ✗ immunity to prosecution and ✗ immunity from measles both sound wrong to an American ear, though the second is heard. Immunity against is immunology's own variant and is normal in a virology paper, odd in a newspaper. Against exemption from: an exemption releases you from a requirement, immunity shields you from a consequence. American terms to keep straight: qualified immunity protects officials from suit, sovereign immunity protects governments, transactional immunity protects a witness from prosecution for whatever he testifies about.

Protection from legal consequences, or the body's resistance to a disease.

  1. He testified only after the U.S. Attorney granted him immunity from prosecution.
  2. Most adults have lifelong immunity to chickenpox after one infection.
  3. Qualified immunity shielded both officers from the civil suit.
  4. The statute gives volunteers immunity from liability for ordinary negligence.
  5. Immunity against the newer variant faded within about five months.
  6. Diplomatic immunity covers the parking tickets, which is why there are so many.
See also
exemption from, protection from/against, waiver of, liability for/to, right to/of/over, from

impact on#

Pronunciation
/ˈɪmpækt ɑn/ · IM-pakt on · weak /ˈɪmpækt ən/
Русский
сильное воздействие на, влияние на — рус. «на», не «для»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase naming what is affected (an impact on enrollment). Gerunds are possible but heavy; never a to-infinitive (✗ an impact to reduce costs) and never a clause. The full frame names both sides: the impact of X on Y.
Pattern
an impact on [thing affected] · the impact of X on Y · have / make an impact on [noun phrase] · the impact of [event] · high-impact · on impact
Variants
an impact upon — formal, identical in meaning · an effect on — the plainer synonym; see effect on
Contrast
On is the preposition and to is corporate noise. ✗ the impact to the schedule has spread through American business writing since about 2000; it is not standard, and editors change it to the impact on the schedule. ✗ an impact for is likewise wrong. Of names the cause, on names the target, exactly as with effect on. Note that impact as a verb in the figurative sense (the tariff impacted sales) is disliked by many American editors, who prefer affected; the literal transitive verb (an impacted tooth, the bullet impacted the wall) is unobjectionable.

A strong effect on something.

  1. The closure had an immediate impact on enrollment at both schools.
  2. Nobody has measured the impact of the ordinance on small landlords.
  3. Her testimony made a real impact on the committee.
  4. The new rules will have almost no impact on rural carriers.
  5. The environmental impact of the pipeline is still under review.
  6. The airbag deployed on impact.
See also
effect on, influence on/over, result of, consequence of, on

implication for#

Pronunciation
/ˌɪmpləˈkeɪʃən fɔr/ · im-pluh-KAY-shun for · weak /ˌɪmpləˈkeɪʃən fɚ/
Русский
последствие для кого-л.; скрытый намёк; причастность к чему-л. (in)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
for: who or what is affected, as a noun phrase — never a gerund (✗ implications for raising rates; write implications for the rate decision) and never an infinitive. that: a bare that-clause, in the "unstated suggestion" sense (the implication that he knew). in: involvement in wrongdoing (his implication in the scheme).
Pattern
implications for [a group / a policy / a field] · the implication that [clause] · implication in [wrongdoing] · by implication · far-reaching / troubling / practical implications · the implications of [an event] for [a group]
Contrast
Three frames, three meanings. Implications for small landlords are consequences; the implication that he knew is a hint; his implication in the fraud is involvement. The full consequence frame stacks both prepositions: the implications of the ruling for small landlords — and dropping the of leaves the sentence hanging. Note the pair every editor watches: a writer or a fact implies, a reader infers, so the implication is in the text and the inference is in the reader's head. ✗ implications to the industry is an error.

A consequence for someone, an unstated suggestion, or involvement in something.

  1. The ruling has real implications for every month-to-month tenant in the state.
  2. Nobody has thought through the implications for the pension fund.
  3. The implication that she approved the transfer is not supported by a single email.
  4. His implication in the bid-rigging came out three years later.
  5. By implication, the memo concedes the deadline was missed.
  6. A four-cent change has implications for margins nobody wants to discuss.
See also
consequence of, effect on, impact on, bearing on, relevance to, for

importance of/to#

Pronunciation
/ɪmˈpɔrtəns ʌv/, /ɪmˈpɔrtəns tu/ · im-POR-tunss uv, im-POR-tunss too · weak /əmˈpɔrtəns əv/ → /ə/; /əmˈpɔrtəns tə/
Русский
важность чего-л. для кого-л., значимость
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: what is important — noun phrase or gerund (the importance of showing up) — never a to-infinitive (✗ the importance to show up). to: the person or thing it matters to. Both frequently appear at once: the importance of sleep to recovery.
Pattern
the importance of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · of importance to [person] · attach importance to [noun phrase] · of little / no importance · stress the importance of [verb-ing]
Contrast
Of names what matters; to names whom it matters to. ✗ the importance to sleep is the classic slip, produced by reaching for an infinitive where a gerund belongs. Note the separate frame attach importance to something, where to introduces the thing valued rather than the person — so he attaches great importance to punctuality is correct and does not contradict the rule. The adjective is important to someone and important for something (important for growth), and there ✗ important of is wrong.

The quality of mattering, and to whom.

  1. The report stresses the importance of early screening.
  2. She never grasped the importance of showing up on time to a union meeting.
  3. Water rights are of enormous importance to every rancher in the basin.
  4. The board attaches great importance to the auditor's independence.
  5. The importance of the find was not appreciated until the 1960s.
  6. That distinction is of no importance here.
See also
emphasis on/stress on, attention to, benefit of/to, point in/of, of

improvement in/on#

Pronunciation
/ɪmˈpruvmənt ɪn/, /ɪmˈpruvmənt ɑn/ · im-PROOV-munt in, im-PROOV-munt on · weak /əmˈpruvmənt ən/
Русский
in — улучшение в чём-л.; on — усовершенствование по сравнению с прежним
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
in: what is getting better — noun phrase or gerund (an improvement in reporting) — never a to-infinitive (✗ an improvement to report faster). on / over: the earlier version being beaten. to: a physical alteration made to a property (improvements to the building).
Pattern
an improvement in [thing improving] · an improvement on / over [earlier version] · improvements to [property] · show / see an improvement in [noun phrase] · room for improvement
Variants
an improvement on — the standard comparative form · an improvement over — equally American and slightly commoner in technical writing
Contrast
Three prepositions and three claims. An improvement in air quality says the air is better than it was; an improvement on the 2019 model says this one beats that one; improvements to the building are renovations. ✗ an improvement of air quality is not idiomatic in the change sense, though the improvement of public health is fine as a formal abstract noun. The verb takes a direct object with no preposition (improve the design) or stands alone (she's improving), and improve on the design is not an error but a different claim: it means "do better than it," not "make it better" — a real and easily missed difference.

A change for the better, or a version that beats an earlier one.

  1. There's been a measurable improvement in air quality since the plant closed.
  2. The second edition is a real improvement on the first.
  3. Her handwriting shows some improvement, mostly in the numerals.
  4. The lease requires the tenant to pay for any improvements to the storefront.
  5. This engine is a clear improvement over the one it replaced.
  6. There's still room for improvement in how we handle refunds.
See also
increase in/decrease in, progress in/on, change in/of, advantage of/over, in

incidence of/prevalence of#

Pronunciation
/ˈɪnsədəns ʌv/, /ˈprɛvələns ʌv/ · IN-suh-dunss uv, PREV-uh-lunss uv · weak /ˈɪnsədəns əv/ → /ˈɪnsədəns ə/
Русский
заболеваемость (новые случаи) и распространённость (все случаи разом)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
technical — epidemiology, public health, criminology — and their reporting; both words are singular mass nouns
Complement
of: the condition or event counted, as a noun phrase — never a gerund (✗ the incidence of smoking cigarettes is odd; write the incidence of smoking), never an infinitive, and never a clause. in / among: the population (prevalence among adolescents, incidence in the exposed group).
Pattern
the incidence of [a disease / an event] · the prevalence of [a condition] · incidence in / among [a population] · a high / low / rising incidence · per 100,000 · incidence rate
The technical split is the point
incidence counts new cases over a period, prevalence counts existing cases at a moment. A long-lasting disease has low incidence and high prevalence; a short one, the reverse — so ✗ the incidence of diabetes in the county is nine percent misuses the word, since a percentage of the living population is prevalence. And the spelling trap: incidence is a rate, incidents are events. ✗ the incidents of theft rose means individual thefts rose, which may be what you meant, but the incidence of theft rose is the statistic. Both words are uncountable in this sense: ✗ two incidences.

How often a condition occurs — either newly, over a period, or in total, at a moment.

  1. The incidence of Lyme disease in the county has tripled since 2010.
  2. Prevalence of untreated hypertension is highest in the two rural districts.
  3. Researchers compared the incidence of infection in vaccinated and unvaccinated groups.
  4. The prevalence of asthma among children near the interstate is the study's finding.
  5. A rising incidence of fraud complaints prompted the audit.
  6. Note that incidence is a rate and incidents are the things that happened.
See also
probability of/likelihood of, ratio of ... to, rise in/fall in, correlation with/between, exposure to, of

increase in/decrease in#

Pronunciation
/ˈɪnkris ɪn/, /ˈdikris ɪn/ · IN-kreess in, DEE-kreess in · weak /ˈɪnkris ən/, /ˈdikris ən/
Русский
рост чего-л. / снижение чего-л. — рус. родительный, не «в»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
in: the thing that grew or shrank (an increase in premiums). of: the size of the change (an increase of nine percent). to: the endpoint reached (an increase to $18 an hour). Gerunds follow in (a decrease in reporting); never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
an increase / decrease in [thing measured] · an increase of [amount] · an increase to [new level] · an increase from X to Y · on the increase · a [percentage] increase in [noun phrase]
Variants
a rise in and a fall in behave identically; see rise in/fall in · a decline in is the formal synonym of a decrease in · a raise in American English is a pay increase, where British English says a rise
Contrast
In for what changed, of for how much, and this is the error people actually make. ✗ an increase of premiums says the premiums themselves were increased in number; you want an increase in premiums. Conversely ✗ an increase in nine percent is wrong for the size. All four can stack: an increase in premiums of nine percent, from $200 to $218. ✗ an increase on premiums is an error, though a tax on premiums is fine — see tax on. The verb takes no preposition when transitive (they increased the fee) and by for the amount (increased by nine percent).

A rise or fall in an amount, and the size of it.

  1. There's been a sharp increase in emergency room visits since June.
  2. The board approved an increase of four percent for all classified staff.
  3. We saw a steady decrease in complaints after the new signage went up.
  4. The fee goes up by an increase to $45 effective in March.
  5. Enrollment showed an increase from 812 to 940 in two years.
  6. Bicycle theft is on the increase downtown.
See also
rise in/fall in, improvement in/on, change in/of, demand for, in

influence on/over#

Pronunciation
/ˈɪnfluəns ɑn/, /ˈɪnfluəns ˈoʊvɚ/ · IN-floo-unss on, IN-floo-unss OH-ver · weak /ˈɪnfluəns ən/; over has no weak form
Русский
влияние на что-л., власть над кем-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun; a wh-clause follows on in the frame influence on what gets published. Never a to-infinitive (✗ influence to change the vote) and never a that-clause.
Pattern
an influence on [outcome / person] · influence over [person / process] · the influence of X on Y · under the influence of [noun phrase] · exert / have influence on [noun phrase] · a bad influence on [person]
Variants
influence on — the effect produced · influence over — the power held, always with a person or process as complement · influence with — standing that lets you get things done (she has influence with the zoning board)
Contrast
On is an effect; over is power; with is access. His influence on the design changed how it looks; his influence over the committee means he controls it; his influence with the committee means they listen to him. ✗ influence to and ✗ influence for are errors. Under the influence on its own is an American legal idiom for intoxication and takes no complement. The verb takes a direct object with no preposition: the weather influenced the decision, ✗ influenced on the decision.

Power to affect how something turns out, or the effect itself.

  1. Her thesis adviser had an enormous influence on the way she writes.
  2. The family retains real influence over the endowment's investments.
  3. The influence of Dutch settlement on local place names is still visible.
  4. He was arrested for driving under the influence.
  5. That neighbor has been a bad influence on both of my kids.
  6. She has some influence with the county commissioners.
See also
effect on, impact on, control over/of, authority on/over, over

information about/on#

Pronunciation
/ˌɪnfɚˈmeɪʃən əˈbaʊt/ · in-fer-MAY-shun uh-BOWT · weak /ˌɪnfɚˈmeɪʃən ˈbaʊt/, /ˌɪnfɚˈmeɪʃən ən/
Русский
информация о чём-л., сведения по — в англ. неисчисляемо (✗ informations)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (information about applying for aid), or wh-clause (information on how to appeal) — never a to-infinitive (✗ information to apply for aid). It does take a bare that-clause in formal use: information that the shipment had been diverted.
Pattern
information about / on [topic] · information from [source] · information for [audience] · for your information · a piece of information · withhold / disclose information about [noun phrase]
Variants
information on — a subject formally treated (information on the vaccine) · information about — the neutral conversational form · information regarding — bureaucratic
Contrast
The countability rule is the whole trap. Information is uncountable in American English: ✗ an information, ✗ informations, ✗ many informations, ✗ these informations. Use a piece of information, some information, much information, a lot of information. Speakers of French, Spanish, Italian, and German all have a countable equivalent, which is why this error is so durable. On prepositions, ✗ information of the delay is an error (though notification of the delay is correct), and for names the audience rather than the subject.

Facts provided or learned about something.

  1. There's very little information about the site's earlier owners.
  2. The website has information on how to appeal a denial.
  3. I got that information from the county recorder's office.
  4. This packet is information for new parents, not a legal document.
  5. One useful piece of information came out of the whole meeting.
  6. The agency withheld information about the second inspection.
See also
knowledge of/about, advice on/about, report on/of, evidence of/for, about

injunction against#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈdʒʌŋkʃən əˈɡɛnst/ · in-JUNK-shun uh-GENST · weak against has no weak form; the noun keeps full stress on -JUNK-
Русский
юр. судебный запрет — предписание суда не делать чего-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
legal — motions, orders, and the news coverage of them; an American meets the word when a court blocks a law, a strike, or a merger
Complement
against: the person or act enjoined, as a noun phrase or gerund (an injunction against publishing the file). to: a bare infinitive naming what the order compels (an injunction to restore service), which is the mandatory rather than the prohibitory kind. Never a that-clause. The archaic non-legal sense ("a solemn instruction") also takes to + infinitive.
Pattern
an injunction against [a person / an act] · an injunction to [verb] · seek / obtain / dissolve / violate an injunction · a preliminary / permanent injunction · a temporary restraining order · enjoin [someone] from [verb-ing] (the verb)
Contrast
Against stops something; to plus a verb starts something. An injunction against the demolition forbids; an injunction to reconnect the water commands. ✗ an injunction to demolish would order the opposite of what the plaintiff wanted, so the slip is expensive. Note the verb, which takes a different preposition entirely: the court enjoined the county from demolishing the building — enjoin from + -ing, never ✗ enjoin to stop. Against prohibition on/against: a prohibition is a standing rule, an injunction is a court order aimed at named parties.

A court order forbidding or compelling a specific act.

  1. The union sought an injunction against the lockout and got it in a day.
  2. A federal judge issued an injunction against enforcing the ordinance.
  3. The injunction against publishing the documents lasted nine days.
  4. She violated the injunction twice and was held in contempt.
  5. The order is an injunction to restore service, not merely to stop cutting it.
  6. Plaintiffs asked for a preliminary injunction against the merger closing.
See also
prohibition on/against, moratorium on, judgment against/for, remedy for/against, jurisdiction over, against

inquiry into#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈkwaɪri ˈɪntu/, /ˈɪnkwəri ˈɪntu/ · in-KWY-ree IN-too, IN-kwuh-ree IN-too · weak /ˈɪntə/ · both stress patterns are standard American; IN-kwuh-ree is commoner in academic speech
Русский
расследование чего-л.; также запрос, наведение справок о чём-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
into: the subject investigated, as a noun phrase or gerund (an inquiry into how the money moved takes a wh-clause; an inquiry into hiring practices takes a gerund) — never a to-infinitive (✗ an inquiry to find the cause, unless to find is a purpose clause). about / regarding: the simple-question sense (an inquiry about the vacancy). from: who is asking.
Pattern
an inquiry into [a subject] · an inquiry about [a detail] · inquiries from [customers / reporters] · open / launch / conduct / close an inquiry · a line of inquiry · a congressional / internal inquiry
Variants
American English spells it inquiry in both senses; enquiry is British and, where the distinction is kept there, is reserved for the ordinary question. Do not import the split into American copy.
Contrast
Into for an investigation, about for a question. An inquiry into the crash is a formal proceeding; an inquiry about the flight is a phone call. ✗ an inquiry on the crash is an error. Against investigation into, which is nearly a synonym: an inquiry is the more neutral and more official-sounding word, which is why bodies that do not want to accuse anyone open an inquiry rather than an investigation. Note the American office phrase for further inquiries, contact — plural, and only in the question sense.

A formal investigation, or a request for information.

  1. The board opened an inquiry into how the contract was awarded.
  2. She had one inquiry about the apartment and it came from a bot.
  3. A congressional inquiry into the outage is scheduled for June.
  4. That line of inquiry went nowhere in the first two interviews.
  5. The paper's inquiry into the county's overtime billing ran for a year.
  6. All press inquiries go through the communications office.
See also
investigation into, research on/into, report on/of, information about/on, insight into, into

insight into#

Pronunciation
/ˈɪnsaɪt ˈɪntu/ · IN-syte IN-too · weak /ˈɪnsaɪt ˈɪntə/
Русский
понимание сути чего-л., проникновение в суть сложного
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (insight into running a small hospital), or a wh-clause (insight into how the fund actually works) — never a to-infinitive (✗ insight to run a hospital) and never a that-clause.
Pattern
insight into [noun phrase / verb-ing / wh-clause] · gain / offer / provide insight into [noun phrase] · a valuable insight into [noun phrase] · with the insight of [experience]
Variants
insights into (plural) — the commoner form in business and academic writing; the singular is more often abstract (a person of great insight)
Contrast
Into is fixed — ✗ insight in, ✗ insight on, ✗ insight about, ✗ insight of. The last of these is the trap for anyone who has learned understanding of, grasp of, and awareness of, all of which take of: insight is the odd one in that set. Note the second, uncountable sense meaning perceptiveness (a woman of considerable insight), which takes no complement at all, and the clinical sense in psychiatry (the patient lacks insight), likewise bare.

A clear or penetrating understanding of something complicated.

  1. The diaries give real insight into how the shop was run.
  2. Her time on the ward gave her insight into what the families actually need.
  3. The audit offered no insight into where the money went.
  4. That's a useful insight into the way the committee makes decisions.
  5. Twenty years of teaching gave him insight into how children fake reading.
  6. The book's insights into small-town politics are its best feature.
See also
understanding of, grasp of, awareness of, knowledge of/about, into

interest in#

Pronunciation
/ˈɪntrəst ɪn/ · IN-trust in · weak /ˈɪntrəst ən/
Русский
интерес к чему-л.; доля, участие в чём-л.; ср. interest on — проценты по кредиту
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (an interest in restoring old radios) — never a to-infinitive (✗ an interest to restore old radios) and never a that-clause (✗ an interest that the radios be restored). A wh-clause is fine (an interest in what happens to the building). The financial sense takes on: interest on the loan.
Pattern
an interest in [subject / verb-ing] · interest on [a loan / a balance] · an interest in [a company] (a stake) · in the interest(s) of [noun phrase] · of interest to [person] · take an interest in [noun phrase] · a conflict of interest
Variants
in the interest of and in the interests of — both standard American English; the singular is commoner in in the interest of time · interest on is not a separate headword but the same noun in its financial frame: interest on the loan, interest on unpaid balances, accrue / pay / earn interest on [an amount] — and it is the one frame of the three where ✗ interest of is flatly wrong
Contrast
In for curiosity or a stake; on for money. Her interest in the bank is either her curiosity about it or her ownership share; the interest on the loan is what it costs to borrow — and ✗ the interest of the loan is an error. ✗ an interest for photography and ✗ an interest about photography are errors as well. Note that of interest to reverses the direction: the file is of interest to investigators means they are interested in it. The adjective pair is the notorious one — interested in something, but interesting to someone — and ✗ I am interested to buy should be interested in buying unless you mean curiosity about a result (interested to hear how it went).

Curiosity about something, a financial stake, or money paid for borrowing.

  1. She's had an interest in nineteenth-century glass since college.
  2. The interest on the second mortgage is what's killing them.
  3. He holds a twelve percent interest in the brewery.
  4. In the interest of time, let's take the rest by email.
  5. The recordings are of considerable interest to linguists.
  6. Nobody on the board took the slightest interest in the audit.
See also
fondness for, focus on, concern about/for, share in/of, interested in, in

interview with#

Pronunciation
/ˈɪntɚvju wɪθ/ · IN-ter-vyoo with · weak /ˈɪntɚvju wəθ/
Русский
интервью с кем-л.; также собеседование с кандидатом
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
with: the other person, in either role (an interview with the mayor). for: the job or program sought. about / on: the subject. Never a gerund after with, never an infinitive, never a clause.
Pattern
an interview with [person] · an interview for [job / program] · an interview about / on [topic] · give / grant / conduct an interview · in an interview with [outlet] · a job interview
Contrast
With
names the other party regardless of who is asking
an interview with the mayor is the same event whether the mayor sat down with a reporter or with a hiring panel. ✗ an interview to the mayor is an error, as is ✗ an interview of the mayor in ordinary usage, though an interview of forty candidates is possible in formal survey writing. For names the position: an interview for the assistant director job, ✗ an interview of the job. The verb takes a direct object and no preposition — she interviewed the mayor, ✗ she interviewed with the mayor if she was the reporter — but American English does use interview with for the candidate's side: I interviewed with three firms.

A formal conversation, for a story or for a job.

  1. In an interview with the local paper, she refused to rule it out.
  2. I have an interview for the assistant curator position on Thursday.
  3. The podcast is a two-hour interview with the man who built the bridge.
  4. He gave one interview about the settlement and never spoke of it again.
  5. The interview with the arresting officer was never recorded.
  6. She interviewed with four firms and got three offers.

introduction to#

Pronunciation
/ˌɪntrəˈdʌkʃən tu/ · in-truh-DUK-shun too · weak /ˌɪntrəˈdʌkʃən tə/
Русский
знакомство с чем-л., введение в предмет; также внедрение нового
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
to: a subject or a person — noun phrase, or a gerund in the subject sense (an introduction to keeping bees) — never a to-infinitive (✗ an introduction to keep bees). of: the thing or person being brought in (the introduction of the new system).
Pattern
an introduction to [subject / person] · the introduction of [thing brought in] · a letter of introduction · by way of introduction · give [someone] an introduction to [subject]
Contrast
To for what you are being introduced to; of for what is being introduced. An introduction to statistics is a first course; the introduction of statistics into the curriculum is the act of adding it. ✗ an introduction of statistics in the course sense is a common and confusing error. The -ing rule applies to the to frame because a noun fits the slot: an introduction to keeping bees, ✗ an introduction to keep bees. The verb takes both: introduce someone to someone, introduce a bill, and ✗ introduce someone with someone is an error.

A first exposure to a subject or a person, or the act of bringing something in.

  1. The course is a gentle introduction to medieval Latin.
  2. That summer was my introduction to commercial fishing.
  3. She wrote him a letter of introduction to the archivist in Madrid.
  4. The introduction of electronic filing cut the backlog in half.
  5. This book is a decent introduction to keeping bees in a cold climate.
  6. By way of introduction, I should say I have no legal training.
See also
access to, reference to, approach to, experience of/in/with, to

investigation into#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˌvɛstəˈɡeɪʃən ˈɪntu/ · in-ves-tuh-GAY-shun IN-too · weak /ɪnˌvɛstəˈɡeɪʃən ˈɪntə/
Русский
расследование чего-л. — рус. родительный, не «в»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
into / of: the subject or target, as a noun phrase, gerund, or wh-clause (an investigation into how the fire started) — never a to-infinitive (✗ an investigation to determine the cause as a complement, though that is fine as a purpose clause). for: the suspected offense, in under investigation for. by: the investigating body.
Pattern
an investigation into [an event / a practice] · an investigation of [a person / a matter] · under investigation for [an offense] · open / launch / drop / close an investigation · a criminal / internal / independent investigation · the subject of an investigation
Variants
investigation into and investigation of are both standard American English: into emphasizes the probing of a question, of the examination of an object or a person. An investigation of Senator Ruiz points at a person; an investigation into the contracts points at a question — and journalists prefer into precisely because it accuses no one.
Contrast
✗ An investigation on is an error, and it is the commonest one from Spanish and Portuguese speakers. Against inquiry into: an investigation implies suspected wrongdoing, an inquiry need not. Against research on/into: research seeks knowledge, an investigation seeks a culprit or a cause — which is why research into the cause of a fire and an investigation into the same fire are done by different people.

An official effort to find out how something happened or who is responsible.

  1. The NTSB opened an investigation into the derailment within hours.
  2. An internal investigation of the sergeant's conduct is still open.
  3. He has been under investigation for wire fraud since last spring.
  4. The investigation into how the ballots were stored took four months.
  5. Two reporters spent a year on an investigation into county overtime.
  6. She was told the investigation had been closed without findings.
See also
inquiry into, research on/into, report on/of, evidence of/for, judgment against/for, into

invitation to#

Pronunciation
/ˌɪnvɪˈteɪʃən tu/ · in-vih-TAY-shun too · weak /ˌɪnvɪˈteɪʃən tə/
Русский
приглашение на (мероприятие) или к кому-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
to + noun phrase: the event (an invitation to the wedding). to + bare infinitive: the act invited (an invitation to speak). Both are standard, and the to is a preposition in the first and an infinitive marker in the second. for: a date or an occasion (an invitation for Friday). ✗ an invitation to speaking.
Pattern
an invitation to [event] · an invitation to [verb] · an invitation from [host] · an invitation for [date] · accept / decline / extend an invitation · at the invitation of [person] · by invitation only
Contrast
This is the one noun in the file where to + noun and to + infinitive are both right, and the meanings differ. An invitation to the conference is a summons to attend; an invitation to speak at the conference is a request to perform. Neither takes -ing: ✗ an invitation to speaking. ✗ an invitation for the wedding is an error where an invitation for Friday is fine, because for takes a time, not an event. The verb keeps both frames: invite her to the wedding, invite her to speak, and ✗ invite her for speaking is wrong.

A request that someone attend or take part.

  1. The invitation to the wedding came six weeks late.
  2. She turned down an invitation to testify before the subcommittee.
  3. There's an invitation from the dean sitting in your inbox.
  4. We got an invitation for the Saturday session instead.
  5. He was there at the invitation of the tribal council.
  6. The reception is by invitation only.
See also
request for, demand for, order for, permission for, to

J#

judgment against/for#

Pronunciation
/ˈdʒʌdʒmənt əˈɡɛnst/, /ˈdʒʌdʒmənt fɔr/ · JUJ-munt uh-GENST, JUJ-munt for · weak /ˈdʒʌdʒmənt fɚ/; against has no weak form
Русский
юр. решение суда против кого-л. / в чью-л. пользу; также суждение, рассудительность
Type
noun + preposition
Register
legal in the against/for frames; neutral in the everyday sense of an opinion
Complement
against / for: the losing and winning party, as a noun phrase. on: the motion or claim decided (judgment on the pleadings). of: the court that entered it. about / on: what an opinion is about, in the everyday sense, where a gerund works (a judgment about hiring him). Never a to-infinitive (✗ a judgment to hire him) and, in the legal frames, never a clause.
Pattern
a judgment against [a defendant] · judgment for [a plaintiff] · judgment on [the pleadings / the merits] · enter / vacate / satisfy / appeal a judgment · a default / summary / money judgment · in my judgment · against my better judgment
Variants
the American spelling is judgment, without the e, in both the legal and the everyday sense; judgement is British and, in an American brief, reads as a typo
Contrast
Against is who pays, for
is who collects
a judgment against the contractor and judgment for the homeowner describe one piece of paper from two sides. ✗ a judgment to the plaintiff is an error. In the everyday sense the prepositions change completely — a judgment about his character, in my judgment — and judgment there is uncountable in some frames (poor judgment, ✗ a poor judgment). Note judgment call, an American fixed compound, and against my better judgment, which needs no complement at all.

A court's final decision in a case, or, in ordinary use, an opinion or the capacity to form one.

  1. The bank has a judgment against him for $18,000 and it follows him.
  2. The court entered judgment for the plaintiff on the second count only.
  3. The motion asks for judgment on the pleadings before discovery starts.
  4. She has good judgment about people and terrible judgment about cars.
  5. Against my better judgment, I co-signed the lease.
  6. A default judgment against the tenant issued when nobody appeared.
See also
damages for, claim against/for/to, injunction against, liability for/to, default on/under, against

jurisdiction over#

Pronunciation
/ˌdʒʊrɪsˈdɪkʃən ˈoʊvɚ/ · joor-iss-DIK-shun OH-ver · over has no weak form
Русский
юрисдикция над, подсудность — правомочие суда или ведомства и его пределы
Type
noun + preposition
Register
legal and governmental; also ordinary speech about which agency handles what
Complement
over: the person, place, subject, or dispute, as a noun phrase — never a gerund (✗ jurisdiction over deciding the fee), never an infinitive, and never a clause. in: a place or a field (jurisdiction in admiralty, no jurisdiction in Ohio). to: a bare infinitive, in the "power to act" frame (jurisdiction to hear the appeal).
Pattern
jurisdiction over [a person / a case / a territory] · jurisdiction to [verb] · within / outside the jurisdiction of [a body] · have / lack / decline / assert jurisdiction · personal / subject-matter / original / appellate jurisdiction · concurrent jurisdiction
Contrast
✗ Jurisdiction on is an error and a frequent one; the noun takes over, matching authority on/over in its over sense and control over/of. Note the two to's again: jurisdiction to hear the case takes a bare verb, and there is no noun-phrase version of it — ✗ jurisdiction to the case. The word is also a countable noun meaning a place (forty-two jurisdictions have adopted the rule), and in that sense it takes no preposition at all.

A court's or an agency's legal power to act, and the reach of that power.

  1. The federal court has no jurisdiction over a purely local zoning dispute.
  2. Whether the sheriff has jurisdiction over the campus was the whole argument.
  3. The agency asserted jurisdiction over wetlands connected to navigable water.
  4. The panel lacks jurisdiction to hear an interlocutory appeal here.
  5. Forty-two jurisdictions have adopted some version of the model act.
  6. That building sits outside the jurisdiction of the city fire marshal.
See also
authority on/over, control over/of, custody of, injunction against, applicability to, over

justification for#

Pronunciation
/ˌdʒʌstəfəˈkeɪʃən fɔr/ · jus-tuh-fuh-KAY-shun for · weak /ˌdʒʌstəfəˈkeɪʃən fɚ/
Русский
оправдание чего-л., обоснование правильности или необходимости
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (no justification for raising the fee) — the edited frame. A to-infinitive occurs (no justification to raise the fee) and is common in speech and business writing, but for + -ing is the form that never draws a mark. A bare that-clause attaches to the noun as well: the justification that the roof was leaking.
Pattern
justification for [noun phrase / verb-ing] · no justification for [noun phrase] · offer / provide / demand a justification · in justification of [an act] (formal) · without justification
The verb takes a gerund and refuses the infinitive outright
nothing justifies raising the fee, ✗ nothing justifies to raise the fee — a harder rule than the noun's, and the reason writers who get the verb right still stumble on the noun. Against reason for: a reason explains why something happened, a justification argues that it was right — which is why the reason for the delay is neutral and the justification for the delay implies somebody is being blamed. ✗ justification of the fee increase means the act of justifying it, not the argument.

An argument that something was right or necessary.

  1. There's no justification for charging tenants for a repair the landlord caused.
  2. The memo offers three justifications for the reorganization and believes none of them.
  3. What was the justification for holding the vote without notice?
  4. He fired her without justification and the arbitrator said so.
  5. The only justification for keeping the second office is the lease.
  6. In justification of the decision, the chair cited a rule that does not exist.
See also
reason for, grounds for, basis for, rationale for, excuse for, for

K#

key to#

Pronunciation
/ki tu/ · kee too · weak /ki tə/
Русский
ключ к — то, что даёт разгадку или делает что-л. возможным
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (the key to solving it) — never a to-infinitive (✗ the key to solve it). This is one of the most frequently botched -ing patterns in American business and self-help writing.
Pattern
the key to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · a key to [lock / door] · hold the key to [noun phrase] · the key to success · key [adjective] to [noun phrase]
Contrast
Because a noun fits the slot — the key to the door, the key to success — the to is a preposition, and a verb after it must take -ing: the key to getting funded, ✗ the key to get funded. The adjective key uses the same preposition and is likewise fixed: a factor key to the outcome, ✗ key for the outcome in that sense, though key for our purposes is fine. ✗ the key of success is an error; the key of C is music, a different noun. Compare solution to and answer to, which share the preposition and the trap.

The thing that makes something possible or explains it.

  1. The key to getting a permit here is starting in January.
  2. Patience is the key to not ruining a brisket.
  3. The county clerk holds the key to the whole dispute.
  4. The key to the back gate is under the third flowerpot.
  5. Consistent watering is the key to keeping tomatoes from splitting.
  6. Nobody has found the key to why the ledgers stop in 1913.
See also
solution to, answer to, reason for, access to, to

knowledge of/about#

Pronunciation
/ˈnɑlɪdʒ ʌv/ · NOL-ij uv · weak /ˈnɑlɪdʒ əv/ → /ˈnɑlɪdʒ ə/
Русский
знание чего-л., знания о чём-л. — в англ. неисчисляемо
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: noun phrase, gerund (knowledge of having been passed over), or wh-clause (no knowledge of what the account was for) — never a to-infinitive. The noun also takes a bare that-clause: knowledge that the tank was leaking.
Pattern
knowledge of [subject] · knowledge about [subject] · knowledge that [clause] · a working knowledge of [skill] · to the best of my knowledge · without someone's knowledge · common knowledge
Variants
knowledge of — the edited default · knowledge about — conversational, and normal when the subject is a person or an event rather than a skill
Contrast
Of is the form to write. ✗ knowledge on the subject is an error; ✗ knowledges does not exist in American English, which treats the noun as uncountable — say a body of knowledge, a working knowledge of Spanish, ✗ he has good knowledges. The one indefinite article that is allowed is in the pattern a working / thorough / detailed knowledge of X, where the adjective licenses it. Distinguish from awareness of (perception that something exists) and understanding of (grasp of how it works); knowledge is the possession of facts.

Facts, information, and skills a person has.

  1. She has a working knowledge of Portuguese and fluent Spanish.
  2. His knowledge of the building's wiring saved us three days.
  3. The lease was signed without my knowledge.
  4. To the best of my knowledge, nobody has lived there since 2011.
  5. Knowledge about the outbreak spread faster than the outbreak.
  6. Knowledge that the tank was leaking would have changed the sale.
See also
awareness of, understanding of, grasp of, information about/on, of

L#

lack of#

Pronunciation
/læk ʌv/ · lak uv · weak /læk əv/ → /læk ə/
Русский
отсутствие, нехватка чего-л. — рус. родительный (глагол lack идёт без of)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (a lack of planning, a lack of anyone taking responsibility) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a lack of to plan) and never a that-clause. The complement is normally uncountable or plural — a lack of funding, a lack of options — and a singular count noun needs a modifier to sit comfortably: a lack of a clear plan is fine where bare a lack of a plan is stiff and no plan is the plainer fix.
Pattern
a lack of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · for lack of [noun phrase] · through lack of [noun phrase] · a lack of [interest / evidence / sleep] · lacking in [quality]
Contrast
A star noun-versus-verb shift. The verb lack is bare transitive — the report lacks detail — so ✗ the report lacks of detail and ✗ he lacks of experience are errors, and they are extremely common among speakers of Romance languages, where the equivalent verb is prepositional. The noun cannot go without of: ✗ a lack detail. A third form completes the set: the adjective is lacking in (the argument is lacking in evidence), so all three words in the family take a different construction — verb bare, noun of, adjective in. Lack for survives only in the fixed litotes he lacked for nothing (he had everything he needed); outside it, ✗ the report lacks for detail is an error.

The absence or shortage of something needed.

  1. The whole project failed for lack of a single accountable owner.
  2. There's a real lack of affordable rentals within walking distance of the plant.
  3. Her lack of interest in the job showed in the first ten minutes.
  4. The case was dismissed for lack of evidence.
  5. A lack of sleep will do more damage than a bad diet.
  6. The estate was large and the children lacked for nothing.
See also
need for, supply of, lack something, demand for, lack, of

letter to/from#

Pronunciation
/ˈlɛtɚ tu/, /ˈlɛtɚ frʌm/ · LET-er too, LET-er frum · weak /ˈlɛtɚ tə/, /ˈlɛtɚ frəm/
Русский
письмо кому-л. / от кого-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
to: the recipient. from: the sender. of: the letter's function, in fixed compounds (a letter of resignation, a letter of recommendation, a letter of credit). about: the subject. Never a gerund, an infinitive, or a clause after to.
Pattern
a letter to [recipient] · a letter from [sender] · a letter of [resignation / recommendation] · a letter about [subject] · write a letter to [person] · a letter to the editor
Contrast
To and from are unmissable; the trap is the verb. American English allows write me with no preposition — write me when you get in — where British English requires write to me. Both write to her and write her are correct in the United States, but only write to her travels. ✗ a letter for the editor is an error where a letter for you (meaning addressed to you and now in my hand) is fine. The of compounds are fixed: ✗ a letter for resignation, ✗ a recommendation letter of her — say a letter of recommendation for her.

A written message, named by who sent it and who gets it.

  1. She wrote a blistering letter to the editor about the sidewalk plan.
  2. There was a letter from the county assessor waiting on the porch.
  3. He needs three letters of recommendation by the fifteenth.
  4. The letter to shareholders never mentioned the write-down.
  5. I got a letter about the easement two years after the sale.
  6. Her letter of resignation ran to a single sentence.
See also
reply to, response to, request for, report on/of, to

liability for/to#

Pronunciation
/ˌlaɪəˈbɪləti fɔr/, /ˌlaɪəˈbɪləti tu/ · ly-uh-BIL-uh-tee for, ly-uh-BIL-uh-tee too · weak /ˌlaɪəˈbɪləti fɚ/, /ˌlaɪəˈbɪləti tə/
Русский
юр. ответственность за что-л. перед кем-л.; также обязательство, пассив баланса
Type
noun + preposition
Register
legal, financial, and insurance; the "burden" sense is neutral and informal
Complement
for: the harm, debt, or act you can be made to answer for, as a noun phrase or gerund (liability for failing to inspect) — never a to-infinitive (✗ liability to inspect, which would mean something else entirely). to: the person or body you owe it to (liability to third parties). under: the instrument or statute it arises beneath. Never a clause.
Pattern
liability for [damages / negligence / the debt] · liability to [a claimant / creditors] · liability under [a statute / the contract] · limit / assume / disclaim / insure against liability · joint and several liability · a limited liability company · liabilities (the balance-sheet plural)
Contrast
For the harm, to
the person
liability for the collapse names what happened, liability to the tenants names who can sue — and a single sentence often needs both, its liability to the tenants for the collapse. The adjective is the classic trap: liable for means responsible (liable for the damage), but liable to plus a verb means likely (liable to fall), and liable to plus a person means answerable to them. Note the accounting plural, which takes no preposition: assets and liabilities. In everyday American speech a liability with no complement means a burden — the third baseman is a liability.

Legal responsibility for something, or the person or body one is responsible to.

  1. The waiver does not eliminate liability for gross negligence.
  2. The company's liability to bondholders comes ahead of anything owed to shareholders.
  3. Liability for failing to inspect the boiler fell on the management company.
  4. He set up an LLC to keep personal liability away from the rental property.
  5. The audit moved $4 million from assets to liabilities.
  6. Their liability under the indemnity clause is capped at the purchase price.
See also
responsibility for, damages for, obligation to/under, duty to/of, immunity from, waiver of

lien on/against#

Pronunciation
/lin ɑn/, /lin əˈɡɛnst/ · leen on, leen uh-GENST · weak /lin ən/ · American /lin/ rhymes with lean; the older two-syllable /ˈliən/ survives in some courtrooms
Русский
юр. право удержания, залоговое обременение имущества в обеспечение долга
Type
noun + preposition
Register
legal — real-estate closings, contractor disputes, tax collection. An American meets it when a title search turns up a mechanic's lien, or when the IRS files one.
Complement
on / against: the property or fund encumbered, as a noun phrase — never a gerund, never an infinitive, and never a clause. for: what is owed (a lien for unpaid taxes). in favor of: who holds it.
Pattern
a lien on [property] · a lien against [property / an estate] · a lien for [unpaid work / taxes] · file / perfect / release / foreclose a lien · a mechanic's / tax / judgment lien · free and clear of liens
Variants
lien on and lien against are interchangeable in American practice; on is commoner in statutes and title reports, against in collection letters and in loose speech about a person (a lien against him really means against his property)
Contrast
The object is the property, not the debtor: strictly, ✗ a lien on the contractor is imprecise, because the lien attaches to the building. Against claim against/for/to: a claim is a demand for payment, a lien is a security interest that rides on a specific thing until the debt clears. A mortgage is one kind of consensual lien; a mechanic's lien is imposed by statute whether the owner agrees or not. ✗ a lien of the property is an error.

A legal claim attached to a piece of property as security for a debt.

  1. The roofer filed a mechanic's lien on the house eleven days after the last invoice.
  2. There's a tax lien against the property that predates their purchase.
  3. The title came back with two liens on it and one of them was thirty years old.
  4. A judgment lien attaches to any real estate he owns in the county.
  5. The bank released its lien on the truck the week the note was paid.
  6. Buyers want the parcel delivered free and clear of liens.
See also
title to, claim against/for/to, easement over/across, default on/under, judgment against/for, on

limit to/on#

Pronunciation
/ˈlɪmɪt tu/, /ˈlɪmɪt ɑn/ · LIM-it too, LIM-it on · weak /ˈlɪmɪt tə/, /ˈlɪmɪt ən/
Русский
предел чего-л., ограничение на что-л. — естественная или установленная граница
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
to: what has a natural boundary (a limit to my patience). on: what is being restricted by rule (a limit on spending). Gerunds follow both (no limit to what she'll try, a limit on borrowing). Never a to-infinitive (✗ a limit to spend) and never a clause.
Pattern
a limit to [thing with a natural end] · a limit on [activity restricted] · the limit of [range / property] · within limits · off limits · set / impose / exceed a limit
Contrast
To is a natural ceiling; on is an imposed rule. There's a limit to how much I'll take describes reality; a limit on how much you may withdraw describes policy. ✗ a limit to withdrawals in the policy sense is the usual slip. A third form, the limit of, names the extent of a thing (the limits of the property, the limits of my knowledge). Note off limits, an American military idiom that takes no complement, and the plural in the fixed within limits. The verb takes a direct object and to for the ceiling: limit contributions to $500, ✗ limit contributions on $500.

A boundary, natural or imposed.

  1. There's a limit to how many times I'll explain the same form.
  2. The city put a limit on short-term rentals in the historic district.
  3. The speed limit drops to twenty-five past the school.
  4. That whole floor is off limits to visitors.
  5. We're at the limit of what the old wiring can carry.
  6. There seems to be no limit to what she'll volunteer for.
See also
control over/of, exception to, rule against/for, room for, on

link between/to#

Pronunciation
/lɪŋk bɪˈtwin/, /lɪŋk tu/ · link bih-TWEEN, link too · weak /lɪŋk bəˈtwin/, /lɪŋk tə/
Русский
связь между чем-л. / с чем-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
between: two or more items joined by and (a link between diet and blood pressure). to / with: a single item. Gerunds follow both. Never a to-infinitive (✗ a link to reduce risk) and never a that-clause.
Pattern
a link between X and Y · a link to / with [noun phrase] · a link in the chain · establish / find / deny a link between X and Y · a link to [a web page]
Variants
link with — an association or partnership, slightly more British in feel · link to — the American default and the only form for hyperlinks
Contrast
Between
requires two things and the others require one
✗ a link between smoking is incomplete; ✗ a link to smoking and cancer should be between. In epidemiology and reporting, a link between X and Y is deliberately weaker than a cause of — see cause of — and confusing the two is the standard failure of science journalism. The verb takes both frames: link X to Y, link X with Y, and ✗ link between X and Y is not available to the verb.

A connection or association between two things.

  1. Researchers found no link between the additive and the tumors.
  2. There's a documented link between childhood lead exposure and later reading scores.
  3. The company denies any link to the offshore accounts.
  4. The archive page has a link to the original filing.
  5. He was the last link to the generation that built the place.
  6. The report suggests a link with two earlier cases in Ohio.
See also
connection with/to/between, relationship with/between, cause of, similarity to/between, between

love for/of#

Pronunciation
/lʌv fɔr/, /lʌv ʌv/ · luv for, luv uv · weak /lʌv fɚ/, /lʌv əv/ → /lʌv ə/
Русский
любовь к — к человеку или к своему делу; рус. «к», не «для»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for: a person, or a thing regarded with warmth (her love for her students). of: an activity, art, or abstraction (a love of music, a love of arguing) — gerunds go here, never after for in edited prose. Never a to-infinitive (✗ a love to sing). In love with takes a person.
Pattern
love for [person] · love of [activity / art] · in love with [person] · for the love of [noun phrase] · a labor of love · fall in love with [person]
Variants
love for and love of overlap heavily with things (a love for jazz and a love of jazz are both fine); the split holds firmly only with people, where for is required
Contrast
For points at a person, of at a pursuit — his love for his sister is affection, while his love of his sister reads as connoisseurship, which is why the swap misfires. With activities, of is the safer choice, and it is close to obligatory before a gerund: a love of cooking, where a love for cooking is heard but marked. ✗ love to as a noun is an error, although the verb takes it freely (I'd love to). The adjective phrase is in love with, never ✗ in love to or ✗ in love of, and fall in love with is fixed.

Deep affection for a person, or strong enthusiasm for a pursuit.

  1. Her love for her students outlasted three principals.
  2. A love of early jazz recordings runs through everything he wrote.
  3. He married her for love, whatever the family said.
  4. It was a labor of love and it took eleven years.
  5. For the love of God, close the gate.
  6. She fell in love with the house before she saw the roof.
See also
fondness for, taste for/in, respect for, desire for, for

M#

marriage to#

Pronunciation
/ˈmɛrɪdʒ tu/ · MAIR-ij too · weak /ˈmɛrɪdʒ tə/
Русский
брак с кем-л., женитьба на — рус. «с/на», англ. to (✗ marriage with)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
to: the spouse (her marriage to Tom). between: both parties from outside (a marriage between cousins). Never a gerund, never an infinitive, never a clause. ✗ marriage with is not standard American English in the personal sense, though it survives in figurative use (a marriage of convenience, a marriage with industry).
Pattern
a marriage to [spouse] · a marriage between X and Y · by marriage · a marriage of convenience · the marriage of X and Y (figurative)
Contrast
One of the sharpest verb-to-noun shifts in English, and the source of a famous error. The verb marry is bare transitive — she married Tom, ✗ she married with Tom, ✗ she married to Tom — while the past-participial adjective takes to: she is married to Tom, ✗ married with Tom. The noun takes to as well: her marriage to Tom. So the sequence is: marry Ø, married to, marriage to. ✗ married with is the single most recognizable Spanish, Italian, and Portuguese transfer error in English, and married with children is correct only because with there means "having."

The union of two people, named by who married whom.

  1. Her marriage to Tom lasted longer than either family expected.
  2. This is his second marriage, and her first.
  3. The marriage between the two families ended a forty-year feud over water.
  4. He's my uncle by marriage, not by blood.
  5. It was a marriage of convenience that turned out surprisingly well.
  6. The book's subject is the marriage of engineering and design at Bell Labs.
See also
relationship with/between, marry someone, connection with/to/between, love for/of, married to, to

matter with#

Pronunciation
/ˈmætɚ wɪθ/ · MAT-er with · weak /ˈmætɚ wəθ/
Русский
что не так с чем-л. — только в вопросах и отрицаниях: What's the matter with the car?
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral, chiefly spoken
Complement
with + noun phrase, in the fixed interrogative frame what's the matter with X? — never a gerund, an infinitive, or a clause. The unrelated a matter of takes a noun or a gerund (a matter of waiting), and a matter for takes the party responsible (a matter for the police).
Pattern
what's the matter with [noun phrase] · there's something the matter with [noun phrase] · a matter of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · a matter for [party] · as a matter of fact · no matter what / who / how
Contrast
With belongs only to the "what's wrong" frame. What's the matter with the car? asks what is broken; a matter of money means an issue of money; a matter for the courts means the courts' business — and ✗ what's the matter of the car mixes two idioms. The word takes no article in no matter what, and the fixed as a matter of fact has no complement. Note that in the "trouble" sense matter is almost always preceded by the and appears in questions and negatives, not in plain assertions: ✗ there is a matter with the car is not idiomatic; say there's something wrong with the car.

Whatever is wrong with something, and other fixed uses of the word.

  1. What's the matter with the printer this time?
  2. There's something the matter with the left rear tire.
  3. Getting the permit is just a matter of waiting out the clerk.
  4. Whether to prosecute is a matter for the district attorney.
  5. As a matter of fact, I have the receipt right here.
  6. We're going, no matter what the forecast says.
See also
problem with, trouble with, difficulty with/in, point in/of, with

memory of#

Pronunciation
/ˈmɛməri ʌv/ · MEM-uh-ree uv · weak /ˈmɛməri əv/ → /ˈmɛməri ə/
Русский
память о, воспоминание о чём-л.; также память как способность
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: what is remembered — noun phrase or gerund (a memory of standing in the driveway) — never a to-infinitive. for: the faculty, what a person is good at remembering (a memory for names). The noun also takes a bare that-clause in formal use: a clear memory that the door was locked.
Pattern
a memory of [thing remembered / verb-ing] · a memory for [names / faces / dates] · in memory of [person] · from memory · within living memory · jog someone's memory
Contrast
Of is what you remember; for is what you are good at remembering. A memory of faces is a recollection; a memory for faces is a talent — a total reversal from one preposition, and the pair is worth memorizing. ✗ a memory about is an error, and ✗ in memory to should be in memory of. Distinguish from memory (without notes) from in memory (as a memorial). The verb remember takes a direct object with no preposition (✗ remember of it, ✗ remember about it, though remind someone of it is correct).

A recollection, or the faculty that produces one.

  1. My clearest memory of that summer is the smell of creosote.
  2. She has an uncanny memory for names and none at all for faces.
  3. The bench was placed there in memory of a teacher who drowned in 1962.
  4. He recited all forty lines from memory.
  5. The flood of '72 is still within living memory in this town.
  6. A memory of standing in the driveway waiting for headlights is all he has left.
See also
awareness of, knowledge of/about, understanding of, idea of/for, of

moratorium on#

Pronunciation
/ˌmɔrəˈtɔriəm ɑn/ · mor-uh-TOR-ee-um on · weak /ˌmɔrəˈtɔriəm ən/
Русский
мораторий на — временный официальный запрет на какую-л. деятельность
Type
noun + preposition
Register
formal to neutral — government, finance, and journalism
Complement
a noun phrase or gerund (a moratorium on issuing new permits) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a moratorium to issue new permits) and never a clause.
Pattern
a moratorium on [an activity] · impose / declare / extend / lift a moratorium · a temporary / one-year / de facto moratorium · under a moratorium
Variants
the plural is moratoriums in American English and moratoria in stiffer or older prose; both are correct, and moratoriums is the safer choice outside a law review
Contrast
✗ A moratorium of and ✗ a moratorium against are errors: the noun takes on and nothing else. Against embargo on: a moratorium is a pause, expected to end; an embargo is a ban with a political purpose. Against prohibition on/against: a prohibition is permanent until repealed. Against injunction against: an injunction comes from a court and names parties, a moratorium comes from a legislature or an agency and applies to everyone.

An official, temporary halt to an activity.

  1. The county put a six-month moratorium on new short-term rental permits.
  2. Congress extended the moratorium on evictions twice and then let it lapse.
  3. There's been a de facto moratorium on hiring since the freeze in March.
  4. The board lifted the moratorium on drilling in the watershed last fall.
  5. A moratorium on issuing bonds would strand three projects mid-build.
  6. Nothing in the ordinance says when the moratorium ends.
See also
embargo on, prohibition on/against, injunction against, limit to/on, delay in, on

N#

need for#

Pronunciation
/nid fɔr/ · need for · weak /nid fɚ/
Русский
потребность в чём-л., нужда в — рус. «в», не «для»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for: a noun phrase naming what is needed (a need for more housing). The same noun takes a to-infinitive with no preposition: no need to worry. ✗ a need of survives only in the fixed in need of. ✗ a need for to worry mixes the two.
Pattern
a need for [noun phrase] · no need to [verb] · in need of [noun phrase] · meet / address a need for [noun phrase] · if need be · there's no need for [noun phrase]
Contrast
For takes a thing; to takes an act; of appears only after in need. A need for an inspection names the thing wanted, a need to inspect names the act, and the house is in need of an inspection is the fixed adjectival phrase — ✗ the house is in need for an inspection. There is need of is stiff but not wrong; American writing prefers there is a need for. The verb takes an object or an infinitive and never a preposition: we need an inspection, we need to inspect it, ✗ we need for an inspection (except in the dialectal need for you to sign, common in the American South).

A requirement for something, and the act it calls for.

  1. There's a documented need for another elementary school on the north side.
  2. No need to apologize — the mail does that to everybody.
  3. The whole porch is in need of rebuilding.
  4. The grant is meant to meet a need for rural broadband.
  5. There's no need for alarm; the readings are within range.
  6. I'll drive, if need be.
