notice of/to#

Pronunciation
/ˈnoʊtɪs ʌv/, /ˈnoʊtɪs tu/ · NOH-tiss uv, NOH-tiss too · weak /ˈnoʊtɪs əv/ → /ˈnoʊtɪs ə/, /ˈnoʊtɪs tə/
Русский
юр. уведомление о чём-л., направленное кому-л.; также предупреждение об увольнении
Type
noun + preposition
Register
legal in the of/to frames — pleadings, leases, employment letters; neutral in take notice of and give notice
Complement
of: the subject of the notice, as a noun phrase or gerund (notice of intending to appeal is stiff; notice of intent to appeal is the idiom). to: the recipient. that: a bare that-clause (notice that the lease will not be renewed). to + bare infinitive in fixed legal phrases: notice to quit, notice to appear. Never notice about in the legal sense.
Pattern
notice of [default / appeal / termination / a meeting] · notice to [a party] · notice that [clause] · give / serve / receive / waive notice · on notice · without notice · thirty days' notice · take notice of [noun phrase]
Contrast
Of names the subject and to names the recipient, and a single document carries both: notice to the tenant of default. ✗ notice about the default is an error in a legal document, though it is fine in conversation. Watch the possessive in periods of time: thirty days' notice takes an apostrophe, and ✗ thirty days notice is a common slip. Note on notice, which means warned, and the everyday verb notice, which takes a bare object and no preposition at all: she noticed the leak, ✗ she noticed of the leak.

A formal communication of a fact, given to someone entitled to know it.

  1. The landlord served a notice of termination on the first of the month.
  2. Notice to the guarantor has to be sent by certified mail.
  3. She filed a notice of appeal one day inside the deadline.
  4. The lease requires sixty days' notice before any rent increase.
  5. The company put the vendor on notice that the account was under review.
  6. The board gave notice of the special meeting by email, which the bylaws do not allow.
See also

O#

objection to#

Pronunciation
/əbˈdʒɛkʃən tu/ · ub-JEK-shun too · weak /əbˈdʒɛkʃən tə/
Русский
возражение против — рус. «против», не «к»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (an objection to being recorded) — never a to-infinitive (✗ an objection to be recorded, ✗ no objection to sign). The noun also takes a bare that-clause in formal and legal use: an objection that the question assumes facts not in evidence.
Pattern
an objection to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · have no objection to [verb-ing] · raise / overrule / sustain an objection · an objection on the grounds that [clause] · objections from [party]
Contrast
A prime -ing trap, and the test is easy: a noun fits the slot (an objection to the fee), so the to is a preposition and any verb after it takes -ing. ✗ I have no objection to pay should be to paying; ✗ her objection to be filmed should be to being filmed. ✗ an objection against is an error, and an objection about is heard but marked. The verb keeps the same preposition and the same trap: object to being asked, ✗ object to be asked, and ✗ object something is not English.

A stated reason for disapproving of something.

  1. Her only objection to the plan was the cost of the sidewalk.
  2. I have no objection to working weekends if the schedule is posted in advance.
  3. The neighbors raised an objection to rezoning the corner lot.
  4. Objections to being recorded should be noted for the minutes.
  5. The judge overruled the objection and let the answer stand.
  6. There were three written objections to the easement.

obligation to/under#

Pronunciation
/ˌɑbləˈɡeɪʃən tu/, /ˌɑbləˈɡeɪʃən ˈʌndɚ/ · ob-luh-GAY-shun too, ob-luh-GAY-shun UN-der · weak /ˌɑbləˈɡeɪʃən tə/; under has no weak form
Русский
обязательство перед кем-л. / по договору, обязанность
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; the under frame is legal drafting
Complement
to: either a person or body owed (an obligation to his creditors) or a bare infinitive naming the act required (an obligation to disclose). Never a gerund after to (✗ an obligation to disclosing). under: the agreement, statute, or section the duty arises beneath. of: whose obligation it is. Never a that-clause.
Pattern
an obligation to [person] · an obligation to [verb] · obligations under [a contract / the statute] · meet / discharge / breach an obligation · be under an obligation to [verb] · without obligation
Contrast
The two to's, again, and the noun-slot test settles them: an obligation to the bank takes a noun, an obligation to repay takes a bare verb. Against duty to/of: obligations are typically created by agreement and duties by role, which is why a contract lists obligations and a fiduciary owes duties — but the grammar is identical and the two words are swapped freely in ordinary writing. ✗ an obligation for paying is an error; the obligation of paying is possible but stilted. Note the American sales phrase with no obligation to buy, where the infinitive frame is doing the work.

Something a person is bound to do, and the instrument or relationship that binds them.

  1. The company has no obligation to renew the contract and has said so.
  2. Her obligations under the settlement end when the last payment clears.
  3. He feels an obligation to the people who hired him in 1994.
  4. There's a statutory obligation to report a spill within twenty-four hours.
  5. You can cancel in thirty days with no obligation to buy anything.
  6. Nothing in the letter creates an obligation under the master agreement.
See also
duty to/of, responsibility for, liability for/to, commitment to, entitlement to, hereunder

obstacle to#

Pronunciation
/ˈɑbstəkəl tu/ · OB-stuh-kul too · weak /ˈɑbstəkəl tə/
Русский
препятствие для чего-л., помеха чему-л. — рус. «для», не «к»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (an obstacle to finishing on time) — never a to-infinitive (✗ an obstacle to finish on time) and never a that-clause; a wh-clause is fine after the preposition (an obstacle to what the district is trying to do). A noun fits the slot (an obstacle to peace), which is what makes the to a preposition and forces the -ing.
Pattern
an obstacle to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · the main / chief / biggest obstacle to · pose / present / remove / clear an obstacle · an obstacle in [someone's] path
Variants
a barrier to · an impediment to · a hurdle to — all three take the same preposition and the same -ing rule
Contrast
To is the only preposition here — ✗ an obstacle of finishing, ✗ an obstacle against the deal, ✗ an obstacle for finishing. The near-synonyms behave identically, so the rule generalizes: a barrier to entry, an impediment to recovery, and ✗ a barrier to enter the market is the same mistake one word over. The one legitimate for frame takes a person and names who is blocked rather than what is blocked: an obstacle for first-time buyers. Compare difficulty with/in, where American English drops the preposition before the gerund altogether (difficulty finishing), and limit to/on, which marks a ceiling rather than a blockage.

Something that stands in the way of progress.

  1. The biggest obstacle to getting the permit is the parking study.
  2. Financing turned out to be the only real obstacle to the sale.
  3. Her age was treated as an obstacle to promotion, which was the whole problem.
  4. Striking that one clause removed every obstacle to signing.
  5. The court called the waiting period a substantial obstacle to access.
  6. Language was never an obstacle between the two of them.
See also
difficulty with/in, problem with, limit to/on, solution to, to

onus on#

Pronunciation
/ˈoʊnəs ɑn/ · OH-nuss on · weak /ˈoʊnəs ən/
Русский
бремя доказывания на ком-л. — the onus is on you: доказывать придётся тебе
Type
noun + preposition
Register
formal to neutral; common in editorials, court opinions, and sports commentary, rare in casual speech
Complement
on: the person or body who bears the burden, as a noun phrase, usually followed by a to-infinitive saying what they must do (the onus is on the state to prove it). of: the burden itself (the onus of proof). Never a gerund (✗ the onus on proving it) and never a clause.
Pattern
the onus is on [someone] · the onus is on [someone] to [verb] · put / place / shift the onus on [someone] · the onus of [proof / responsibility] · the onus falls on [someone]
Contrast
The noun is nearly frozen in one frame. The onus is on you is idiomatic; ✗ you have the onus and ✗ the onus to you are not. Onus of names the burden (the onus of proof) and onus on names the bearer, and mixing them gives ✗ the onus on proof. The word is a mass noun with no plural: ✗ two onuses. It is Latin for "burden," and it lives in English only in this frame, alongside the fuller legal phrase burden of proof — see responsibility for for the ordinary word.

The burden of doing or proving something, and who carries it.

  1. The onus is on the state to prove every element beyond a reasonable doubt.
  2. Putting the onus on tenants to document mold is how the ordinance fails.
  3. After the fumble the onus was on the defense.
  4. The onus of proof shifts once the plaintiff makes out a prima facie case.
  5. The onus is on the seller to disclose a known defect in this state.
  6. Nobody wants the onus and everybody has an opinion.
See also
responsibility for, duty to/of, obligation to/under, liability for/to, presumption of/against, on

opinion on/of/about#

Pronunciation
/əˈpɪnjən ɑn/ · uh-PIN-yun on · weak /əˈpɪnjən ən/, /əˈpɪnjən əv/ → /ə/, /əˈpɪnjən ˈbaʊt/
Русский
мнение о чём-л.; of — ещё и оценка чьих-л. качеств
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
on / about: an issue — noun phrase, gerund (an opinion on raising the levy), or wh-clause (an opinion on what to do). of: a person or thing being evaluated (a low opinion of him). The noun also takes a bare that-clause: the opinion that the levy is regressive. Never a to-infinitive.
Pattern
an opinion on / about [issue] · an opinion of [person / thing] · the opinion that [clause] · in my opinion · a matter of opinion · a second opinion · public opinion on [issue]
Variants
opinion on — an issue formally at stake, and the form in a legal opinion on · opinion about — the conversational default · opinion of — an estimate of worth, not a position
Contrast
On is a position; of is a verdict on someone's quality. Her opinion on the mayor's budget is what she thinks should happen; her opinion of the mayor is what she thinks of him as a person — and they can be opposite. ✗ my opinion for the plan is an error. The fixed hedge is in my opinion, never ✗ according to my opinion, ✗ by my opinion, or ✗ to my opinion — all three are frequent transfer errors. According to takes a source other than yourself (according to the auditor), which is why according to me sounds absurd.

A view held about an issue, or an estimate of someone's worth.

  1. What's your opinion on the proposed levy?
  2. He has an unaccountably high opinion of his own singing.
  3. In my opinion, the second draft was the better one.
  4. The county attorney issued a formal opinion on the setback rule.
  5. Public opinion about the closure shifted after the first winter.
  6. Whether it counts as art is a matter of opinion.
See also
view on/of, attitude to/toward, comment on, advice on/about, on

opposition to#

Pronunciation
/ˌɑpəˈzɪʃən tu/ · op-uh-ZISH-un too · weak /ˌɑpəˈzɪʃən tə/
Русский
сопротивление чему-л., противодействие; несогласие с планом или человеком
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (opposition to raising taxes) — never a to-infinitive (✗ opposition to raise taxes) and never a clause. In the political sense the noun stands alone with the definite article: the opposition.
Pattern
opposition to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · in opposition to [noun phrase] · face / meet / drop opposition to [noun phrase] · organized opposition to [noun phrase] · the opposition (a party)
Contrast
The -ing rule is the entire difficulty: ✗ opposition to raise the levy is the error, opposition to raising the levy the fix, and the noun test settles it (opposition to the levy). ✗ opposition against is a redundancy, though it appears in translated prose. In opposition to is a fixed connector meaning "contrary to" and behaves like a complex preposition — see as opposed to for its close relative. The verb takes a direct object with no preposition: they opposed the levy, ✗ they opposed to the levy — an error made especially often by people who know the noun well.

Resistance to a plan, policy, or person.

  1. Opposition to the pipeline came from both ends of the county.
  2. There was surprisingly little opposition to closing the pool.
  3. She resigned in opposition to the new testing policy.
  4. The bill faces organized opposition to raising the assessment cap.
  5. Opposition to the war cost him his seat in 1968.
  6. The measure passed with only token opposition.
See also
objection to, resistance to, argument with/about, disagreement with/about, contrast to/with, opposed to, to

order for#

Pronunciation
/ˈɔrdɚ fɔr/ · OR-der for · weak /ˈɔrdɚ fɚ/
Русский
заказ на что-л.; отдельно — приказ (order to) и порядок, последовательность
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for: goods or services requested (an order for fifty units). to + bare infinitive: a command (orders to evacuate). from: the customer or the source. of: a quantity or a sequence (an order of magnitude, the order of the names). ✗ an order for to evacuate.
Pattern
an order for [goods] · an order to [verb] · an order from [customer / authority] · place / fill / cancel an order for [noun phrase] · in order to [verb] · out of order · an order of [food]
Contrast
Commercial for, military to, and a pile of fixed phrases. An order for ammunition is a purchase; an order to withdraw is a command; ✗ an order for withdrawing mixes them. In order to is a purpose connector, not this noun at all (see in order to), and out of order means broken or procedurally improper. Note the American restaurant use: an order of fries is a portion, where British English says a portion of chips. The verb takes a person plus an infinitive (ordered him to leave, ✗ ordered that he leaves — the subjunctive is ordered that he leave) and a bare object for goods (ordered fifty units).

A request for goods, a command, or a sequence.

  1. We placed an order for two hundred replacement filters in March.
  2. The crew received orders to evacuate the platform by dawn.
  3. That's the third order from the same address this week.
  4. He put in an order of onion rings for the table.
  5. The names are listed in order of seniority.
  6. The second elevator has been out of order since Thanksgiving.
See also
request for, demand for, permission for, rule against/for, for

party to#

Pronunciation
/ˈpɑrti tu/ · PAR-tee too · weak /ˈpɑrti tə/
Русский
юр. сторона договора, участник разбирательства или сговора
Type
noun + preposition
Register
legal — contracts, pleadings, treaties; also ordinary formal English in be a party to something disreputable
Complement
a noun phrase naming an agreement, a proceeding, or a scheme — never a gerund (✗ a party to concealing it is heard but ✗ in edited prose; write a party to the concealment), never an infinitive, and never a clause.
Pattern
a party to [a contract / a suit / a treaty] · the parties to [an agreement] · be / become / remain a party to [noun phrase] · a third party · an interested party · a party of six (the restaurant sense)
Contrast
✗ A party of the contract is the error; the noun takes to and only to, and it appears in the opening line of nearly every American agreement (the parties to this Agreement). Keep the other nouns separate: a party of six is a table at a restaurant, a party is the event, and the party is a political organization — none of them takes to. Note the moral use, which is common in journalism: he was a party to the cover-up means he took part in it, and it does not require a signature. Against signatory to: every signatory is a party, but a party may be bound without signing.

Someone who takes part in an agreement, a lawsuit, or a scheme.

  1. Both parties to the lease have to initial every change.
  2. The United States is not a party to that convention.
  3. She was never a party to the suit and was deposed anyway.
  4. He refuses to be a party to anything that ends up in front of a grand jury.
  5. The parties to the settlement agreed to keep the amount confidential.
  6. A third party to the negotiation leaked the draft on Friday.
See also
signatory to, beneficiary of/under, claim against/for/to, exhibit to/schedule to/annex to, agreement with/on, to

penalty for#

Pronunciation
/ˈpɛnəlti fɔr/ · PEN-ul-tee for · weak /ˈpɛnəlti fɚ/
Русский
наказание, штраф за что-л. — рус. «за», не «для»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral; legal in under penalty of, and a fixed term in American sports
Complement
for: the offense, as a noun phrase or gerund (a penalty for filing late) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a penalty to file late) and never a clause. of: the punishment itself (a penalty of $500, the penalty of death). against / on: the team or player, in sports.
Pattern
a penalty for [an offense / verb-ing] · a penalty of [an amount] · under penalty of perjury · impose / assess / waive / pay a penalty · a penalty against [a team] · the death penalty · a prepayment penalty
Contrast
For the offense, of the punishment, and they stack: a penalty of $500 for filing late. ✗ a penalty for $500 reverses them and is a common slip in letters to the IRS. Against damages for: a penalty punishes, damages compensate — a contract clause that sets a number too high to be compensation is void as a penalty and enforceable as liquidated damages, which is the whole fight. In American football the penalty is against a team and on a player, and the penalty for the foul is the yardage. Note under penalty of perjury, the fixed formula at the bottom of a federal form.

The punishment attached to an offense, and the offense that draws it.

  1. The penalty for filing after April 15 compounds monthly.
  2. There's a stiff penalty for early withdrawal from that certificate.
  3. The court imposed a penalty of $2,500 and a year of probation.
  4. I declare under penalty of perjury that the foregoing is true.
  5. A holding penalty against the left tackle wiped out the touchdown.
  6. The loan has no prepayment penalty, which is worth reading the note for.
See also
damages for, tax on, sanction against/sanctions on, prohibition on/against, liability for/to, for

permission for#

Pronunciation
/pɚˈmɪʃən fɔr/ · per-MISH-un for · weak /pɚˈmɪʃən fɚ/
Русский
разрешение на что-л. — рус. «на»; ср. permission to + инф. — разрешение сделать
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for: a noun phrase naming the thing permitted (permission for the field trip). The same noun takes a to-infinitive with no preposition: permission to leave early. ✗ permission of doing and ✗ permission for to leave are errors. from names the person granting it.
Pattern
permission for [noun phrase] · permission to [verb] · permission from [authority] · with the permission of [person] · ask for / grant / deny permission · without permission
Contrast
For takes a thing; to takes an act; of appears only in with the permission of. Permission for the recording is consent for a thing; permission to record is consent for an act — and ✗ permission of recording is wrong in both senses. ✗ permission of the teacher is an error where permission from the teacher is correct, though with the permission of the teacher is the fixed formal frame. The noun is uncountable: ✗ a permission, ✗ permissions in the general sense, although the plural is standard in computing (file permissions).

Consent to do something.

  1. We never got permission for the second dumpster.
  2. She has permission to leave early on Thursdays.
  3. You'll need written permission from the property owner.
  4. The photograph is reproduced with the permission of the estate.
  5. He took the truck without permission.
  6. Permission for the field trip has to be back by Friday.

plan for#

Pronunciation
/plæn fɔr/ · plan for · weak /plæn fɚ/
Русский
план чего-л., планы на что-л. — намеченный курс действий
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for: a noun phrase naming the occasion or object (a plan for the weekend, plans for the building). The same noun takes a to-infinitive with no preposition: a plan to sell the building. of appears in the fixed a plan of action, a plan of attack. ✗ a plan for to sell and ✗ a plan about are errors.
Pattern
a plan for [occasion / object] · a plan to [verb] · plans for [the future] · a plan of action · make plans for [noun phrase] · according to plan · a floor plan of [building]
Contrast
For takes a thing or a time; to takes an act. A plan for the renovation names what the plan concerns; a plan to renovate names what will be done — and ✗ a plan for renovate mixes them. Note that plans for the weekend means arrangements, while a plan of the weekend would be a diagram. The verb takes both an infinitive and on + -ing, and the on frame is the one that trips people: we plan to leave at six and we're planning on leaving at six are both American, but ✗ we plan on leave and ✗ we plan to leaving are not.

An intended course of action, and what it concerns.

  1. Do you have any plans for the long weekend?
  2. The county has a plan for the old rail corridor that nobody likes.
  3. There's a plan to sell the building and lease back two floors.
  4. We need a plan of action before Tuesday's hearing.
  5. Everything went according to plan until the fourth inning.
  6. Her plans for retirement changed twice in one year.

pleasure in/of#

Pronunciation
/ˈplɛʒɚ ɪn/, /ˈplɛʒɚ ʌv/ · PLEZH-er in, PLEZH-er uv · weak /ˈplɛʒɚ ən/, /ˈplɛʒɚ əv/ → /ˈplɛʒɚ ə/
Русский
удовольствие от чего-л., радость от — рус. «от», не «в»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
in + gerund: what someone enjoys doing (takes pleasure in correcting people) — never a to-infinitive after in (✗ pleasure in to correct). of + noun phrase: the thing enjoyed, in fixed formal frames (the pleasure of your company). The noun separately takes a to-infinitive with no preposition in the greeting formula it's a pleasure to meet you.
Pattern
take pleasure in [verb-ing] · the pleasure of [noun phrase] · it is a pleasure to [verb] · with pleasure · at [someone's] pleasure · for pleasure
Contrast
Three frames that cannot be swapped. Pleasure in doing it is the enjoyment taken from an activity; the pleasure of your company is a formal noun frame from invitations; a pleasure to meet you is the greeting formula. ✗ the pleasure to your company and ✗ I take pleasure of correcting you are both errors. Note the register: take pleasure in usually carries a faint disapproval in American English (he takes pleasure in saying no), while it's a pleasure to is warm. The archaic at your pleasure (whenever you like) survives in legal and ceremonial writing.

Enjoyment, and the thing enjoyed.

  1. He takes obvious pleasure in telling people the office is closed.
  2. The pleasure of your company is requested at seven o'clock.
  3. It's a pleasure to finally put a face to the emails.
  4. She reads maps for pleasure, which explains a lot about our vacations.
  5. There's real pleasure in watching somebody who is very good at a small job.
  6. I'd help you with pleasure, but I'll be in Denver.
See also

point in/of#

Pronunciation
/pɔɪnt ɪn/, /pɔɪnt ʌv/ · poynt in, poynt uv · weak /pɔɪnt ən/, /pɔɪnt əv/ → /pɔɪnt ə/
Русский
смысл делать что-л. (no point in waiting); также пункт, тезис в рассуждении
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
in / of + gerund: the purpose or use of an activity (no point in arguing, what's the point of arguing) — never a to-infinitive (✗ no point to argue). In American English the preposition can be dropped entirely: no point arguing. about: the topic of a remark (a point about the deadline).
Pattern
there's no point in [verb-ing] · what's the point of [verb-ing] · no point [verb-ing] · a point about [topic] · the point of [thing] · make / take / miss the point · a point in someone's favor
Variants
no point in doing, no point of doing, and bare no point doing — all three are current American English; in is the most usual in writing, and dropping the preposition is the most usual in speech
Contrast
In or of plus -ing,
and never an infinitive
✗ there's no point to complain, ✗ what's the point to wait. Write no point in complaining, what's the point of waiting. The separate frame the point of the exercise takes a noun and is fine. Do not confuse this with the verb point to (indicate: the evidence points to arson) or point at (gesture toward), which are unrelated. A point about introduces the subject of a remark, and a point in her favor is a fixed idiom of credit.

The purpose or use of doing something, or an item in an argument.

  1. There's no point in arguing with the parking division.
  2. What's the point of a smoke detector nobody replaces the battery in?
  3. No point waiting — the last bus left at nine.
  4. She made a good point about the cost of the surveys.
  5. The whole point of the exercise was to see who would speak up.
  6. That he showed up at all is a point in his favor.
See also

possession of#

Pronunciation
/pəˈzɛʃən ʌv/ · puh-ZESH-un uv · weak /pəˈzɛʃən əv/ → /pəˈzɛʃən ə/
Русский
обладание чем-л., фактическое владение — независимо от права собственности
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral; a term of art in criminal law and property law, and a fixed term in American sports
Complement
a noun phrase — never a gerund (✗ possession of holding the deed), never an infinitive, and never a clause.
Pattern
possession of [a thing] · in possession of [a thing] · take / gain / lose possession of [a thing] · in the possession of [a person] · vacant possession · possession of a controlled substance · possession is nine-tenths of the law
Contrast
In possession of and in the possession of
run in opposite directions
she was in possession of the file says she had it; the file was in the possession of her lawyer says who had it — the article flips the perspective, and getting it backward produces a sentence that means the file owned the lawyer. ✗ possession over is an error, though control over/of does take over. Note the criminal-law senses that stack with no extra preposition: constructive possession, possession with intent to distribute. In basketball and football, possession takes of the ball and stands alone in first possession.

Physical control of a thing, whether or not you own it.

  1. He was arrested for possession of a firearm by a convicted felon.
  2. The buyer takes possession of the house on the fifteenth.
  3. The originals are in the possession of the county recorder.
  4. She was in possession of a key she was not supposed to have.
  5. The team lost possession three times in the fourth quarter.
  6. Possession of the deed is not the same thing as title to the land.

possibility of#

Pronunciation
/ˌpɑsəˈbɪləti ʌv/ · pos-uh-BIL-uh-tee uv · weak /ˌpɑsəˈbɪləti əv/ → /ˌpɑsəˈbɪləti ə/
Русский
возможность чего-л., вероятность того, что нечто случится
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: noun phrase or gerund (the possibility of rain, the possibility of losing the contract) — never a to-infinitive (✗ the possibility to rain, ✗ the possibility to lose the contract). The noun also takes a bare that-clause: the possibility that the seal failed.
Pattern
the possibility of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · the possibility that [clause] · raise / rule out / consider the possibility of [verb-ing] · there is a possibility of [noun phrase]
Contrast
Of + -ing or that + clause, never an infinitive. ✗ the possibility to work from home is one of the most common errors in professional English written by Germans, Dutch speakers, and Scandinavians, whose languages allow it. Write the possibility of working from home — or, more naturally, the option to work from home or the chance to work from home, since chance of and option do take the infinitive. ✗ possibility for is likewise wrong. The adjective possible takes an infinitive freely (it is possible to work from home), which is exactly why the noun feels as though it should.

Something that might happen or might be done.

  1. We can't rule out the possibility of a second recall.
  2. There's a real possibility of frost before the end of the month.
  3. She raised the possibility that the invoice had been paid twice.
  4. The possibility of working four ten-hour days is being studied.
  5. No one considered the possibility of the pump simply being unplugged.
  6. The possibility that the seal had failed was dismissed too quickly.
See also

precedent for#

Pronunciation
/ˈprɛsədənt fɔr/ · PRESS-uh-dunt for · weak /ˈprɛsədənt fɚ/ · the noun is PRESS-, not pruh-SEED-; the adjective precedent /prɪˈsidənt/ is a different word
Русский
прецедент для — более ранний случай, оправдывающий повторение
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; a term of art in American law, where precedent is what binds a lower court
Complement
for: what the precedent would support, as a noun phrase or gerund (a precedent for reopening the case) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a precedent to reopen the case) and never a clause. in: the jurisdiction or body of law (no precedent in this circuit).
Pattern
a precedent for [an act / verb-ing] · precedent in [a court / a field] · set / establish / follow / overrule / break with precedent · without precedent · binding / persuasive precedent · legal precedent (uncountable)
Contrast
The countability rule is split by sense. A precedent is countable when it means one earlier case or act (that would set a bad precedent); precedent is uncountable when it means the whole body of decided law (there is no precedent for this in the circuit). Watch the near-homographs: precedent the noun is PRESS-uh-dunt, and president is PREZ-uh-dunt — a spelling error that survives spellcheck. ✗ a precedent of is an error in this frame, and the precedent of Marbury means the case itself rather than what it supports.

An earlier case or act that justifies doing the same thing again.

  1. There's no precedent for letting a board member vote by proxy.
  2. Approving this variance sets a precedent for every corner lot on the street.
  3. The ruling breaks with forty years of precedent in the Ninth Circuit.
  4. She found one precedent for reopening a closed claim after five years.
  5. The appointment was without precedent and drew two lawsuits.
  6. Lower courts are bound by precedent whether or not they like it.
See also
example of, basis for, grounds for, judgment against/for, citation to, for

preference for#

Pronunciation
/ˈprɛfrəns fɔr/ · PREF-runss for · weak /ˈprɛfrəns fɚ/
Русский
предпочтение чего-л., склонность к одному в ущерб другому
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (a preference for working alone) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a preference to work alone). The fixed comparative frame is in preference to + noun phrase.
Pattern
a preference for [noun phrase / verb-ing] · in preference to [noun phrase] · have / show / express a preference for [noun phrase] · give preference to [group] · a stated preference
Contrast
For names what you like better; to appears only in in preference to and give preference to. ✗ a preference to the older model is an error where a preference for the older model and chose the older model in preference to the new one are both correct. ✗ a preference of is wrong. The related verb takes yet another construction and is the real trap: prefer X to Y (✗ prefer X than Y), prefer to do it (infinitive), and prefer doing X to doing Y — so the verb takes to where the noun takes for.

A liking for one thing over another.

  1. She has a marked preference for older buildings with bad wiring.
  2. The survey found a clear preference for evening appointments.
  3. He chose the county road in preference to the interstate.
  4. The scholarship gives preference to applicants from the district.
  5. My preference for working alone is not a comment on the team.
  6. State your preference for morning or afternoon on the form.
See also
fondness for, taste for/in, desire for, alternative to, prefer to, for

premium over/on#

Pronunciation
/ˈprimiəm ˈoʊvɚ/, /ˈprimiəm ɑn/ · PREE-mee-um OH-ver, PREE-mee-um on · weak /ˈprimiəm ən/; over has no weak form
Русский
over — надбавка сверх базовой цены; on — страховой взнос либо особая ценность
Type
noun + preposition
Register
finance and insurance; the place a premium on frame is general formal English
Complement
over / above / to: the baseline the price exceeds, as a noun phrase. on: the policy or the thing valued, as a noun phrase or gerund (a premium on getting there first). for: what the extra buys. Never an infinitive (✗ a premium to arrive first) and never a clause.
Pattern
a premium over [a price / the market] · a premium on [a policy] · place / put a premium on [noun phrase / verb-ing] · pay a premium for [something] · at a premium · a premium of [a percentage]
Contrast
Over measures an excess, on names either a policy or a prized quality, and the two never swap: ✗ a premium on the market price is an error for the takeover sense, and ✗ the premium over my policy is an error for the insurance sense. At a premium means scarce and expensive and takes no complement (parking is at a premium). Note that premium also works as an adjective with no preposition at all — premium gas, a premium account — which is where American advertising has taken the word.

An amount paid above a baseline, the payment on an insurance policy, or unusual value placed on something.

1. The extra above a baseline price. The complement is over, above, or to, and the baseline is a market price, a book value, or a previous offer. The percentage comes in with of: a premium of 34 percent over Friday's close.

  1. The offer is a thirty percent premium over Friday's closing price.
  2. Buyers are paying a premium over asking on anything with a garage.

2. The periodic payment on an insurance policy. The complement is on, and the noun is countable.

  1. The premium on the flood policy doubled after the map was redrawn.
  2. He let the premium lapse and found out in the worst possible week.

3. High value placed on a quality. Only in the frame place / put a premium on, which takes a noun phrase or a gerund.

  1. The new manager puts a premium on showing up prepared.
  2. A hospital that places a premium on speed will lose something else.
See also
price of/for, return on, interest in, tax on, importance of/to, over

presumption of/against#

Pronunciation
/prɪˈzʌmpʃən ʌv/, /prɪˈzʌmpʃən əˈɡɛnst/ · prih-ZUMP-shun uv, prih-ZUMP-shun uh-GENST · weak /prɪˈzʌmpʃən əv/ → /prɪˈzʌmpʃən ə/; against has no weak form
Русский
юр. презумпция чего-л. / против чего-л. — исходное допущение до опровержения
Type
noun + preposition
Register
legal and formal — jury instructions, statutory interpretation, editorial writing
Complement
of: what is assumed true until disproved, as a noun phrase (a presumption of innocence, a presumption of validity). against: what the law starts out disfavoring (a presumption against retroactivity). in favor of: the mirror image. The noun also takes a bare that-clause (the presumption that the letter was received). Never a to-infinitive.
Pattern
a presumption of [innocence / regularity / death] · a presumption against [a result] · a presumption in favor of [a party] · the presumption that [clause] · rebut / overcome / raise a presumption · on the presumption that [clause] · a rebuttable presumption
Contrast
Of is what you start by believing, against is what you start by doubting — and the same statute can carry both (a presumption of validity for the agency's rule, a presumption against applying it retroactively). ✗ a presumption to is an error. Do not confuse the word with presumptuousness: the presumption of innocence is a legal default, but the presumption of the man means his gall — a second, older sense that takes of as well and has ruined more than one sentence. Against idea of/for: an assumption is anything taken for granted; a presumption is one the law or the argument requires until rebutted.

A starting position that holds until someone proves otherwise.

  1. The presumption of innocence is not a rule of evidence but the whole frame.
  2. There's a strong presumption against reading a statute to apply retroactively.
  3. Courts apply a presumption of regularity to what an agency says it did.
  4. The presumption that a mailed notice was received can be rebutted by testimony.
  5. A seven-year absence raises a presumption of death in most states.
  6. The board acted on the presumption that the grant would be renewed.
See also
onus on, probability of/likelihood of, evidence of/for, doubt about, judgment against/for, against

price of/for#

Pronunciation
/praɪs ʌv/, /praɪs fɔr/ · pryce uv, pryce for · weak /praɪs əv/ → /praɪs ə/; /praɪs fɚ/
Русский
цена чего-л. (на рынке) / за что-л. (в конкретной сделке)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: what the item costs in general (the price of gas). for: an amount named in a particular transaction (a fair price for the truck). A gerund follows of in the figurative sense (the price of being right). Never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
the price of [commodity] · a price for [item in a deal] · at a price of [amount] · the price of [verb-ing] (figurative) · put a price on [noun phrase] · at any price · price per [unit]
Contrast
Of is the market; for is the deal. The price of the truck is what trucks like that cost; a good price for the truck is what you and I agreed on. Neither is ungrammatical, but the price for gas is up reads oddly in a market report and what's the price of your truck reads oddly in a negotiation. Put a price on is fixed — ✗ put a price to. The figurative sense takes of + -ing: the price of being right about this is what it costs you socially.

What something costs, in a market or in a particular deal.

  1. The price of diesel went up eleven cents in a week.
  2. He got a decent price for the truck considering the mileage.
  3. The price of being early to a bad idea is that everyone remembers.
  4. The units sold at a price of $340,000 apiece.
  5. You can't put a price on a Saturday morning with nothing scheduled.
  6. The price for the same procedure varies by a factor of six across the state.
See also
supply of, demand for, tax on, importance of/to, of

probability of/likelihood of#

Pronunciation
/ˌprɑbəˈbɪləti ʌv/, /ˈlaɪklihʊd ʌv/ · prob-uh-BIL-uh-tee uv, LYKE-lee-hood uv · weak /ˌprɑbəˈbɪləti əv/ → /ˌprɑbəˈbɪləti ə/
Русский
вероятность чего-л. — насколько правдоподобно, что нечто случится
Type
noun + preposition
Register
probability is technical in statistics and neutral elsewhere; likelihood is neutral
Complement
of: a noun phrase or gerund (the probability of rain, the likelihood of being audited) — never a to-infinitive (✗ the probability to rain, ✗ the likelihood to be audited). Both also take a bare that-clause: the probability that the well is dry. Never probability for.
Pattern
the probability of [an event / verb-ing] · the likelihood of [an event / verb-ing] · the probability that [clause] · in all likelihood · a high / low / vanishing probability · increase / reduce the likelihood of [noun phrase]
Contrast
The adjectives take the infinitive and the nouns refuse it — this is the single highest-yield fact in the entry. It is likely to rain is correct; ✗ the likelihood to rain is not. Convert with of + -ing or with that: the likelihood of rain, the likelihood that it will rain. Against chance of, which splits by meaning: a chance of winning is a probability and a chance to win is an opportunity, so chance is the one noun in this family that does take an infinitive — with a different sense. ✗ the probability for rain and ✗ likelihood on are errors.

How probable something is.

  1. The probability of a hundred-year flood in any given year is one percent.
  2. The likelihood of being audited on a return like this is very small.
  3. What's the probability that both machines fail in the same week?
  4. Insulating the attic reduces the likelihood of ice dams.
  5. In all likelihood the case settles before anyone sees a jury.
  6. He put the probability of success at about one in three and was optimistic.
See also
chance of, possibility of, risk of, incidence of/prevalence of, presumption of/against, of

problem with#

Pronunciation
/ˈprɑbləm wɪθ/ · PROB-lum with · weak /ˈprɑbləm wəθ/
Русский
проблема с чем-л., неполадка, затруднение — рус. «с», не «в»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
with: the thing or person that is causing trouble (a problem with the wiring). of: the nature of the problem, formal (the problem of homelessness). in: a domain (a problem in logic). Gerunds follow with (a problem with getting parts); never a to-infinitive (✗ a problem to get parts) and never a that-clause in ordinary use.
Pattern
a problem with [thing / person] · the problem of [social issue] · a problem in [field] · have a problem with [noun phrase / verb-ing] · no problem · the problem is that [clause] · solve / cause a problem
Contrast
With for a specific fault, of for a category. A problem with the wiring is a fault to fix; the problem of rural broadband is a policy area. ✗ a problem about is an error, and ✗ a problem for the wiring means something else (whom it inconveniences). Note that the problem is that... takes a clause with no preposition — ✗ the problem is because... — the same fault treated under reason for. I have a problem with that is American idiom for disagreement, not for a malfunction.

A fault, difficulty, or matter needing a solution.

  1. There's a problem with the wiring in the back bedroom.
  2. The problem of deferred maintenance is not going away on its own.
  3. I have a problem with being told after the fact.
  4. That's a classic problem in elementary probability.
  5. The problem is that nobody kept the original survey.
  6. Two coats and it's fine — no problem.
See also
trouble with, difficulty with/in, solution to, matter with, with

progress in/on#

Pronunciation
/ˈprɑɡrɛs ɪn/, /ˈprɑɡrɛs ɑn/ · PROG-ress in, PROG-ress on · weak /ˈprɑɡrɛs ən/
Русский
прогресс в чём-л., продвижение в работе над — в англ. неисчисляемо
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
in: a field or general area, and gerunds (progress in reducing waste). on: a specific project or task (progress on the report). toward: a goal. Never a to-infinitive (✗ progress to reduce waste) and never a clause.
Pattern
progress in [field / verb-ing] · progress on [project] · progress toward [goal] · make progress · in progress · the progress of [disease / negotiation]
Variants
progress toward — American, no -s; progress towards is British
Contrast
In for a domain, on for a task, toward for a goal, and of for something unfolding. Progress in cancer research is a field advancing; progress on the grant application is one job moving; progress toward accreditation is a target approached; the progress of the disease is its course. ✗ progress for is an error. The noun is uncountable in this sense: ✗ a progress, ✗ progresses, ✗ many progresses — say some progress, a lot of progress, real progress. Note that progress the verb (pro-GRESS) stresses differently from the noun (PROG-ress).

Forward movement toward a goal or through a task.

  1. There's been real progress in reducing emergency room wait times.
  2. What's the progress on the Fillmore Street repaving?
  3. The district has made almost no progress toward its literacy goals.
  4. Renovation is still in progress on the second floor.
  5. The progress of the negotiations was reported daily and inaccurately.
  6. Two years in, we've made some progress and no money.
See also
improvement in/on, increase in/decrease in, success in/at, delay in, in

prohibition on/against#

Pronunciation
/ˌproʊəˈbɪʃən ɑn/, /ˌproʊəˈbɪʃən əˈɡɛnst/ · proh-uh-BISH-un on, proh-uh-BISH-un uh-GENST · weak /ˌproʊəˈbɪʃən ən/ · note the silent h: proh-uh-, not pro-hib-
Русский
запрет на что-л. — правило, запрещающее какое-л. действие
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — statutes, ordinances, policy writing
Complement
on / against: the act forbidden, as a noun phrase or gerund (a prohibition on selling alcohol before noon) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a prohibition to sell alcohol) and never a clause. of is possible and formal (the prohibition of torture).
Pattern
a prohibition on / against [an act / verb-ing] · the prohibition of [a practice] (formal) · impose / lift / enforce a prohibition · Prohibition (capitalized: the American era, 1920–1933) · a blanket prohibition
Variants
prohibition on and prohibition against are both standard American English and freely interchangeable; on is slightly commoner in ordinances, against in constitutional writing (the prohibition against cruel and unusual punishment)
Contrast
✗ A prohibition to is the error, and it is the direct translation from several European languages. Against moratorium on: a moratorium pauses, a prohibition forbids indefinitely. Against injunction against: an injunction is a court order against named parties, a prohibition is a rule against everyone. Capitalized and bare, Prohibition is the American constitutional experiment of 1920–1933, and it takes no complement at all.

A rule forbidding something.

  1. The ordinance imposes a prohibition on overnight parking from November to April.
  2. The constitutional prohibition against ex post facto laws settled the question.
  3. There's a blanket prohibition on accepting gifts from vendors.
  4. The lease has a prohibition against subletting that nobody enforces.
  5. A prohibition on selling flavored products took effect in three cities at once.
  6. Prohibition closed the brewery in 1920 and the family never reopened it.
See also
moratorium on, embargo on, injunction against, rule against/for, limit to/on, against

proof of#

Pronunciation
/pruf ʌv/ · proof uv · weak /pruf əv/ → /pruf ə/
Русский
доказательство чего-л., подтверждение — то, что устраняет сомнения
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: noun phrase or gerund (proof of having paid) — never a to-infinitive. The noun also takes a bare that-clause: proof that the check cleared. ✗ proof for is not standard in the evidentiary sense; use evidence of/for.
Pattern
proof of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · proof that [clause] · proof of purchase / address / insurance · the burden of proof · beyond proof · a proof of [theorem]
Contrast
Of is the only preposition here. ✗ proof for the theory should be evidence for the theory, because proof in American usage claims certainty where evidence claims support — a difference worth keeping.
The countability rule is split by domain
in ordinary use proof is uncountable, so ✗ two proofs that he paid and ✗ he offered several proofs are errors — the fixed compounds a proof of purchase and a proof of address are the one place an article turns up. In mathematics and printing the noun is fully countable (two proofs of the theorem, the galley proofs). Note the fixed proof of purchase, proof of address, and proof of insurance, which take no article.

Something that establishes a fact beyond doubt.

  1. Bring proof of residence and a photo ID.
  2. There's no proof of forced entry, only a broken latch.
  3. The receipt is your proof of purchase.
  4. He wanted proof that the check had actually cleared.
  5. The burden of proof is on the party seeking the injunction.
  6. The paper gives two independent proofs of the same result.
See also
evidence of/for, guarantee of, example of, grounds for, of

protection from/against#

Pronunciation
/prəˈtɛkʃən frʌm/, /prəˈtɛkʃən əˈɡɛnst/ · pruh-TEK-shun frum, pruh-TEK-shun uh-GENST · weak /prəˈtɛkʃən frəm/, /prəˈtɛkʃən əˈɡɛnst/
Русский
защита от чего-л. — рус. «от», не «против»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (protection against being sued) — never a to-infinitive (✗ protection to be sued) and never a clause. Protection for names who is protected; protection of names what is being kept safe.
Pattern
protection from / against [threat] · protection for [person protected] · the protection of [thing kept safe] · under the protection of [authority] · offer / provide protection against [threat]
Variants
protection from and protection against — both standard American English; from is commoner with immediate physical threats (protection from the rain), against with risks and adversaries (protection against fraud)
Contrast
Four prepositions, and only two of them are interchangeable. Protection from/against the wind names the threat; protection for the seedlings names who benefits; the protection of the wetlands names what is being conserved — and ✗ protection of the wind would mean the wind is what you are safeguarding. ✗ protection to is an error. The verb takes both prepositions of threat and a bare object: protect the seedlings from frost, ✗ protect from the seedlings.

A defense against a threat, and who or what it covers.

  1. The awning offers some protection from the afternoon sun.
  2. The policy provides protection against flood damage up to $250,000.
  3. There's almost no legal protection for tenants in month-to-month leases.
  4. The statute concerns the protection of archaeological sites.
  5. Witnesses were placed under the protection of the marshals.
  6. Two coats of sealer give real protection against freeze-thaw cracking.
See also
danger of/to, threat to, risk of, cure for, protect from, protect against, against

R#

ratio of ... to#

Pronunciation
/ˈreɪʃoʊ ʌv/, /ˈreɪʃoʊ tu/ · RAY-shoh uv, RAY-shoh too · weak /ˈreɪʃoʊ əv/ → /ˈreɪʃoʊ ə/, /ˈreɪʃoʊ tə/ · American RAY-shoh; the older RAY-shee-oh /ˈreɪʃioʊ/ is heard in mathematics classrooms
Русский
соотношение чего-л. к чему-л., пропорция двух величин
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral; technical in finance, statistics, and engineering
Complement
two noun phrases, the first after of and the second after to — never a gerund, never an infinitive, and never a clause. The numbers themselves may fill the of ... to frame directly (a ratio of three to one).
Pattern
the ratio of X to Y · a ratio of [number] to [number] · in a ratio of 2:1 · a high / low ratio · the student-teacher ratio (compound, no preposition) · the debt-to-equity ratio
Contrast
Both halves are obligatory in the full frame: ✗ the ratio of doctors and patients is an error — and cannot replace to — and ✗ the ratio between doctors to patients mixes two frames. A ratio between X and Y does exist and is looser; the edited form for a computed figure is of ... to. Note that the compound forms hyphenate and drop the prepositions entirely (the debt-to-equity ratio, a 3-to-1 ratio, the student-teacher ratio), which is why writers forget the prepositions when they need them. Against in proportion to: a proportion is a part of a whole, a ratio compares two quantities that need not belong to the same whole.

A comparison of two quantities, expressed as one number against another.

  1. The ratio of applicants to seats was almost nine to one.
  2. Mix it in a ratio of three parts sand to one part cement.
  3. The county's ratio of nurses to residents is the lowest in the state.
  4. Their debt-to-equity ratio got worse every quarter for two years.
  5. The ratio of signal to noise in the recording is hopeless.
  6. A student-teacher ratio of thirty-two to one is not a class, it is a crowd.
See also
in proportion to, correlation with/between, incidence of/prevalence of, share in/of, disparity between/in, to

rationale for#

Pronunciation
/ˌræʃəˈnæl fɔr/ · rash-uh-NAL for · weak /ˌræʃəˈnæl fɚ/ · four syllables, final stress; not RASH-un-ul, which is the adjective rational
Русский
обоснование чего-л., логика решения или практики
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — memos, opinions, grant applications
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (the rationale for closing the branch) — never a to-infinitive (✗ the rationale to close the branch) and never a clause; a that-clause needs a verb between (the rationale was that the branch lost money).
Pattern
the rationale for [a decision / verb-ing] · the rationale behind [a decision] · offer / explain / question the rationale · the stated rationale · the underlying rationale
Variants
rationale for and rationale behind are both standard American English; behind suggests a reason that was not stated openly, for the reason given. Rationale of occurs in older and stiffer prose and is now uncommon.
Contrast
Rationale is a noun and rational is an adjective, and they are not pronounced alike: rash-uh-NAL against RASH-un-ul. ✗ the rational for the decision is a spelling error that spellcheck will not catch. Against reason for: a reason can be a bare fact, a rationale is a worked-out justification — which is why an agency publishes the rationale for a rule and a person gives a reason for being late. Against justification for: a justification argues that the act was right, a rationale only explains the thinking.

The reasoning offered for a decision or a practice.

  1. Nobody has explained the rationale for moving the deadline twice.
  2. The rationale behind the merger was cost savings that never showed up.
  3. The memo gives a two-sentence rationale for closing the branch.
  4. What's the rationale for charging residents and not commuters?
  5. The stated rationale and the actual reason were not the same thing.
  6. Her rationale for keeping the old system is that the new one has never been tested.
See also
reason for, justification for, basis for, grounds for, excuse for, for

reaction to#

Pronunciation
/riˈækʃən tu/ · ree-AK-shun too · weak /riˈækʃən tə/
Русский
реакция на — рус. «на», не «к»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (her reaction to being passed over) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a reaction to be passed over) and never a clause. Reaction against names a movement in opposition to something.
Pattern
a reaction to [event / substance] · a reaction against [trend / movement] · an allergic reaction to [substance] · in reaction to [event] · provoke / gauge a reaction
Contrast
To for a response, against for a backlash. A reaction to modernism is what people thought of it; a reaction against modernism is a movement rejecting it. ✗ a reaction on the news and ✗ a reaction about the news are errors, produced by borrowing from comment on and opinion on/of/about. The -ing rule holds: a noun fits the slot (a reaction to the news), so ✗ a reaction to be told should be to being told. The verb keeps the preposition — react to the news, ✗ react the news — and the chemistry sense adds with (sodium reacts with water).

A response to something that has happened.

  1. Her reaction to the news was to reread the letter twice.
  2. The doctor thinks it's an allergic reaction to the new detergent.
  3. The whole style was a reaction against the ornament of the previous decade.
  4. Public reaction to the closure was louder than anyone at the district expected.
  5. In reaction to the audit, three managers took early retirement.
  6. His reaction to being corrected in public was predictably awful.
See also
response to, reply to, attitude to/toward, opposition to, react to, to

reason for#

Pronunciation
/ˈrizən fɔr/ · REE-zun for · weak /ˈrizən fɚ/
Русский
причина чего-л., основание для — reason why и reason that тоже норма
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for: noun phrase or gerund (the reason for the delay, the reason for leaving early) — never a to-infinitive after for (✗ the reason for to leave). The same noun takes a bare to-infinitive meaning a justification (a reason to leave), a bare why-clause (the reason why she left), a bare that-clause (the reason that she left), and a zero clause (the reason she left).
Pattern
the reason for [noun phrase / verb-ing] · a reason to [verb] · the reason why [clause] · the reason that [clause] · the reason ø [clause] · the reason is that [clause] · for some reason · by reason of [noun phrase] · within reason
Variants
the reason why, the reason that, and the bare the reason + clause are all standard American English; why is the most emphatic, the bare form the most conversational
Contrast
The four good frames and the one bad one. The reason for the delay takes a noun; the reason why / that / ø she left takes a clause; a reason to leave takes an infinitive and means a justification for a future act rather than an explanation of a past one. The error is the reason is because: because already means "for the reason that," so ✗ the reason is because he was sick says it twice — write the reason is that he was sick, or just he was sick. Two further notes: ✗ the reason of the delay is not American English, and the old objection to the reason why as redundant is a nineteenth-century invention with no basis in the record; it has been in continuous good use since Middle English.

The explanation for something, or a justification for doing it.

  1. Nobody has given us a reason for the delay.
  2. The reason for leaving early was that the last ferry is at six.
  3. That's the reason why she never puts anything in writing.
  4. The reason that the deed was never filed is buried in a 1948 probate file.
  5. There's every reason to believe the numbers will be revised.
  6. The reason the road floods is that the culvert was sized for 1962.
  7. The reason is that the county never accepted the dedication.
See also
cause of, grounds for, excuse for, point in/of, for

recipe for#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɛsəpi fɔr/ · RESS-uh-pee for · weak /ˈrɛsəpi fɚ/ · three syllables, stress on the first; not /rɪˈsaɪp/
Русский
рецепт чего-л. — кулинарный и переносный («верный способ получить»)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral; the figurative frame is idiomatic and very common in American speech and journalism
Complement
a noun phrase or gerund (a recipe for getting yourself fired) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a recipe to get yourself fired) and never a clause.
Pattern
a recipe for [a dish] · a recipe for [disaster / trouble / success] · follow / double / ruin a recipe · a recipe calls for [an ingredient] · my grandmother's recipe
Contrast
✗ A recipe of is an error in both the literal and the figurative sense. The figurative frame is nearly fixed and overwhelmingly negative: a recipe for disaster, for trouble, for failure — a recipe for success exists but sounds like a motivational poster. Note the recipe calls for, an idiom where the recipe itself does the asking, and note that receipt was once the same word and now means only a proof of purchase: ✗ a receipt for banana bread is a nineteenth-century usage that will read as a mistake.

A set of instructions for making a dish — or, figuratively, a reliable way of producing a result.

  1. She still uses her grandmother's recipe for cornbread and refuses to write it down.
  2. Cutting the inspection budget is a recipe for disaster.
  3. Do you have a good recipe for pickled okra?
  4. Three managers with the same authority is a recipe for nobody deciding anything.
  5. The recipe for the sauce calls for twice as much butter as anyone admits.
  6. Borrowing against a house to pay a credit card is a recipe for losing both.
See also
cure for, solution to, way of/to, key to, plan for, for

recourse to/against#

Pronunciation
/ˈrikɔrs tu/, /rɪˈkɔrs əˈɡɛnst/ · REE-korss too, rih-KORSS uh-GENST · weak /ˈrikɔrs tə/ · American stress varies: REE-korss and rih-KORSS are both current
Русский
юр. обращение к средству защиты; право регресса к должнику
Type
noun + preposition
Register
formal; legal and financial in the against and without frames
Complement
to: the remedy, authority, or means turned to, as a noun phrase or gerund (recourse to litigating every claim) — never a to-infinitive (✗ recourse to sue). against: the person or asset a creditor can proceed against. Never a clause.
Pattern
recourse to [the courts / force / an authority] · recourse against [a borrower / a guarantor] · have / seek / be left with no recourse · without recourse · a non-recourse loan · as a last recourse
Contrast
To is what you resort to, against is whom you can pursue. Recourse to arbitration is a path; recourse against the guarantor is a target — and the American mortgage term non-recourse means the lender may take the house and nothing else. ✗ recourse for is an error. The bare no recourse is the everyday form (the tenant has no recourse), and without recourse written above a signature on a negotiable instrument disclaims liability. The verb is resort to, not ✗ recourse to — recourse is a noun only in modern American usage.

The option of turning to some remedy or authority, and whom a creditor may pursue.

  1. Tenants in that building have almost no recourse when the heat fails.
  2. The clause forces recourse to arbitration before anyone files suit.
  3. The lender has no recourse against her personally, only against the property.
  4. He was left with no recourse but to sell at a loss.
  5. Any recourse to the courts under this section waives the informal process.
  6. She endorsed the note without recourse and slept better for it.
See also
remedy for/against, claim against/for/to, liability for/to, lien on/against, resort to, to

reference to#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɛfrəns tu/ · REF-runss too · weak /ˈrɛfrəns tə/
Русский
упоминание чего-л., ссылка на; также рекомендация или рекомендатель
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (a reference to having served overseas) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a reference to serve overseas) and never a that-clause. In the employment sense, a reference for takes the person recommended.
Pattern
a reference to [thing mentioned] · a reference for [job applicant] · with reference to [noun phrase] · in reference to [noun phrase] · make reference to [noun phrase] · a frame of reference · for future reference
Variants
with reference to and in reference to — both formal correspondence openers; American editors cut both to about or regarding wherever possible. See with reference to
Contrast
To for a mention, for for a recommendation. A reference to the 1996 agreement is a mention of it; a reference for the applicant is a letter vouching for her — ✗ a reference of the applicant is an error. The -ing rule holds because a noun fits the slot: a reference to being overseas, ✗ a reference to be overseas. Note that the phrasal opener with reference to is a preposition in its own right, treated in the topic and reference file, and that without reference to means "disregarding."

A mention of something, or a person who vouches for you.

  1. The contract makes no reference to the easement at all.
  2. There's a passing reference to the fire in the county history.
  3. She listed two former supervisors as references.
  4. I'm writing with reference to your letter of March 4.
  5. The map was drawn without reference to the actual property lines.
  6. Keep the confirmation number for future reference.
See also
comment on, report on/of, connection with/to/between, example of, to

relationship with/between#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈleɪʃənʃɪp wɪθ/ · rih-LAY-shun-ship with · weak /rəˈleɪʃənʃəp wəθ/, /rəˈleɪʃənʃəp bəˈtwin/
Русский
отношения с кем-л.; связь, зависимость между величинами
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
with: the other party (a relationship with my father). between: both parties or variables named from outside (the relationship between diet and blood pressure). to: a formal or structural connection (her relationship to the deceased). Never a gerund after with, never an infinitive, never a clause.
Pattern
a relationship with [person / institution] · the relationship between X and Y · [someone's] relationship to [person / thing] · have / build / end a relationship with [person] · in a relationship
Contrast
With is personal, between is external, to is structural. Her relationship with the victim describes how they got along; her relationship to the victim asks how she is related — sister, employer, neighbor — and a police report that confuses the two produces a very different document. Between requires two named items: ✗ the relationship between diet is incomplete. ✗ a relationship of is an error except in formal compounds (a relationship of trust). In American speech in a relationship with no complement means romantically involved.

A connection between people, or between variables.

  1. She has a complicated relationship with her father's second family.
  2. The study found no relationship between the additive and the symptoms.
  3. What is your relationship to the deceased?
  4. The firm has had a relationship with that supplier since 1978.
  5. The relationship between rainfall and yield is not linear.
  6. They've been in a relationship for about two years.
See also
connection with/to/between, link between/to, marriage to, similarity to/between, between

relevance to#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɛləvəns tu/ · REL-uh-vunss too · weak /ˈrɛləvəns tə/
Русский
отношение к делу, значимость для рассматриваемого вопроса
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; a working term in courtrooms, where an objection is simply "Relevance."
Complement
a noun phrase or gerund (its relevance to choosing a site) — never a to-infinitive (✗ relevance to choose a site) and never a clause.
Pattern
relevance to [a question / a case / a decision] · the relevance of X to Y · have / lack / question the relevance of [noun phrase] · of no relevance · contemporary relevance
Variants
relevance and relevancy are both current in American English; relevance is the ordinary form and relevancy survives in evidence law (the relevancy of the exhibit)
Contrast
✗ Relevance on and ✗ relevance for are errors — the noun takes to, matching the adjective relevant to. Against bearing on, which means the same thing with a different preposition: no relevance to the case and no bearing on the case are equivalent, and swapping the prepositions produces two errors at once. The full frame needs both halves when the source is named: the relevance of his tax returns to the zoning question.

The quality of having a bearing on the matter under discussion.

  1. I don't see the relevance to anything we're deciding tonight.
  2. Counsel objected to the relevance of the question to the charged conduct.
  3. The 1974 study has real relevance to how the pipe was specified.
  4. His military service is of no relevance to the permit application.
  5. The committee questioned the survey's relevance to rural districts.
  6. Half the exhibit was excluded for lack of relevance.
See also
bearing on, applicability to, importance of/to, connection with/to/between, relevant to, to

remedy for/against#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɛmədi fɔr/, /ˈrɛmədi əˈɡɛnst/ · REM-uh-dee for, REM-uh-dee uh-GENST · weak /ˈrɛmədi fɚ/; against has no weak form
Русский
средство от чего-л.; юр. способ правовой защиты
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral in the everyday sense; legal in the against frame and in remedies at law
Complement
for: the problem or ailment cured, as a noun phrase or gerund (a remedy for overcharging customers) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a remedy to overcharge). against: the party a legal remedy runs against. Never a clause.
Pattern
a remedy for [a problem / an illness] · a remedy against [a defendant] · seek / have / exhaust a remedy · remedies at law and in equity · a home remedy · the exclusive remedy
Contrast
For the trouble, against the person. A remedy for the leak fixes the leak; a remedy against the seller is what you can make him do. ✗ a remedy to the problem is an error, and it is the commonest one here, borrowed from solution to — which does take to. Note the American contract phrase sole and exclusive remedy, which limits everything a party can ask for, and exhaust your remedies, which means using every available process before going to court. In everyday speech, a home remedy takes for an ailment.

Something that cures a problem, and, in law, the relief a court can give.

  1. There's no quick remedy for a foundation that has already shifted.
  2. The statute gives homeowners a remedy against the original builder.
  3. Repair or replacement is the buyer's sole and exclusive remedy under this warranty.
  4. Her grandmother's remedy for a cough involved more whiskey than honey.
  5. Plaintiffs must exhaust administrative remedies before filing.
  6. Firing the vendor is not a remedy for a process that was broken to begin with.
See also
cure for, solution to, recourse to/against, damages for, injunction against, for

reply to#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈplaɪ tu/ · rih-PLY too · weak /rəˈplaɪ tə/
Русский
ответ на письмо или реплику — рус. «на», не «к»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun naming the message answered (a reply to my email). Never a gerund, never an infinitive, never a that-clause in ordinary use. In reply to is the fixed formal opener.
Pattern
a reply to [message / person] · in reply to [noun phrase] · a reply from [sender] · await / receive a reply · reply all
Contrast
The preposition survives the shift from verb to noun, which is what makes answer to treacherous. Reply keeps to in both classes: she replied to my email and a reply to my email — so ✗ she replied my email is an error. Answer drops it in the verb and keeps it in the noun: she answered my email, the answer to my email. Learn the two as a pair. ✗ a reply of my email and ✗ in reply of are errors, and reply takes no direct object at all: ✗ he replied me.

An answer to a message or a statement.

  1. I never got a reply to the second letter.
  2. In reply to your inquiry, the fee schedule is attached.
  3. His reply to the accusation was four words long.
  4. There's been no reply from the county in six weeks.
  5. She hit reply all by mistake and copied the whole board.
  6. A prompt reply to the notice will keep the account open.
See also
answer to, response to, letter to/from, request for, to

report on/of#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈpɔrt ɑn/, /rɪˈpɔrt ʌv/ · rih-PORT on, rih-PORT uv · weak /rəˈpɔrt ən/, /rəˈpɔrt əv/ → /rəˈpɔrt ə/
Русский
доклад, отчёт о чём-л.; также сообщение о происшествии
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
on: a formal study of a subject — noun phrase or gerund (a report on closing the plant). of: an account that something occurred (reports of gunfire). to: the recipient or the supervisor. The noun also takes a bare that-clause: reports that the levee had failed. Never a to-infinitive.
Pattern
a report on [subject] · reports of [occurrence] · a report to [authority] · a report from [source] · reports that [clause] · file / issue / suppress a report on [noun phrase]
Contrast
On is a study; of is a rumor or an account. A report on gunfire is a document analyzing it; reports of gunfire are people saying they heard it — a newsroom distinction with real consequences. A report about the incident is acceptable but vaguer than either. The verb has three frames worth separating: report on the hearing (write it up), report the theft (notify authorities, no preposition), and report to the regional director (be supervised by) — so ✗ report about the theft and ✗ report to the theft are both wrong.

A formal account of a subject, or word that something happened.

  1. The consultant's report on deferred maintenance runs to 300 pages.
  2. There were reports of looting on the second night.
  3. She files a monthly report to the state board.
  4. Reports that the levee had failed turned out to be premature.
  5. The report from the field office contradicted the press release.
  6. He was asked to write a short report on whether the software is worth renewing.
See also
information about/on, research on/into, comment on, evidence of/for, on

reputation for#

Pronunciation
/ˌrɛpjəˈteɪʃən fɔr/ · rep-yuh-TAY-shun for · weak /ˌrɛpjəˈteɪʃən fɚ/
Русский
репутация, слава человека, известного каким-л. качеством
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for: a quality or an activity, as a noun phrase or gerund (a reputation for showing up unprepared) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a reputation to show up late) and never a clause. as: a role or identity (a reputation as a fixer). with / among: whose opinion it is.
Pattern
a reputation for [a quality / verb-ing] · a reputation as [a role] · a reputation with / among [a group] · build / earn / ruin / live down a reputation · by reputation · a reputation to protect
Contrast
For takes a quality, as
takes a role
a reputation for candor and a reputation as a straight shooter say nearly the same thing with different grammar, and ✗ a reputation as candor is an error. A reputation of being difficult is heard everywhere and is still the unedited form — write a reputation for being difficult. Note the one place to does appear, and it is not a complement: a reputation to protect is a reduced relative clause (a reputation that she has to protect), not the noun's preposition.

What people generally believe about someone, named by the quality involved.

  1. The shop has a reputation for honest estimates and a two-month wait.
  2. She earned a reputation for saying the thing everyone else was avoiding.
  3. His reputation as a closer did not survive the third quarter.
  4. The firm has a bad reputation with subcontractors and a good one with banks.
  5. A reputation for paying late follows a company from job to job.
  6. He knew the judge only by reputation and it did not prepare him.
See also

request for#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈkwɛst fɔr/ · rih-KWEST for · weak /rəˈkwɛst fɚ/
Русский
просьба о чём-л., запрос на — глагол request идёт без предлога
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
for: a noun phrase naming what is asked for (a request for records). The same noun takes a to-infinitive with no preposition (a request to speak) and a that-clause with the subjunctive (a request that the fee be waived). ✗ a request for to speak mixes them; ✗ a request of records is an error.
Pattern
a request for [thing asked for] · a request to [verb] · a request that [clause with subjunctive] · a request from [asker] · at the request of [person] · on request · deny / grant / submit a request
Contrast
A textbook noun-versus-verb shift. The verb request is bare transitive — she requested the records, ✗ she requested for the records — while the noun requires for. The error is reinforced by ask, which does take for (ask for the records), so the pair ask for / request Ø has to be memorized together. Note the American subjunctive after the noun: a request that the fee be waived, not ✗ that the fee is waived. On request and at the request of are fixed and take no article.

A formal asking for something.

  1. The agency denied our request for the inspection records.
  2. She submitted a written request to address the council.
  3. The audit was conducted at the request of the state treasurer.
  4. There's a standing request for volunteers on Saturday mornings.
  5. A request that the penalty be waived is pending.
  6. Copies of the plat are available on request.
See also
demand for, order for, invitation to, permission for, for

research on/into#

Pronunciation
/ˈrisɜrtʃ ɑn/, /ˈrisɜrtʃ ˈɪntu/ · REE-surch on, REE-surch IN-too · weak /ˈrisɜrtʃ ən/, /ˈrisɜrtʃ ˈɪntə/
Русский
исследование чего-л. — в англ. неисчисляемо (✗ researches)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (research into reducing infection rates), or a wh-clause (research on how children learn to read) — never a to-infinitive (✗ research to reduce infection rates, unless to introduces a purpose clause) and never a that-clause.
Pattern
research on / into [subject] · research in [field] · do / conduct / publish research on [subject] · a body of research on [subject] · research shows that [clause]
Variants
research on — the American default · research into — implies an inquiry with a question to answer, slightly more investigative · research in — a discipline (research in organic chemistry)
Contrast
The countability rule is the trap and it costs credibility. Research is uncountable in American English: ✗ a research, ✗ researches, ✗ many researches, ✗ I did a research on it. Say a piece of research, a study, some research, a body of research. The verb is likewise a problem: ✗ make a research — the verb is do research or the transitive research the subject, with no preposition. ✗ research about is an error, though it is spreading in student writing.

Systematic investigation of a subject.

  1. There's surprisingly little research on long-term outcomes for this procedure.
  2. Her research into nineteenth-century land grants took eleven years.
  3. He does research in soil microbiology at the extension station.
  4. The research on early reading intervention is fairly settled.
  5. The lab published two pieces of research on the same sample.
  6. Research into why the runs failed is still ongoing.
See also
report on/of, evidence of/for, knowledge of/about, insight into, into

resistance to#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈzɪstəns tu/ · rih-ZISS-tunss too · weak /rəˈzɪstəns tə/
Русский
сопротивление чему-л., неприятие; также устойчивость к воздействию
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (resistance to being told what to do) — never a to-infinitive (✗ resistance to change the schedule) and never a that-clause; a wh-clause is possible (resistance to what the board proposed). A noun fits the slot (resistance to change), so the to is a preposition.
Pattern
resistance to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · meet / encounter / face / overcome resistance · stiff / fierce / passive resistance · resistance from [party] · the path of least resistance
Variants
resistance against — normal only with an occupying force (resistance against the occupation) · resistant to — the adjective, same preposition and same -ing rule
Contrast
To names what is being resisted; from names who is doing the resisting — resistance to the merger against resistance from the field offices, and the two often appear together. ✗ resistance of the merger is an error, though the resistance of the copper wire is the unrelated electrical noun. The -ing trap is live here because change is the commonest complement and a verb is always lurking behind it: ✗ resistance to change the schedule has to be resistance to changing the schedule. The verb is bare — they resisted the merger, ✗ they resisted to the merger — one more verb-bare / noun-prepositional pair of the kind collected at the end of this file.

Refusal to accept something, or the act of opposing it.

  1. There was fierce resistance to raising the assessment.
  2. The plan met more resistance than anyone in the mayor's office expected.
  3. Resistance to antibiotics is now the central problem on the ward.
  4. She takes the path of least resistance every single time.
  5. Most of the resistance came from the two oldest departments.
  6. His resistance to being photographed made the profile nearly impossible.
See also
opposition to, objection to, reaction to, protection from/against, to

respect for#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈspɛkt fɔr/ · rih-SPEKT for · weak /rəˈspɛkt fɚ/
Русский
уважение к кому-л. — рус. «к»; ср. with respect to — «что касается»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
