for: a person, institution, or principle — noun phrase or gerund (respect for doing the unglamorous work) — never a to-infinitive (✗ respect to do the work). The fixed phrases with respect to and in respect of are prepositions in their own right and mean "regarding," not "esteem."
Pattern
respect for [person / rule] · out of respect for [person] · with respect to [topic] · in this respect · pay one's respects to [person] · have / earn / lose respect for [person]
Variants
with respect to — American · in respect of — British and Commonwealth; see in respect of
Contrast
Two unrelated words share the spelling. Respect for the judge is esteem; with respect to the judge's ruling means "concerning" it, and the two must not be blended — ✗ respect to the judge in the esteem sense is an error. In this respect means "in this particular way" and takes no complement. The plural respects appears only in pay one's respects to (attend a funeral or call formally). ✗ respect of a person is not American English. The verb takes a bare object: she respects the process, ✗ she respects for the process.

Esteem for a person or principle, and the unrelated "regarding" phrases.

  1. He has enormous respect for anyone who does that job for twenty years.
  2. Respect for the process is what keeps the hearings short.
  3. Out of respect for the family, the names were withheld.
  4. With respect to the second question, the county has no position.
  5. The two proposals differ in one important respect.
  6. Half the town came to pay their respects.
See also
sympathy for/with, opinion on/of/about, attention to, confidence in, for

response to#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈspɑns tu/ · rih-SPONSS too · weak /rəˈspɑns tə/
Русский
ответ на, отклик на — рус. «на», не «к»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (a response to being asked twice) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a response to be asked) and never a that-clause. In response to is the fixed connector.
Pattern
a response to [message / event] · in response to [noun phrase] · a response from [party] · elicit / draw a response · the response of [group] · an emergency response to [event]
Contrast
To for what is being responded to, from for who responds. ✗ a response of the letter is an error, though the response of the neighborhood correctly names whose response it is. Distinguish from reply to: a reply is specifically an answer to a message, while a response can be any reaction, which is why emergency response and immune response work and ✗ emergency reply does not. The verb keeps the preposition: respond to the letter, ✗ respond the letter.

An answer or reaction to something.

  1. The department's response to the complaint took four months.
  2. In response to the outcry, the board reopened public comment.
  3. We got no response from the insurer until we hired a lawyer.
  4. Her response to being asked twice was to leave the room.
  5. The community response to the fundraiser was overwhelming.
  6. Emergency response times in the north county average eleven minutes.
See also
reply to, reaction to, answer to, request for, to

responsibility for#

Pronunciation
/rɪˌspɑnsəˈbɪləti fɔr/ · rih-spon-suh-BIL-uh-tee for · weak /rəˌspɑnsəˈbɪləti fɚ/
Русский
ответственность за что-л. — рус. «за», не «для»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for: a noun phrase or gerund naming what one is answerable for (responsibility for closing up) — never a to-infinitive after for (✗ responsibility for close up). The noun separately takes a to-infinitive with no preposition (a responsibility to report it) and to + a person (a responsibility to the shareholders).
Pattern
responsibility for [thing / verb-ing] · a responsibility to [person] · a responsibility to [verb] · take / accept / deny responsibility for [noun phrase] · claim responsibility for [attack] · on one's own responsibility
Contrast
For is what you answer for; to is who you answer to — responsibility for the accounts against responsibility to the board. Both can appear at once: a responsibility to the board for the accounts. The third frame, a responsibility to report it, is an infinitive, not the same to: the test is that a noun cannot replace it. ✗ responsibility of the accounts is an error, though the responsibility of the treasurer correctly names whose duty it is. Note claim responsibility for, the fixed news idiom.

The duty to answer for something, and to whom.

  1. She has responsibility for everything east of the river.
  2. Nobody wanted to take responsibility for the missing key.
  3. The board has a fiduciary responsibility to the members.
  4. You have a responsibility to report it within twenty-four hours.
  5. No group has claimed responsibility for the vandalism.
  6. Responsibility for closing up rotates weekly.
See also
authority on/over, control over/of, apology for, need for, responsible for, for

result of#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈzʌlt ʌv/ · rih-ZULT uv · weak /rəˈzʌlt əv/ → /rəˈzʌlt ə/
Русский
результат, следствие чего-л. — рус. родительный
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: noun phrase or gerund (the result of cutting the maintenance budget) — never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause. from appears with the verb, not the noun: the damage resulted from the leak.
Pattern
the result of [cause] · as a result of [noun phrase] · as a result (no object) · the results of [test / election] · with the result that [clause] · without result
Contrast
Of is the only preposition for the noun: ✗ the result from the leak, ✗ the result to the leak. The verb is where from and in live, and they point in opposite directions: X results from Y means Y caused X, while X results in Y means X caused Y — reversing them inverts the sentence. As a result of is a fixed causal connector treated at as a result of; bare as a result takes no object and works as a sentence adverb. Compare consequence of, which implies something unwelcome where result is neutral.

What something produced.

  1. The backlog is a direct result of two unfilled positions.
  2. As a result of the storm, the county offices closed for three days.
  3. The results of the soil test came back on Friday.
  4. Higher rents are the predictable result of building nothing for a decade.
  5. The line was down for six hours; as a result, the whole shift was lost.
  6. Two years of appeals, and without result.
See also
consequence of, effect on, cause of, impact on, result in, result from, of

return on#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈtɜrn ɑn/ · rih-TURN on · weak /rɪˈtɜrn ən/
Русский
доходность чего-л., отдача от вложений — return on investment
Type
noun + preposition
Register
financial and neutral; return on investment is ordinary American business speech
Complement
on: what was invested, as a noun phrase — never a gerund (✗ a return on buying the building; write a return on the building), never an infinitive, and never a clause. of: the rate or the amount (a return of nine percent), or the giving back of a thing (the return of the deposit). to: a place or a state, in the ordinary movement sense.
Pattern
a return on [investment / capital / equity / assets] · a return of [a percentage] · return on investment (ROI) · the return of [a thing / a person] · a return to [a place / normal] · in return for [something] · a tax return (compound)
Contrast
On names what the money was in, of names how much came back, and the two stack exactly as in tax on: a return of nine percent on capital. ✗ a return of the investment means the principal came back, not that it earned anything — a distinction traders make out loud ("I'm less worried about the return on my money than the return of my money"). Keep the homographs apart: a tax return is a form, the return of the deposit is a refund, a return to the office is a movement, and only return on is the yield.

The gain produced by an investment, named by what was invested in.

  1. The return on the endowment was four percent after fees.
  2. Nobody could show a return on the eleven million the city spent downtown.
  3. They want a return of at least eight percent on invested capital.
  4. The return on equity looks good until you notice the leverage.
  5. He filed the tax return in October and the refund came in March.
  6. In return for the easement, the utility repaved the whole driveway.
See also
premium over/on, interest in, benefit of/to, tax on, share in/of, in return for

right to/of/over#

Pronunciation
/raɪt tu/, /raɪt ʌv/, /raɪt ˈoʊvɚ/ · ryte too, ryte uv, ryte OH-ver · weak /raɪt tə/, /raɪt əv/ → /raɪt ə/; over has no weak form
Русский
право на что-л., право сделать что-л.; over — право на чужое имущество
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; the central noun of American constitutional and property law
Complement
to: either a noun phrase (the right to counsel, a right to privacy) or a bare infinitive (the right to remain silent) — both are standard and the to is a different word in each. Never a gerund after to (✗ the right to remaining silent). of: whose right it is (the right of the people), or a fixed compound (the right of way). over: property or persons controlled (rights over the mineral estate). in: an interest in an intangible (rights in the copyright).
Pattern
the right to [noun phrase] · the right to [verb] · the right of [a person] to [verb] · rights over [property] · rights in [a work] · have / assert / waive / reserve a right · within one's rights · by rights · the right of way
Contrast
The noun-slot test, one more time. The right to an attorney takes a noun; the right to confront the witnesses takes a bare verb; ✗ the right to confronting is impossible either way. Against entitlement to: a right is held against the world, an entitlement is a specific benefit owed under a program. ✗ the right of vote and ✗ the right for privacy are errors. Note the fixed compounds that drop the preposition entirely — civil rights, water rights, mineral rights, voting rights, the right of way — and the adverbial by rights, which means "properly speaking" and takes no complement.

A legally or morally protected claim to do something or to have something.

  1. You have the right to remain silent, and almost nobody exercises it.
  2. The lease gives the tenant a right of first refusal on the adjoining unit.
  3. The ranch was sold without the mineral rights over the eastern section.
  4. She reserved all rights in the photographs, including reprints.
  5. He was within his rights to refuse entry without a warrant.
  6. The right of way belongs to whoever is already in the intersection.
See also
entitlement to, claim against/for/to, duty to/of, title to, easement over/across, access to

rise in/fall in#

Pronunciation
/raɪz ɪn/, /fɔl ɪn/ · ryze in, fawl in · weak /raɪz ən/, /fɔl ən/
Русский
рост чего-л. / падение чего-л. — рус. родительный, не «в»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
in: the thing that rose or fell (a rise in unemployment). of: the size of the change (a rise of two points). to / from: the endpoints. Gerunds follow in; never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
a rise / fall in [thing measured] · a rise of [amount] · a rise from X to Y · on the rise · the rise and fall of [empire / person] · give rise to [noun phrase]
Variants
a rise in and an increase in are interchangeable in American English; see increase in/decrease in · but a pay increase is a raise in American English and a rise in British English
Contrast
In for what moved, of for how far — the same split as increase in/decrease in, and the same error: ✗ a rise of unemployment should be a rise in unemployment.
The American-British split on wages is worth knowing
she got a raise is American, she got a rise is British and reads as a different word entirely to American ears. Note the fixed give rise to (cause), which is not this pattern at all, and the rise and fall of, which takes of because it is a narrative title frame.

An upward or downward movement in a measured quantity.

  1. There's been a steady rise in emergency room visits since June.
  2. A fall in enrollment of nine percent closed two schools.
  3. The index showed a rise of four points in a single session.
  4. Bicycle theft is on the rise downtown.
  5. The book traces the rise and fall of the local canning industry.
  6. The ambiguity in the deed gave rise to forty years of litigation.
See also
increase in/decrease in, improvement in/on, change in/of, progress in/on, in

risk of#

Pronunciation
/rɪsk ʌv/ · risk uv · weak /rɪsk əv/ → /rɪsk ə/
Русский
риск чего-л., опасность того, что нечто плохое случится
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: noun phrase or gerund naming the bad outcome (the risk of infection, the risk of losing everything) — never a to-infinitive (✗ the risk to lose everything). to: the person or thing endangered (a risk to public health). The noun also takes a bare that-clause: the risk that the seal fails.
Pattern
the risk of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · a risk to [person / thing endangered] · at risk of [verb-ing] · run the risk of [verb-ing] · at the risk of [verb-ing] · take a risk · at your own risk
Contrast
Of is what might go wrong; to is who is exposed — the risk of contamination against a risk to the water supply. ✗ a risk to contamination is the error. Every one of the fixed frames requires -ing: at risk of losing the house, run the risk of being sued, at the risk of stating the obvious — ✗ at risk to lose, ✗ run the risk to be sued. Risk for belongs to American medical writing (a risk factor for stroke, at risk for readmission) and reads as clinical jargon anywhere else, so ✗ the risk for a strike should be the risk of a strike. The verb takes a gerund too: he risked losing his license, ✗ he risked to lose his license.

The chance that something bad happens, and who it happens to.

  1. Every additional day in the hospital raises the risk of infection.
  2. The unsecured tank is a risk to everyone downstream.
  3. Two hundred households are at risk of losing coverage in January.
  4. You run the risk of voiding the warranty if you open it.
  5. At the risk of stating the obvious, somebody has to sign it.
  6. Swim at your own risk; there is no lifeguard after Labor Day.
See also
danger of/to, chance of, possibility of, threat to, of

role in#

Pronunciation
/roʊl ɪn/ · rohl in · weak /roʊl ən/
Русский
роль в чём-л. — участие в событии, организации или сюжете
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
in: the event, process, or organization, as a noun phrase or gerund (a role in raising the money) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a role to raise the money, unless to raise is a purpose clause). as: the identity or part played (her role as treasurer). for: who or what should have a part (a role for the county). on: a team or a cast, in American usage.
Pattern
a role in [an event / verb-ing] · a role as [a part / a position] · a role for [a person / an institution] · a role on [a team / a show] · play / have / take on a role · a leading / supporting / minor role
Contrast
In for what you took part in, as for who you were, and both can appear at once: her role as treasurer in the reorganization. ✗ a role of treasurer is an error, and ✗ a role to play in the merger is fine only because to play is a reduced relative — the noun's own preposition is still in. American usage note: a part in a cast or on a team takes on (his role on the show, her role on the defense), which surprises learners who have been taught that in is the only option. ✗ a role at the decision is an error.

The part someone or something plays in an event, an organization, or a story.

  1. She had no role in the decision and said so in writing.
  2. His role as interim director lasted nineteen months.
  3. Salt plays a bigger role in baking bread than most people think.
  4. There's a role for the state here, and nobody wants to fund it.
  5. He had a small role on a show nobody remembers.
  6. Her role in raising the money was larger than the plaque suggests.
See also
share in/of, contribution to, responsibility for, influence on/over, participate in, in

room for#

Pronunciation
/rum fɔr/ · room for · weak /rum fɚ/
Русский
место для чего-л.; перен. простор для — room for improvement: есть куда расти
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for: a noun phrase naming what fits (room for one more, room for improvement). The same noun takes a to-infinitive with no preposition: room to breathe, room to maneuver. ✗ room for to breathe mixes them; ✗ room of is an error.
Pattern
room for [noun phrase] · room to [verb] · make room for [noun phrase] · no room for [noun phrase] · room for improvement · leave room for [noun phrase]
Contrast
For takes a thing; to takes an act. Room for doubt is space for a thing; room to doubt is freedom to act — both are English, and room for doubting is not. ✗ room for improve is the error to watch. In this pattern room is uncountable and takes no article (there's room for one more, ✗ there's a room for one more, which would mean a spare bedroom). Make room for and room for improvement are fixed.

Available space, literal or figurative.

  1. There's room for one more at the end of the table.
  2. The report shows room for improvement in almost every category.
  3. The lease leaves no room for interpretation.
  4. Move the chair and give him room to work.
  5. We made room for the piano by getting rid of the sofa.
  6. There's not much room for error on a one-lane bridge.
See also
limit to/on, chance of, need for, supply of, for

rule against/for#

Pronunciation
/rul əˈɡɛnst/, /rul fɔr/ · rool uh-GENST, rool for · weak /rul əˈɡɛnst/, /rul fɚ/
Русский
правило, запрещающее / предписывающее что-л.; норма, принцип
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
against: a noun phrase or gerund naming the prohibited act (a rule against smoking) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a rule against smoke). on: the topic a rule governs (the rules on overtime). for: whom or what the rules apply to (the rules for the tournament). The noun also takes a bare that-clause: a rule that all bids be sealed.
Pattern
a rule against [noun phrase / verb-ing] · the rules on [topic] · the rules for [event / group] · a rule that [clause] · as a rule · a rule of thumb · break / bend / enforce a rule
Contrast
Three prepositions, three angles. A rule against parking overnight prohibits; the rules on parking govern the subject; the rules for residents apply to a group. ✗ a rule to park overnight is an error, and ✗ a rule of parking is wrong outside fixed compounds. As a rule means "usually" and takes no complement, while a rule of thumb is a rough guide. The related verb splits by sense: rule on the motion (decide it), rule against the plaintiff (decide adversely), rule out the possibility (eliminate it).

A prohibition, requirement, or governing principle.

  1. There's a rule against parking on the street after two in the morning.
  2. The rules on overtime changed in January and nobody told the crews.
  3. The rules for the tournament fit on one index card.
  4. The board adopted a rule that all bids be sealed.
  5. As a rule, I don't sign anything on the same day I read it.
  6. A good rule of thumb is one cup of water to two of rice.
See also
limit to/on, exception to, order for, permission for, against

S#

sanction against/sanctions on#

Pronunciation
/ˈsæŋkʃən əˈɡɛnst/, /ˈsæŋkʃənz ɑn/ · SANK-shun uh-GENST, SANK-shunz on · weak /ˈsæŋkʃənz ən/; against has no weak form
Русский
санкции против кого-л.; в ед. ч. — наоборот, официальное разрешение
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — foreign-policy reporting, Treasury press releases, and litigation. An American meets the plural in a headline about Iran or Russia and the singular in a judge's order sanctioning a lawyer who missed a deadline.
Complement
on / against: a noun phrase naming a country, a person, a company, or a sector (sanctions on Russian banks, sanctions against the junta). for: the conduct being punished (sanctions for discovery abuse). of: in the approval sense, the body that gives permission (the sanction of Congress). Never a to-infinitive (✗ sanctions to export oil) and never a that-clause. A gerund after on is possible in the ban sense (sanctions on doing business with the regime) but reads as compressed journalese; prohibition on/against is the natural frame for an act.
Pattern
impose / lift / tighten / ease sanctions on [country / entity] · sanctions against [regime / individual] · economic / financial / secondary sanctions · a sanction for [misconduct] · under sanctions · with the sanction of [authority] · give sanction to [an act]
Variants
sanctions on and sanctions against are interchangeable for a target; on is commoner in American trade and Treasury writing, against in political and historical prose · the singular a sanction in the penalty sense is normal only in law (a sanction for late filing); elsewhere the plural is fixed
Contrast
One spelling, two opposite meanings. Plural sanctions on a country are punishments; singular the sanction of the crown is permission — and the verb inherits both, so the court sanctioned the attorney punishes her while the board sanctioned the merger approves it. Context is the only guide, which is why careful American writers reach for penalize or approve when the stakes are high. Against embargo on: an embargo is one total ban on trade, sanctions are a package that may contain one. Against prohibition on/against: a prohibition forbids an act, sanctions punish an actor. Against penalty for: a penalty is a single consequence, sanctions are a policy. ✗ sanctions of Iran and ✗ sanctions to Iran are both errors.

Coercive penalties imposed on a state, a company, or a person — and, in the singular, official permission.

1. Penalties meant to force a change in behavior. Nearly always plural. On and against both name the target; for names the offense, which is the standard frame in American litigation, where a judge imposes sanctions for misconduct.

  1. The Treasury imposed sanctions on three shipping companies the same afternoon.
  2. Sanctions against the generals were lifted as soon as the vote was certified.
  3. Europe has been slower than Washington to tighten sanctions on the energy sector.
  4. The judge threatened sanctions for any further delay in producing the emails.

2. Official approval or authorization. Singular, uncountable, formal and faintly old-fashioned; it survives mainly in with the sanction of and without the sanction of.

  1. The raid went ahead without the sanction of Congress.
  2. No agreement of that size carries weight without the sanction of the states that signed it.
See also
embargo on, prohibition on/against, penalty for, injunction against, tariff on/duty on/levy on, against

search for#

Pronunciation
/sɜrtʃ fɔr/ · surch for · weak /sɜrtʃ fɚ/
Русский
поиск, поиски чего-л.; отдельно — обыск помещения
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for: a noun phrase or gerund naming what is sought (the search for a new director). Never a to-infinitive (✗ the search to find a director) and never a that-clause; a wh-clause is fine (the search for what caused the outage). The fixed phrase is in search of + noun phrase, with no article before search.
Pattern
the search for [thing sought] · in search of [noun phrase] · a search of [place] · call off / widen a search · a search warrant for [premises]
Contrast
For is what you are looking for; of is the place being looked through. A search for the missing hiker is an effort to find him; a search of the cabin is going through it — police reports depend on the difference, and ✗ a search for the cabin would mean the cabin itself is lost.
The verb carries the same split
search for the keys (try to find them) against search the drawer (go through it, no preposition) — so ✗ search for the suspect when you mean frisking him is a real error. Note in search of, never ✗ in search for.

An effort to find something, or an examination of a place.

  1. The search for a new superintendent has been going on since March.
  2. A search of the storage unit turned up the missing ledgers.
  3. They went out in search of firewood and came back with a dog.
  4. The search for the missing hiker was called off at dusk on the fourth day.
  5. Police executed a search warrant at two addresses.
  6. Her search for an affordable rental within the district lasted eight months.
See also
demand for, need for, research on/into, request for, search for, for

share in/of#

Pronunciation
/ʃɛr ɪn/, /ʃɛr ʌv/ · shair in, shair uv · weak /ʃɛr ən/, /ʃɛr əv/ → /ʃɛr ə/
Русский
доля чего-л.; участие, пай в чём-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: a portion of a divisible whole (a share of the profits). in: a stake in an enterprise or a part in an activity, and here a gerund is possible (a share in running the place) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a share in run the place).
Pattern
a share of [divisible whole] · a share in [company / activity] · shares in [a company] · do one's share of [work] · a fair share of [noun phrase] · the lion's share of [noun phrase]
Contrast
Of is a slice; in is a stake. A share of the company's profits is money; a share in the company is ownership — and in American usage stock is shares in Ford or, more commonly, shares of Ford stock. ✗ a share to the profits is an error. Note the idiom had my share of + noun, which means "more than enough" rather than a fair portion (I've had my share of bad landlords), and the lion's share, which despite the fable now means most of it.

A portion of something, or a stake in something.

  1. Each partner gets a share of the net after expenses.
  2. She holds a small share in the brewery her cousin started.
  3. He's had more than his share of bad luck with contractors.
  4. The state's share of the settlement was under two million.
  5. I want a share in deciding how the money is spent.
  6. The lion's share of the grant went to administrative costs.
See also
interest in, supply of, contribution to, responsibility for, in

shot at/crack at#

Pronunciation
/ʃɑt æt/, /kræk æt/ · shot at, krak at · weak /ʃɑt ət/, /kræk ət/; the k of crack is fully pronounced, unlike the silent one in knack
Русский
разг. попытка, шанс попробовать — «дай-ка я попробую»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
informal American English — speech, email, and sports writing. In a brief, a grant application, or a scientific paper, use attempt at or an opportunity to instead.
Complement
a noun phrase (a shot at the title) or a gerund (a crack at fixing it) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a shot to make the team, ✗ a crack to fix it) and never a clause. The string a shot to win the game is grammatical but is a different noun altogether: that is the basketball shot itself, not the chance of winning.
Pattern
have / get / take a shot at [noun phrase / verb-ing] · give someone a shot at [noun phrase] · take a crack at [noun phrase / verb-ing] · a first / second / last crack at [noun phrase] · a fair shot at [noun phrase] · a long shot (no complement)
Variants
take a stab at and have a go at belong to the same set and behave identically, taking a noun phrase or a gerund and refusing the infinitive · British have a bash at is not American
Contrast
Both mean a try, and neither takes an infinitive — which is the whole trap, because the neutral synonyms split the other way: a chance to speak and an opportunity to speak take the bare verb, while a shot at speaking and a crack at speaking demand the -ing form. Between the two, a shot at is a chance at something worth winning and a crack at is a turn at a job, often one somebody else has already botched — give me a crack at the title sounds off, and a shot at the wiring sounds like gunfire. Against attempt at, the same shape one register up, and chance of, which is a probability rather than an opportunity.

An informal chance or turn at something.

  1. The kid finally got a shot at the starting job in August.
  2. Give me a crack at the wiring before you call an electrician.
  3. She wants a shot at running the Denver office.
  4. Everybody in the family has taken a crack at fixing that gate.
  5. He never got a fair shot at the promotion and he knows it.
  6. It's a long shot, but a second crack at the exam costs nothing.
See also
attempt at, chance of, handle on, success in/at, at

signatory to#

Pronunciation
/ˈsɪɡnəˌtɔri tu/ · SIG-nuh-tor-ee too · weak /ˈsɪɡnəˌtɔri tə/
Русский
подписант чего-л. — государство, компания или лицо, подписавшее документ
Type
noun + preposition
Register
formal and legal — treaties, contracts, and diplomatic reporting. An American meets it in a story about the Paris Agreement, on the signature page of a company's credit agreement, and on the bank card that lists who may sign checks.
Complement
a noun phrase naming a document — a treaty, an agreement, a convention, a charter (a signatory to the convention) — never a person (✗ a signatory to the president), never a gerund (✗ a signatory to reducing emissions), and never a to-infinitive (✗ a signatory to reduce emissions). The to is a preposition, and what follows it has to be the thing that was signed.
Pattern
a signatory to [a treaty / an agreement] · the signatories to this Agreement · an original / founding signatory · become / remain a signatory to [noun phrase] · a signatory of [a letter] · a signatory on [an account]
Variants
a signatory of — standard for a single letter or memo, where of is in fact the commoner choice; to is the form for treaties and multiparty agreements · in American banking the fixed phrase is a signatory on the account, not to it
Contrast
To for the instrument you joined, of for the paper you put your name at the bottom of. A signatory to the Geneva Conventions is a state that acceded to them; the signatory of the memo is one person. Against party to: every signatory is a party, but a party can be bound without ever signing — a successor, a guarantor, or a company bound by its own conduct. Note that signing and ratifying are not the same act, which is why a country can be a signatory to a treaty for decades without being bound by it. Keep the word clear of signature (the mark) and signer (the everyday term); ✗ a signature to the treaty is an error.

A state, company, or person that has signed a formal instrument.

  1. The United States is a signatory to the convention but has never ratified it.
  2. All signatories to the agreement must be given thirty days' notice.
  3. Only two of the founding signatories are still in the alliance.
  4. Her mother is the only other signatory on the checking account.
  5. Canada became a signatory to the treaty in 1994 and a party to it two years later.
  6. The letter went out over the names of forty signatories.
See also
party to, beneficiary of/under, successor to, exhibit to/schedule to/annex to, warranty as to, to

similarity to/between#

Pronunciation
/ˌsɪməˈlɛrəti tu/ · sim-uh-LAIR-uh-tee too · weak /ˌsɪməˈlɛrəti tə/, /ˌsɪməˈlɛrəti bəˈtwin/
Русский
сходство с чем-л. / между чем-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
to: a single item resembled (a similarity to the original). between: two or more items joined by and (similarities between the two accounts). in: the respect in which they are alike (a similarity in tone). Never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
a similarity to [item] · similarities between X and Y · a similarity in [respect] · bear a similarity to [noun phrase] · any similarity to [persons living or dead]
Variants
similarity with — occurs and is not wrong, but to is the American default for the one-item frame
Contrast
To for the model, between for the pair, and never ✗ similarity from. The adjective is similar to (similar to the original, ✗ similar with, ✗ similar as), so for once the noun and the adjective agree — which makes this the mirror image of difference between/in, where the noun takes between and the adjective takes from. ✗ a similarity of the original is an error where a similarity to the original is meant; the of frame works only when it names the pair whose likeness is at issue (the similarity of the two accounts), and even there between is the plainer choice. Note the standard film disclaimer: any similarity to actual persons is coincidental.

Resemblance between things, and the respect in which they resemble.

  1. The second signature bears an obvious similarity to the first.
  2. There are unsettling similarities between the two collapses.
  3. The similarity in tone between the anonymous letters was the giveaway.
  4. Any similarity to actual events is, the publisher insists, coincidental.
  5. The similarities between the dialects run deeper than the vocabulary.
  6. Its similarity to a species found only in Chile puzzled everyone.
See also
difference between/in, comparison with/to, contrast to/with, link between/to, similar to, to

solution to#

Pronunciation
/səˈluʃən tu/ · suh-LOO-shun too · weak /səˈluʃən tə/
Русский
решение задачи или проблемы — рус. родительный, не «к»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
to: a problem — noun phrase or gerund (a solution to getting the water out) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a solution to get the water out). for: an intended market or user, in product writing (a solution for small businesses). of: chemistry and mathematics only (a solution of sodium chloride, the solution of the equation).
Pattern
a solution to [problem] · a solution for [user / market] · a solution of [equation / compound] · find / propose a solution to [noun phrase] · part of the solution
Contrast
To for a problem, of only in the laboratory. ✗ a solution of the parking problem is a common error produced by translation from French and German; the English is a solution to the parking problem. A solution of salt is a liquid mixture and the solution of the equation is mathematics — neither has anything to do with problem-solving. A solution for the parking problem is heard in business writing and reads as vendor jargon; keep for for the audience (a solution for hospitals) and to for the problem. The -ing rule applies: a solution to getting the water out, ✗ to get the water out.

An answer to a problem.

  1. There's no cheap solution to the drainage problem on that lot.
  2. The county proposed three solutions to the parking shortage and adopted none.
  3. The vendor sells a scheduling solution for small clinics.
  4. A saturated solution of sodium chloride will not dissolve any more salt.
  5. The only solution to getting the water out is a bigger pump.
  6. If you're not part of the solution, you know the rest.
See also
answer to, key to, obstacle to, problem with, cure for, to

substitute for#

Pronunciation
/ˈsʌbstɪtut fɔr/ · SUB-stih-toot for · weak /ˈsʌbstɪtut fɚ/
Русский
замена чему-л., заменитель — то, что используют вместо
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for: a noun phrase naming what is being replaced (a substitute for butter). Gerunds occur (no substitute for showing up) — never a to-infinitive (✗ no substitute to show up). ✗ a substitute of and ✗ a substitute to are errors.
Pattern
a substitute for [thing replaced] · substitute X for Y (verb) · substitute for [person] (verb, fill in) · there is no substitute for [noun phrase] · a substitute teacher
Variants
substitute X with Y — a newer American verb frame, now common and accepted in speech, still resisted by careful editors, who prefer replace X with Y or substitute Y for X
Contrast
The verb reverses the roles and that is the whole difficulty. Substitute margarine for butter means margarine goes in and butter comes out — the new thing follows the verb and the old thing follows for. Because that feels backward, people write ✗ substitute butter for margarine when they mean the opposite, and the sentence says the reverse of what they intend. The noun is safer: a substitute for butter is always the replacement. When in doubt, use replace X with Y, which nobody misreads. Note substitute for the regular teacher (fill in for), where the verb is intransitive.

Something used in place of something else.

  1. Applesauce is a decent substitute for oil in that recipe.
  2. There's no substitute for showing up and asking in person.
  3. She worked as a substitute in three districts that year.
  4. The recipe says to substitute buttermilk for the cream.
  5. Video calls are a poor substitute for being in the room.
  6. He substituted for the regular organist all through Lent.
See also
alternative to, preference for, comparison with/to, supply of, substitute for, for

success in/at#

Pronunciation
/səkˈsɛs ɪn/, /səkˈsɛs æt/ · suk-SESS in, suk-SESS at · weak /səkˈsɛs ən/, /səkˈsɛs ət/
Русский
успех в чём-л. — достижение цели и область, где оно случилось
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
in / at + gerund or a noun of activity (success in reducing costs, success at persuading the board) — never a to-infinitive (✗ success to reduce costs). with: a person or a project (success with the new supplier). Success of is a different relation: it names whose success it is, not what the success consists in.
Pattern
success in [field / verb-ing] · success at [activity / verb-ing] · success with [person / project] · the success of [venture] · have success in [verb-ing] · without success · the key to success
Variants
success in — a domain or an effort · success at — a specific activity or knack; both are standard American English and often interchangeable
Contrast
The gerund is obligatory — ✗ she had success to negotiate a lower rate — and the fix is success in negotiating. ✗ success for is an error. Distinguish the success of the campaign (how the campaign fared) from success in the campaign (what someone achieved during it). The adjective takes in: successful in getting the permit, and ✗ successful to get the permit is the same mistake one word-class over. Note without success, a fixed adverbial with no article.

The achieving of an aim, and the area in which it happens.

  1. The clinic has had real success in reducing missed appointments.
  2. He's had more success at raising money than at spending it well.
  3. Our success with the new supplier lasted exactly one quarter.
  4. The success of the festival surprised the people who ran it.
  5. They tried three times to reach him, without success.
  6. Success in getting the grant depends almost entirely on the narrative section.
See also
progress in/on, experience of/in/with, expert on/in, attempt at, successful in, in

successor to#

Pronunciation
/səkˈsɛsɚ tu/ · suk-SESS-er too · weak /səkˈsɛsɚ tə/
Русский
преемник кого-л./чего-л. — следующий в должности, роду или ряду
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — business reporting, obituaries, and legal drafting. An American meets the phrase successors and assigns in the boilerplate at the end of almost every contract.
Complement
a noun phrase naming a person, an office, a title, or a thing being replaced (a successor to the chairman, a successor to the Apollo program) — never a gerund (✗ a successor to running the program) and never a to-infinitive. Note that a successor to run the program is grammatical but is not this pattern: there to run is a purpose infinitive hanging off a complete noun phrase, meaning someone brought in in order to run it.
Pattern
a successor to [person / office / product] · name / choose / appoint [someone] as successor to [person] · the successor to the throne · a successor in interest · successors and assigns (contract boilerplate) · the successor entity
Variants
a successor of — not an error, but markedly less idiomatic in American English than to · successor in interest, successor in title, and successors and assigns are fixed legal phrases that take no to at all
Contrast
The person, the office, and the product all take to: a successor to Justice O'Connor, a successor to the chairmanship, a successor to the Model T. Its opposite number is predecessor, which takes to as well but also in for the job itself — her predecessor in the job, her predecessor to the seat. Watch the noun-verb split: the verb succeed to takes an office or an estate (he succeeded to the title in 1901) while succeed in takes an effort (she succeeded in raising the money) — see succeed in. ✗ the successor for the chairman and ✗ a successor of running the program are errors.

The person or thing that comes next in a position, a line, or a series.

  1. The board still has not named a successor to the retiring chief executive.
  2. She was his hand-picked successor to the seat and lost it in eighteen months.
  3. The F-35 was sold to Congress as the successor to three separate airframes.
  4. Under the Constitution the vice president is the successor to the presidency.
  5. This Agreement binds the parties and their successors and assigns.
  6. Every successor to the trusteeship inherits the same reporting obligation.
See also
signatory to, party to, alternative to, substitute for, entitlement to, succeed in

supply of#

Pronunciation
/səˈplaɪ ʌv/ · suh-PLY uv · weak /səˈplaɪ əv/ → /səˈplaɪ ə/
Русский
запас чего-л., снабжение чем-л.; эконом. предложение
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: a noun phrase naming the commodity (a supply of clean water). to: the recipient (supply to the northern towns). Never a gerund, never an infinitive, never a clause.
Pattern
a supply of [commodity] · the supply to [recipient] · supplies for [purpose / trip] · in short supply · supply and demand · cut off the supply of [noun phrase]
Contrast
Of is what the supply consists of; to is where it goes; for belongs to the plural supplies, meaning provisions. A supply of water, the supply to the hospital, supplies for the trip — and ✗ a supply for water is an error. The plural is a different noun: supplies means the things themselves (office supplies, art supplies), so the supplies of water were cut off reads oddly where the supply of water was cut off is right. In short supply is a fixed phrase with no article.

The amount of something available, and who gets it.

  1. The town has a two-day supply of drinking water and no backup well.
  2. The supply to the northern half of the county runs through one pump station.
  3. We loaded supplies for a four-day trip and used most of it in two.
  4. Qualified welders are in short supply everywhere in the state.
  5. The whole argument comes down to supply and demand.
  6. A steady supply of volunteers keeps the pantry open six days a week.
See also
demand for, need for, lack of, price of/for, of

sympathy for/with#

Pronunciation
/ˈsɪmpəθi fɔr/, /ˈsɪmpəθi wɪθ/ · SIM-puh-thee for, SIM-puh-thee with · weak /ˈsɪmpəθi fɚ/, /ˈsɪmpəθi wəθ/
Русский
for — сочувствие, жалость к кому-л.; with — согласие с позицией
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for: a person or group pitied (sympathy for the families). with: a view, cause, or position agreed with (sympathy with the strikers' demands). Gerunds are marginal; never a to-infinitive and never a clause.
Pattern
sympathy for [person] · sympathy with [view / cause] · in sympathy with [noun phrase] · have / feel / express sympathy for [person] · my sympathies to [the bereaved] · a sympathy card
Contrast
For is pity; with is agreement. Sympathy for the strikers means you feel sorry for them; sympathy with the strikers' demands means you think they are right — and you can have one without the other, which is why the preposition matters. ✗ sympathy to is an error except in the fixed condolence formula my sympathies to the family. Note that sympathetic to and sympathetic toward both exist for the agreement sense (sympathetic to the proposal), so the adjective does not follow the noun's split cleanly. In sympathy with can also mean mechanically in step (the second gauge moves in sympathy with the first).

Pity for someone, or agreement with a position.

  1. I have enormous sympathy for anyone trying to buy a first house right now.
  2. The editorial board expressed sympathy with the union's core demand.
  3. There was little public sympathy for the bank after the third fee increase.
  4. She acted in sympathy with a strike she was not part of.
  5. Please pass on my sympathies to the whole family.
  6. His sympathy for the tenants did not extend to lowering the rent.
See also
concern about/for, respect for, opposition to, agreement with/on, sympathetic to, with

T#

talent for/knack for#

Pronunciation
/ˈtælənt fɔr/, /næk fɔr/ · TAL-unt for, nak for · weak /ˈtælənt fɚ/, /næk fɚ/; the k of knack is silent, so the word is /næk/, a homophone of nack
Русский
талант к чему-л.; сноровка, умение ловко делать что-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
talent for neutral; knack for neutral to informal. Both are at home in speech, in a recommendation letter, and in a performance review.
Complement
a noun phrase or a gerund (a talent for getting people to talk, a knack for finding parking) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a talent to draw, ✗ a knack to find parking) and never a that-clause. The for is a preposition, so a verb after it has to end in -ing.
Pattern
a talent for [noun / verb-ing] · a knack for [noun / verb-ing] · have / show / display a talent for [noun phrase] · a real / rare / genuine talent for [noun phrase] · the knack of [verb-ing] · get the knack of [noun phrase] · there's a knack to it · talent show, talent scout (fixed compounds, no preposition)
Variants
the knack of + gerund — equally standard, and commoner in older writing · there's a knack to it — a fixed phrase in which knack takes to and a pronoun; ✗ there's a talent to it is not possible · a talent to + bare verb survives only as a literary archaism (Noël Coward's A Talent to Amuse) and is an error in ordinary modern American prose
Contrast
Talent is capacity; knack is a trick you picked up. A talent for the piano suggests something innate and large; a knack for fixing printers suggests something small and learned, so calling a concert pianist's ability a knack is faint praise. Knack alone takes of and to as well; talent takes only for. Against fondness for, which is liking rather than ability, and success in/at, which is the result rather than the capacity. The adjective side takes at, never for: good at languages and skilled at negotiation — see good at — while ✗ good for languages means something else entirely.

A natural ability, or a small acquired skill for doing something well.

  1. He has a talent for remembering names that has carried his whole career.
  2. She's got a knack for getting the county clerk to answer the phone.
  3. Nobody in that family has any talent for keeping a secret.
  4. There's a knack to opening the back gate without waking the dog.
  5. The program looks for a talent for math long before it looks at grades.
  6. It took me a month to get the knack of the espresso machine.
See also
fondness for, reputation for, success in/at, grasp of, good at, skilled at

tariff on/duty on/levy on#

Pronunciation
/ˈtɛrɪf ɑn/, /ˈduti ɑn/, /ˈlɛvi ɑn/ · TAIR-if on, DOO-tee on, LEV-ee on · weak /ˈtɛrɪf ən/, /ˈduti ən/, /ˈlɛvi ən/
Русский
тариф, пошлина, сбор на что-л. — государственный сбор с товара
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — trade reporting, customs paperwork, and statute. An American meets duty at the customs desk on the way home and tariff in every election-year argument about trade.
Complement
on: a noun phrase naming the goods taxed, or the country they come from (a tariff on imported steel, tariffs on China). of: the rate (a duty of twelve percent). against: a country, in the retaliatory sense (tariffs against Canada), commoner in speech than in trade writing. Never a gerund — the complement names goods, not an activity, so ✗ a tariff on importing steel is not idiomatic — and never an infinitive or a clause.
Pattern
a tariff on [goods] · impose / levy / slap a tariff on [noun phrase] · a duty of [rate] on [goods] · a levy on [goods / property / activity] · retaliatory / punitive / protective tariffs · duty-free · tariff rate, tariff schedule (fixed compounds)
Variants
a levy on also covers local government charges that have nothing to do with imports — a school levy, a levy on new construction — where tariff and customs duty cannot be used · the verb tariff exists but is rare; American idiom is impose or slap tariffs on
Contrast
Three near-synonyms, one preposition, three different homes. Tariff is the trade-policy word and the one used in politics; duty is the customs word an American actually pays at a border; levy is the broadest and lives mostly in local government and as a verb. All three take on for what is charged and of for the rate, and the two stack: a duty of six percent on olive oil. Against tax on, the general term — every tariff is a tax, not every tax is a tariff. Keep this duty apart from the obligation sense at duty to/of: a duty on olive oil is money, a duty to your clients is not. ✗ a tariff for imported steel and ✗ a tariff to imports are errors.

A government charge on goods, named by what it falls on.

  1. The administration imposed a twenty-five percent tariff on imported washing machines.
  2. There's still a duty on wine over a liter, even in your own suitcase.
  3. Voters rejected the levy on new construction for the second time in three years.
  4. Canada answered with retaliatory tariffs on bourbon and motorcycles.
  5. The duty of six percent applies whether the parts arrive assembled or loose.
  6. Growers say the tariff on fertilizer costs them more than the one on equipment saves them.
See also
tax on, duty to/of, embargo on, sanction against/sanctions on, price of/for, on

tax on#

Pronunciation
/tæks ɑn/ · taks on · weak /tæks ən/
Русский
налог на что-л. — рус. «на», не «с»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
on: a noun phrase naming what is taxed (a tax on imported steel). of: the rate or amount (a tax of nine percent). for: the purpose the revenue serves (a tax for the new library). Never a gerund, an infinitive, or a clause.
Pattern
a tax on [goods / activity] · a tax of [rate / amount] · a tax for [purpose] · pay tax on [income] · before / after tax · a tax break for [group]
Contrast
On for what is taxed, of for how much, and the two stack: a tax of nine percent on short-term rentals. ✗ a tax of short-term rentals is an error. ✗ a tax to imports is likewise wrong. The verb takes a bare object for the thing or person taxed (they tax capital gains, the county taxes nonresidents) and no preposition at all: ✗ they tax on capital gains. Note the American terms tax break, tax bracket, and tax return, all fixed compounds that take for, of, and no preposition respectively.

A compulsory charge, named by what it falls on.

  1. The council is considering a tax on short-term rentals.
  2. There's a tax of nine percent on prepared food in this county.
  3. Voters approved a small tax for the new library and rejected the one for streets.
  4. You'll pay tax on the interest even if you never withdraw it.
  5. The figures in column three are before tax.
  6. The tax on diesel has not been raised since 1993.
See also
price of/for, limit to/on, demand for, rule against/for, on

taste for/in#

Pronunciation
/teɪst fɔr/, /teɪst ɪn/ · tayst for, tayst in · weak /teɪst fɚ/, /teɪst ən/
Русский
for — вкус к чему-л., пристрастие; in — вкус в чём-л. (в одежде, в музыке)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for: a liking — noun phrase or gerund (a taste for taking risks) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a taste to take risks). in: the domain in which someone exercises judgment (taste in music). of: a literal or figurative sample (a taste of what's coming).
Pattern
a taste for [thing liked] · taste in [domain] · a taste of [sample / experience] · acquire a taste for [noun phrase] · in good / poor taste · to taste (in recipes)
Contrast
Three prepositions, three completely different claims. A taste for opera means you like it; taste in opera means your judgment about which opera is good; a taste of opera means a small sample of it. ✗ a taste of adventure when you mean a liking is the usual slip. In good taste and in poor taste judge propriety, not preference, and take no complement. Note the fixed an acquired taste and the recipe instruction salt to taste, both bare.

A liking, a faculty of judgment, or a sample.

  1. He developed a taste for very strong coffee in the Navy.
  2. Her taste in music stopped developing somewhere around 1994.
  3. The first week gave them a taste of what winter here is actually like.
  4. The joke was in poor taste and the room let him know.
  5. Olives are an acquired taste in most families.
  6. Add salt and pepper to taste.
See also
fondness for, preference for, love for/of, interest in, for

tendency to/toward#

Pronunciation
/ˈtɛndənsi tu/, /ˈtɛndənsi təˈwɔrd/ · TEN-dun-see too, TEN-dun-see tuh-WORD · weak /ˈtɛndənsi tə/, /ˈtɛndənsi tɔrd/
Русский
склонность к чему-л., тенденция к — устойчивая наклонность
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
to + bare infinitive: the habitual act (a tendency to overreact) — here to is the infinitive marker, not a preposition. toward + noun phrase or gerund: a direction or inclination (a tendency toward caution, a tendency toward exaggerating). ✗ a tendency to overreacting and ✗ a tendency toward overreact are both errors.
Pattern
a tendency to [verb] · a tendency toward [noun phrase / verb-ing] · have a tendency to [verb] · a growing tendency to [verb] · tendencies (plural, of a person)
Variants
toward — American, no -s · towards — British · a tendency for X to [verb] — the technical frame naming the thing that tends (a tendency for the gasket to fail)
Contrast
This is the entry where the to trap runs in the opposite direction from the rest of the file. After tendency, to is an infinitive marker, so the bare verb is correct — a tendency to interrupt, ✗ a tendency to interrupting — while after toward it is a preposition and -ing is correct. A learner who has just memorized objection to being asked will overcorrect into ✗ a tendency to being late. Test it as always: a noun cannot follow tendency to (✗ a tendency to lateness, where a tendency toward lateness is right), which proves to is not a preposition here.

An inclination to behave or develop in a particular way.

  1. He has a tendency to interrupt when he's nervous.
  2. There's a general tendency toward consolidation in the industry.
  3. The soil here has a tendency to hold water through April.
  4. Her tendency toward understatement makes the memoir harder to read than it should be.
  5. There's a documented tendency for the older valves to stick.
  6. The whole department has a tendency to wait for permission.
See also
habit of, attitude to/toward, willingness to, risk of, toward

threat to#

Pronunciation
/θrɛt tu/ · thret too · weak /θrɛt tə/
Русский
угроза кому-л./чему-л. — рус. дательный, не «к»
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
to: the person or thing endangered (a threat to national security). of: what is threatened (a threat of violence). against: the target of a stated menace (a threat against a judge). from: the source. The noun also takes a to-infinitive naming the threatened act (a threat to resign) and a that-clause (a threat that the plant would close).
Pattern
a threat to [thing endangered] · a threat of [bad outcome] · a threat against [target] · a threat from [source] · a threat to [verb] · under threat · pose a threat to [noun phrase]
Contrast
To is who is endangered, of is what might happen, and the two produce different sentences from the same words: a threat to the water supply is the supply being at risk, while a threat of contamination is the contamination being feared. ✗ a threat for the water supply is an error. Note that a threat to resign is an infinitive, not the preposition — the test is that no noun fits (✗ a threat to resignation). The verb takes a bare object and with: they threatened the mayor, threatened him with a lawsuit, ✗ threatened to the mayor.

Something that endangers, or a stated intention to do harm.

  1. The unlined pit is a direct threat to the shallow aquifer.
  2. There was a vague threat of legal action in the second paragraph.
  3. The court received two credible threats against the presiding judge.
  4. Her threat to resign ended the argument in about four seconds.
  5. The whole business district is under threat from the next big flood.
  6. Invasive mussels pose a real threat to the hatchery.
See also
danger of/to, risk of, protection from/against, attack on, to

threshold for#

Pronunciation
/ˈθrɛʃhoʊld fɔr/ · THRESH-hohld for · weak /ˈθrɛʃhoʊld fɚ/; /ˈθrɛʃoʊld/ without the second h is equally standard in American speech
Русский
порог чего-л. — уровень, с которого что-л. начинает действовать или засчитываться
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — medicine, statistics, statute, and business policy. An American meets it as the income threshold for a tax credit, the threshold for reporting a cash payment, and, in a doctor's office, a threshold for pain.
Complement
for: a noun phrase naming what the level governs (the threshold for eligibility) or a gerund (the threshold for reporting a spill) — never a to-infinitive (✗ the threshold to report a spill, ✗ a threshold to qualify) and never a that-clause. of: the quantity itself (a threshold of five thousand dollars, the threshold of pain). at / on: the level treated as a place (on the threshold of a decision).
Pattern
the threshold for [action / status / benefit] · a threshold of [amount] · raise / lower / set / meet / cross / exceed the threshold · above / below the threshold · on the threshold of [a change] · a pain threshold, a threshold question (fixed compounds)
Variants
a threshold of + amount and a threshold for + purpose combine freely in one phrase — a threshold of ten thousand dollars for reporting cash payments · British writing sometimes has the threshold at which, which is standard in American technical prose too
Contrast
For names what the line governs; of names the line itself. The threshold for filing is who has to file; a threshold of ten thousand dollars is the number. ✗ the threshold to filing is an error. Against limit to/on, which runs the other way: a limit is a ceiling you may not pass, a threshold is a floor you have to reach. Against criterion for, which is a test rather than a level, and eligibility for, which is the status a threshold decides. Keep the literal doorsill in view — on the threshold of takes of and means about to begin, never a number.

The level at which something starts to count, apply, or take effect.

  1. Congress raised the income threshold for the credit to eighty thousand dollars.
  2. Her threshold for nonsense in a budget meeting is famously low.
  3. The statute sets a threshold of ten thousand dollars for reporting cash payments.
  4. Nothing below the threshold for reporting ever reaches the regional office.
  5. Two of the four counties fell just under the threshold and got nothing.
  6. He has an unusually high threshold for pain and a very low one for noise.
See also
limit to/on, criterion for, eligibility for, grounds for, exemption from, for

title to#

Pronunciation
/ˈtaɪtəl tu/ · TY-tul too · weak /ˈtaɪtəl tə/; the flapped t makes the first syllable sound like tie-dul
Русский
юр. право собственности на что-л.; также правоустанавливающий документ
Type
noun + preposition
Register
legal — deeds, closings, title insurance, and vehicle registration. An American meets it twice: when the state mails the title to a car, and at a closing table, where a title company certifies clear title to a house.
Complement
a noun phrase naming property — land, a vehicle, goods (title to the property) — never a gerund (✗ title to owning the land), never a to-infinitive (✗ title to own the land), and never a person. In this sense the noun is uncountable and normally takes no article: she has title to the land, not ✗ she has a title to the land. The unrelated noun title meaning a name or a rank takes of: the title of the book, the title of vice chair.
Pattern
title to [real property / a vehicle / goods] · hold / take / convey / pass title to [noun phrase] · clear / marketable / good title to [noun phrase] · a cloud on title · title insurance, title search, title company (fixed compounds) · the title of [a work / an office] (the other noun entirely)
Variants
title in [property] appears in older deeds and in some state statutes and means the same thing; to is the modern American form · in the sale of goods the verb phrase title passes is intransitive and takes no preposition at all
Contrast
Title to is ownership; the title of is a name. Title to the house is the legal right to it; the title of the song is what it is called; ✗ the title of the house means nothing. Against possession of: possession is holding the thing, title is owning it, and a tenant has the first without the second. Against claim against/for/to: a claim to land is an assertion, title is the settled answer. Against right to/of/over: title is the strongest of the rights in property, which is why drafters write title to and all rights in the parcel. And keep lien on/against separate — a lien sits on title without taking it.

Legal ownership of property, and the document that proves it.

  1. The seller could not produce clear title to the back forty and the deal died.
  2. Title to the vehicle passes when the lien is released, not when the last payment clears.
  3. A quitclaim deed conveys whatever title to the parcel the grantor happens to have.
  4. The bank will not close until the title search comes back clean.
  5. Squatters can acquire title to land in this state after twenty years of open use.
  6. She kept the car's title in a folder in the freezer.
See also
possession of, claim against/for/to, right to/of/over, easement over/across, lien on/against, custody of

trouble with#

Pronunciation
/ˈtrʌbəl wɪθ/ · TRUB-ul with · weak /ˈtrʌbəl wəθ/
Русский
беда, проблема с кем-л./чем-л. — the trouble with him: беда в том, что он...
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
with: a person or thing causing difficulty (trouble with the transmission). Also a bare gerund with no preposition at all in the have trouble frame (trouble finding parking) — never a to-infinitive (✗ trouble to find parking). The trouble is that takes a clause.
Pattern
trouble with [person / thing] · have trouble [verb-ing] · the trouble with [noun phrase] is that [clause] · in trouble · go to the trouble of [verb-ing] · ask for trouble
The gerund is obligatory and the infinitive is the error
✗ I had trouble to understand him, ✗ she has trouble to sleep. Write trouble understanding him, trouble sleeping. American English usually drops any preposition before the gerund, where difficulty optionally keeps in — see difficulty with/in. Note go to the trouble of doing something (fixed, of required), in trouble (in a bad position, no complement), and the trouble with him is that..., the standard American frame for a character criticism. The noun is uncountable here: ✗ a trouble with the transmission, ✗ troubles to sleep.

Difficulty, and the thing or person causing it.

  1. We've had nothing but trouble with that transmission since the rebuild.
  2. She has trouble sleeping in any room with a streetlight.
  3. The trouble with the new form is that nobody knows who signs it.
  4. He's in trouble with the licensing board again.
  5. Nobody went to the trouble of checking whether the permit was still valid.
  6. I had trouble finding the entrance; it's behind the loading dock.
See also
difficulty with/in, problem with, matter with, delay in, with

trust in#

Pronunciation
/trʌst ɪn/ · trust in · weak /trʌst ən/
Русский
доверие к кому-л. — рус. «к», не «в»; отдельно фин. траст, доверительный фонд
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral; the in frame is slightly elevated in speech, where the noun often appears bare (there's no trust left in that office)
Complement
in: a person, an institution, or a system (trust in the courts), or a gerund (trust in getting a fair hearing, uncommon) — never a to-infinitive (✗ trust in to get a fair hearing) and never a clause after the preposition (✗ trust in that the courts will act). The noun does take a bare that-clause with no preposition at all (a naive trust that things would work out). for: in the financial sense, the beneficiary (a trust for her grandchildren).
Pattern
trust in [person / institution] · put / place one's trust in [noun phrase] · have trust in [noun phrase] · a breach of trust · a position of trust · take [something] on trust · hold [property] in trust · in trust for [beneficiary]
Variants
trust that + clause, with no preposition — the standard way to attach a proposition · the verb trust to survives in the fixed phrase trust to luck, and nowhere else in ordinary American usage
Contrast
The noun needs in; the verb refuses it. I trust her takes a bare object, and I trust in her sounds biblical, but the noun cannot do without the preposition: my trust in her. Against confidence in and faith in, which take the same preposition and differ in kind — confidence is calculated, trust is relational, faith is held without evidence — and against belief in, which is about existence or value rather than reliability. ✗ trust on and ✗ trust to are errors in this sense. The financial noun is a different word wearing the same spelling: a trust for the children is a legal arrangement, and property held in trust takes no complement — see in trust for.

Reliance on someone's honesty or reliability — and, in finance, the arrangement that holds property for someone else.

1. Belief that a person or institution will not let you down. The complement after in is the party relied on, never the thing hoped for.

  1. Her trust in the county board did not survive the second audit.
  2. He put far too much trust in a contractor he had never met.
  3. Public trust in the agency has been falling for a decade.
  4. Nothing rebuilds trust in an institution faster than firing the person responsible.

2. A legal arrangement holding property for a beneficiary. Countable, and it takes for naming the beneficiary; the property itself is held in trust.

  1. The money sits in a trust for his grandchildren until they turn twenty-five.
  2. The land is held in trust by the tribe and cannot be sold.
See also
confidence in, faith in, belief in, dependence on/reliance on, beneficiary of/under, in trust for

U#

understanding of#

Pronunciation
/ˌʌndɚˈstændɪŋ ʌv/ · un-der-STAN-ding uv · weak /ˌʌndɚˈstændɪŋ əv/ → /ˌʌndɚˈstændɪŋ ə/
Русский
понимание чего-л.; отдельно — неформальная договорённость
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of: noun phrase, gerund (an understanding of how to file), or a wh-clause (an understanding of what the job requires) — never a to-infinitive. with: the other party to an informal agreement. The noun also takes a bare that-clause: an understanding that the rent would not rise.
Pattern
an understanding of [subject] · an understanding with [person] · on the understanding that [clause] · reach an understanding · a working understanding of [subject] · beyond my understanding
Contrast
Two different nouns. An understanding of the rules is comprehension; an understanding with the landlord is an informal agreement, and the two senses share nothing but a spelling. ✗ an understanding about the rules is an error in the comprehension sense, though an understanding about the parking works in the agreement sense. On the understanding that is a fixed conditional frame — ✗ on the understanding of that. Distinguish from knowledge of/about, which is possession of facts, and grasp of, which is nearly synonymous.

Comprehension of something, or an informal agreement.

  1. She has a solid understanding of how the assessment appeal works.
  2. We reached an understanding with the neighbors about the shared driveway.
  3. He signed on the understanding that the rent would not rise for two years.
  4. The report shows almost no understanding of how the permit process runs.
  5. A working understanding of basic statistics would help here.
  6. Why they filled in the well is beyond my understanding.
See also
knowledge of/about, grasp of, insight into, agreement with/on, of

V#

view on/of#

Pronunciation
/vju ɑn/, /vju ʌv/ · vyoo on, vyoo uv · weak /vju ən/, /vju əv/ → /vju ə/
Русский
мнение о чём-л.; вид на что-л.; намерение (with a view to)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
on / about: an opinion about an issue — noun phrase, gerund (views on raising the levy), or wh-clause. of: what can be seen from a place (a view of the harbor), or an assessment (a dim view of his chances). to: only in with a view to + gerund (with a view to selling), never an infinitive. Never a that-clause after a preposition, though the view that takes one bare.
Pattern
views on / about [issue] · a view of [scene] · the view that [clause] · in my view · in view of [noun phrase] · with a view to [verb-ing] · take a dim view of [noun phrase]
Contrast
Four frames and one classic trap. Views on the levy are opinions; a view of the harbor is what you see; in view of the weather means "because of"; and with a view to selling means "intending to." That last to is a preposition, so the gerund is required: ✗ with a view to sell is the error, and it is common in legal drafting. My view about is acceptable but on is the edited form, and ✗ according to my view should be in my view. Note take a dim view of, a fixed idiom of disapproval.

An opinion, a scene, or an intention.

  1. Her views on zoning have hardened since she bought the lot.
  2. The room has a view of the harbor and, unfortunately, of the parking structure.
  3. In my view, the second bid was never serious.
  4. In view of the forecast, the ceremony was moved indoors.
  5. The property was bought with a view to subdividing it.
  6. The board takes a dim view of people who miss three meetings in a row.
See also
opinion on/of/about, attitude to/toward, comment on, idea of/for, of

visit to#

Pronunciation
/ˈvɪzɪt tu/ · VIZ-it too · weak /ˈvɪzɪt tə/
Русский
визит к кому-л., посещение чего-л., поездка куда-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
to: the place or person visited (a visit to the dentist). from: the visitor. with: in American English, spending time with someone (a visit with my grandmother). Never a gerund, an infinitive, or a clause.
Pattern
a visit to [place / person] · a visit from [visitor] · a visit with [person] (American) · pay a visit to [place] · on a visit · a home visit
Variants
a visit with — distinctly American; British English uses a visit to for people as well, and visit with can strike British readers as odd
Contrast
To is where you went; from is who came to you; with is the time spent together. A visit to my grandmother emphasizes going there; a visit with my grandmother emphasizes the conversation, and only American English has the second. The verb shows the same American split: visit her (go see her) against visit with her (sit and talk), and ✗ visit to her as a verb is an error — visit is bare transitive: we visited the museum, ✗ we visited to the museum.

A short stay somewhere, or time spent with someone.

  1. Her annual visit to the dentist is the only appointment she never misses.
  2. We had a visit from the building inspector on Tuesday.
  3. I had a long visit with my aunt on the porch.
  4. The senator paid a visit to the flooded neighborhoods on Friday.
  5. They're here on a visit from Duluth.
  6. A home visit is scheduled within ten days of discharge.
See also
access to, introduction to, interview with, encounter with, to

W#

waiver of#

Pronunciation
/ˈweɪvɚ ʌv/ · WAY-ver uv · weak /ˈweɪvɚ əv/ → /ˈweɪvɚ ə/
Русский
юр. отказ от права или требования; также документ, фиксирующий такой отказ
Type
noun + preposition
Register
legal and official — contracts, court filings, insurance forms, and school and sports paperwork. An American signs one before a field trip, a 5K, or a trampoline park, and reads about one when a ballplayer clears waivers.
Complement
of: a noun phrase naming the right, claim, fee, or requirement given up (a waiver of liability), or a gerund (a waiver of having to appear in person, rare and clumsy) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a waiver to appear in person) and never a that-clause. from: the requirement escaped, in administrative usage (a waiver from the residency rule). for: the person or program excused (a waiver for rural districts).
Pattern
a waiver of [a right / a claim / a fee / a requirement] · sign / grant / seek / obtain / deny a waiver · a waiver from [a rule] · a waiver for [a person / a program] · no waiver of any provision shall be deemed a waiver of any other (contract boilerplate) · on waivers, the waiver wire (baseball and football)
Variants
a waiver on — heard in American administrative speech (a waiver on the deadline) but not standard in drafting, where of or from is written · the fixed sports phrases place a player on waivers and claim a player off waivers use the bare plural with no complement
Contrast
Of for the right surrendered, from
for the rule escaped
a waiver of the fee is the fee forgiven, a waiver from the vaccination requirement is the rule lifted for one person. ✗ a waiver to liability is an error. Against exemption from: an exemption is written into a rule in advance, a waiver gives up something you already held. Against immunity from, which is a standing status rather than a single act. And mind the homophone — you waive a right and you waver in a decision, and no spellchecker will catch ✗ a waver of liability.

A voluntary surrender of a right, a claim, or a requirement — and the paper that records it.

  1. The camp will not take a child without a signed waiver of liability.
  2. The state asked for a waiver from the federal work requirement.
  3. Nothing in this section is a waiver of any other right under the contract.
  4. She got a tuition waiver for her first two semesters.
  5. Accepting one late payment is not a waiver of the right to insist on time.
  6. He cleared waivers on Tuesday and signed with Detroit on Thursday.
See also
exemption from, immunity from, breach of, liability for/to, notice of/to, of

warranty as to#

Pronunciation
/ˈwɔrənti æz tu/ · WAWR-un-tee az too · weak /ˈwɔrənti əz tə/
Русский
юр. заверение относительно чего-л. — договорная гарантия по конкретному предмету
Type
noun + preposition
Register
legal drafting — the representations-and-warranties section of a purchase agreement, and the all-caps disclaimer on a bill of sale. An American meets it in the sentence beginning SELLER MAKES NO WARRANTY AS TO... on nearly every used-equipment or as-is sale.
Complement
as to: a noun phrase or a wh-clause naming the subject warranted (no warranty as to the accuracy of the figures, no warranty as to whether the software will run) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a warranty as to be accurate). A gerund is grammatically possible (no warranty as to meeting the specification) but is almost never drafted; the noun phrase is the idiom. The same noun also takes of (a warranty of merchantability), that + clause (a warranty that the goods conform), and, in consumer usage, on and for (the warranty on the transmission, a warranty for three years).
Pattern
no warranty as to [subject] · representations and warranties as to [title / condition / authority] · a warranty of [merchantability / fitness for a particular purpose / title] · a warranty that [clause] · the warranty on [a product] · under warranty · an extended warranty
Variants
warranty with respect to and warranty regarding are drafted just as often and mean the same thing — see with respect to · British contracts prefer warranty as to; American ones split between as to and with respect to
Contrast
As to fences off a topic, of names the promise, and that states it. No warranty as to the mileage declines to say anything about mileage at all; a warranty of title is a specific promise; a warranty that the roof is sound spells the promise out in words. Everyday consumer speech uses a third frame entirely — the warranty on the dishwasher, a warranty for three years — and ✗ the warranty as to the dishwasher would be strange anywhere but a contract. Outside legal and academic writing as to reads as stiff and about or on is the natural choice: see as to. Note also that guarantee is the everyday synonym of the noun but not a term of art — see guarantee of.

A contractual promise about a fact, framed by the topic it covers.

  1. The seller makes no warranty as to the accuracy of the square footage.
  2. The bill of sale disclaims any warranty as to the condition of the equipment.
  3. Section 4 contains the buyer's representations and warranties as to its authority to sign.
  4. There is an implied warranty of merchantability unless the contract disclaims it in writing.
  5. The dealer gave us a written warranty on the transmission and nothing else.
  6. She would not sign anything that carried no warranty as to title.
See also
guarantee of, breach of, remedy for/against, liability for/to, exhibit to/schedule to/annex to, as to

way of/to#

Pronunciation
/weɪ ʌv/, /weɪ tu/ · way uv, way too · weak /weɪ əv/ → /weɪ ə/; /weɪ tə/
Русский
способ сделать что-л. (way to + инф.); манера, привычка (way of + -ing)
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of + gerund: a manner or habit (a way of speaking) — never a bare infinitive after of (✗ a way of speak). to + bare infinitive: a means to an end (a way to fix it) — never an -ing form after to (✗ a way to fixing it). to + noun phrase: a route (the way to the station). ✗ a way for doing it.
Pattern
a way of [verb-ing] · a way to [verb] · the way to [place] · in a way · by way of [route / means] · no way · the way [clause] · get one's way
Variants
a way of doing it and a way to do it — both standard; the first names a manner, the second a means. Where the two overlap (a way of making bread, a way to make bread) either is fine
Contrast
The clearest minimal pair in the file. A way to fix this answers "how do I get the result I want?" and takes the bare infinitive. A way of turning every conversation into an argument answers "what is this person's manner?" and takes -ing. The overlap is real but the edges are sharp: ✗ a way to turning it into an argument is impossible, and her way to say thank you is wrong because a manner is meant — write her way of saying thank you. ✗ a way for doing it and ✗ a way of do it are both errors. Note the separate route sense (the way to the station), which takes a noun, and the bare the way + clause (the way he says it), which needs no preposition at all.

A method, a manner, or a route.

  1. There has to be a way to fix this without repouring the slab.
  2. He has a way of making everyone in the room feel slightly accused.
  3. It was her way of saying thank you without having to say it.
  4. Can you tell me the way to the county courthouse?
  5. That's not the way to talk to somebody who's doing you a favor.
  6. In a way, the delay saved us.
  7. There's no way of knowing how long the pump has been running dry.
See also
reason for, key to, solution to, approach to, of

willingness to#

Pronunciation
/ˈwɪlɪŋnəs tu/ · WIL-ing-nus too · weak /ˈwɪlɪŋnəs tə/
Русский
готовность сделать что-л. — согласие пойти навстречу
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
to + bare infinitive only (a willingness to compromise) — here to is the infinitive marker, not a preposition, so ✗ a willingness to compromising is an error and so is ✗ a willingness to the compromise. There is no of frame and no clause frame.
Pattern
