a willingness to [verb] · show / demonstrate a willingness to [verb] · an unwillingness to [verb] · a willingness on the part of [person] to [verb]
Contrast
The test settles it in one move: no noun fits the slot. ✗ a willingness to a compromise is impossible, which proves to is the infinitive marker and the bare verb is required. That makes willingness the exact opposite of objection to, opposition to, and alternative to, where a noun does fit and -ing is required — and a learner who has drilled those three will overcorrect into ✗ a willingness to compromising. Note the parallel nouns that behave the same way: ability to, readiness to, reluctance to, refusal to, decision to, right to, all infinitive, none prepositional.

Readiness to do something.

  1. What the job needs most is a willingness to be corrected in public.
  2. Her willingness to work nights got her the shift she wanted.
  3. The county showed no willingness to revisit the assessment.
  4. There's a general un*willingness to* put anything in writing.
  5. His willingness to drive four hours for a two-hour meeting is not admirable.
  6. The settlement depended on a willingness to drop the counterclaim.
See also
tendency to/toward, desire for, need for, objection to, reluctant to, to

wish for#

Pronunciation
/wɪʃ fɔr/ · wish for · weak /wɪʃ fɚ/
Русский
желание чего-л., стремление к — рус. родительный
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for: a noun phrase naming what is wanted (a wish for privacy). The same noun takes a to-infinitive with no preposition (a wish to be left alone) and a that-clause (a wish that things had gone differently). ✗ a wish of privacy and ✗ a wish for to be alone are errors.
Pattern
a wish for [noun phrase] · a wish to [verb] · a wish that [clause] · best wishes for [occasion] · against someone's wishes · make a wish
Contrast
For takes a thing; to takes an act, exactly as with desire for. ✗ my wish of a quiet retirement is an error; write my wish for a quiet retirement. The verb has three frames and only one takes for: wish for a raise (long for), wish to speak (formal "want"), and wish someone a happy birthday (two objects, no preposition) — so ✗ wish for you a happy birthday is wrong. Note also the counterfactual I wish I had + past perfect, which takes no preposition at all.

A desire for something, or a hope expressed.

  1. Her only wish for the last year was to stay in her own house.
  2. He expressed a wish to be cremated and nobody wrote it down.
  3. Best wishes for a quick recovery.
  4. The sale went ahead against the wishes of half the family.
  5. There's a widespread wish for something simpler than the current form.
  6. She made a wish and told everybody what it was, which ruins it.
See also
desire for, hope for/of, need for, preference for, for

worry about#

Pronunciation
/ˈwɜri əˈbaʊt/ · WUR-ee uh-BOWT · weak /ˈwɜri ˈbaʊt/
Русский
беспокойство о чём-л., тревога из-за чего-л.
Type
noun + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
about / over: noun phrase, gerund (worries about losing the lease), or wh-clause (worry about what the tests will show) — never a to-infinitive (✗ a worry to lose the lease). The noun also takes a bare that-clause: a worry that the roof would not last.
Pattern
a worry about / over [noun phrase / verb-ing] · a worry that [clause] · worries for [someone's safety] · no worries · a source of worry · the least of my worries
Variants
worry about — the default · worry over — slightly more anxious and more literary · worry for — used of a person's safety (worried for her sister), where about would be neutral
Contrast
About for the noun, by for the passive adjective. Worried about the audit is your own anxiety; worried by the audit is the audit acting on you — the second names an external cause, like any passive agent, so ✗ worried about by the audit is a blend. ✗ worry of is an error. The adjective worried takes about, by, and a that-clause but never an infinitive of the thing feared: ✗ worried to lose the lease should be worried about losing the lease. Compare concern about/for, which splits about and for along the same anxiety-versus-welfare line.

Anxiety about something, and the thing causing it.

  1. Her main worry about the move is finding a school midyear.
  2. Worries over the water table have been raised at every hearing since 2019.
  3. There's a real worry that the roof won't make it through another winter.
  4. The rent is the least of my worries right now.
  5. He never expressed any worry about the inspection until the day before.
  6. She's the family's main source of worry and always has been.
See also
concern about/for, doubt about, fear of, risk of, worried about, about

Zero-preposition traps: verbs that take no preposition at all#

The other half of the noun-versus-verb shift. Every verb below takes a direct object with nothing in between, while the noun built on the same root usually demands a preposition — discuss the budget but a discussion about the budget, marry Tom but a marriage to Tom, lack funding but a lack of funding. Learners who have absorbed the noun patterns in the rest of this file reliably leak the preposition backward onto the verb, producing ✗ discuss about it, ✗ married with her, ✗ he lacks of experience. The entries here exist so that the bare frame gets memorized as deliberately as the prepositional ones.

Two cautions. First, several of these verbs do take a preposition in a different sense (attend to a patient, enter into an agreement, answer to a supervisor), and those senses are marked below; the error is using the prepositional form for the plain transitive meaning. Second, the weak forms in this section belong to the object pronoun, not to a preposition — there is no preposition to reduce, which is exactly the point.

answer something#

Pronunciation
/ˈænsɚ ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ · AN-ser SUM-thing · no preposition to reduce — the verb runs straight into its object: answer it /ˈænsɚ ɪt/, answer them /ˈænsɚ ðəm/
Русский
отвечать на что-л. — англ. глагол без предлога (✗ answer to the question)
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct noun phrase — a question, a letter, a phone, a door, a person. It does not take to in this sense (✗ answer to the question). A separate intransitive sense takes to (report to) and for (be accountable for).
Pattern
answer [question / letter / phone / door] · answer [person] · answer to [supervisor] · answer for [misconduct] · answer back (talk back)
Variants
respond to and reply to — the prepositional synonyms; use them when you want the preposition
Contrast
✗ Answer to the question is the error and it is produced by the noun, which does require to: the answer to the question. Keep the pair straight — verb bare, noun to. The prepositional verb exists but means something else entirely: she answers to the regional director (is supervised by), he'll answer for the missing funds (be held accountable). See answer to for the noun, and compare reply to, where the preposition survives in both classes.

To give a response to a question, letter, phone, door, or person.

  1. Just answer the question you were asked.
  2. Nobody answered the phone at the district office all afternoon.
  3. She never answered my second letter.
  4. Answer the door, would you?
  5. He answers to the regional director, not to me.
  6. Somebody is going to have to answer for how this was approved.
See also
answer to, reply to, response to, reaction to

approach something#

Pronunciation
/əˈproʊtʃ ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ · uh-PROHCH SUM-thing · no preposition to reduce: approach it /əˈproʊtʃ ɪt/
Русский
приближаться к чему-л.; браться за что-л. — англ. без предлога (✗ approach to)
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct noun phrase — a place, a person, a problem, or a number (approaching sixty). It does not take to (✗ approach to the desk). The noun built on it does: an approach to the problem.
Pattern
approach [place / person] · approach [person] about [matter] · approach [problem] · approach [number / age]
Contrast
✗ Approach to the runway as a verb is the error, and it comes straight from the noun, which requires to: the approach to the runway. Say the plane approached the runway. When you name the subject you raised with someone, use about: she approached me about the vacancy, ✗ approached me for the vacancy in that sense. See approach to for the noun.

To come near to something, or to begin dealing with it.

  1. The plane approached the runway too fast and went around.
  2. She approached me after class about switching sections.
  3. You have to approach the whole problem differently.
  4. Don't approach the animal, no matter how tame it looks.
  5. He was approaching sixty and had never taken a vacation.
  6. Two men approached the counter and asked for the manager.
See also
approach to, attitude to/toward, way of/to, contact someone

attend something#

Pronunciation
/əˈtɛnd ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ · uh-TEND SUM-thing · no preposition to reduce: attend it /əˈtɛnd ɪt/
Русский
присутствовать на чём-л., посещать — без предлога (attend to — совсем другое)
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
a direct noun phrase — a meeting, a school, a funeral, a conference. It does not take to in this sense (✗ attend to the meeting). The separate phrasal verb attend to means to deal with or care for, and it is a different verb.
Pattern
attend [meeting / school / event] · attend to [patient / matter] · attendance at [event] · attended by [number of people]
Variants
go to — the everyday alternative (go to the meeting), and much commoner in speech than attend
Contrast
The preposition is not just unnecessary, it changes the verb. She attended the patient means she was present at his bedside as a doctor; she attended to the patient means she treated him; she attended to the meeting is simply wrong. The noun requires a preposition — attendance at the meeting, ✗ attendance of the meeting — so the pair is another verb-bare / noun-prepositional split. Note that attend is formal in American English; in speech people go to things.

To be present at an event or enrolled at an institution.

  1. Only nine people attended the budget hearing.
  2. She attended the state university on a music scholarship.
  3. I can't attend Thursday's session; I'll send the notes.
  4. The funeral was attended by most of the department.
  5. A nurse attended to the cut while we waited for the ambulance.
  6. He has attended every meeting since 1994.
See also
attention to, visit to, interview with, access to

contact someone#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑntækt ˈsʌmwʌn/ · KON-takt SUM-wun · no preposition to reduce: contact them /ˈkɑntækt ðəm/
Русский
связаться с кем-л. — англ. глагол без предлога (✗ contact with him)
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct noun phrase — a person, an office, or an agency. It does not take with as a verb (✗ contact with the office). The noun does: contact with the office.
Pattern
contact [person / office] · contact [person] about [matter] · be in contact with [person] · make contact with [person] · a contact at [organization]
Variants
get in touch with — the idiomatic everyday equivalent, and it does take with
Contrast
✗ Please contact with our office is the error, and the noun is what causes it: in contact with our office, make contact with them. The verb takes the person straight: contact the office. Note the American stress difference between the noun and the verb is disappearing — both are now usually KON-takt — and that a contact meaning a useful person takes at: a contact at the permitting office.

To get in touch with a person or organization.

  1. Please contact the registrar's office before the fifteenth.
  2. I've been trying to contact him about the easement for two weeks.
  3. Contact your carrier if the message doesn't clear in an hour.
  4. She's been in contact with the family since Sunday.
  5. We finally made contact with someone who knew where the records were.
  6. He has a contact at the county assessor's office.
See also
connection with/to/between, encounter with, interview with, phone someone

describe something#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈskraɪb ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ · dih-SKRYBE SUM-thing · no preposition to reduce: describe it /dəˈskraɪb ɪt/
Русский
описывать что-л. — англ. без предлога (✗ describe about it)
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct noun phrase, a wh-clause (describe what you saw), or how + clause. It does not take about (✗ describe about the house). It cannot take an indirect object without to: ✗ describe me the house — write describe the house to me.
Pattern
describe [noun phrase] · describe [noun phrase] to [person] · describe [wh-clause] · describe [someone] as [adjective]
Contrast
Two errors, one verb. ✗ Describe about your trip adds a preposition the verb never takes. ✗ Describe me your trip moves the recipient into a slot this verb does not have — unlike tell and give, describe is one of the Latin-derived verbs that refuses the double-object frame, so the person must come after to: describe your trip to me. The same restriction hits explain, suggest, announce, mention, and report: see explain something to someone.

To give an account of what something is like.

  1. Describe the vehicle as precisely as you can.
  2. She described the house to me over the phone and I bought it.
  3. Can you describe what you saw before the lights went out?
  4. The listing describes the roof as "newer," which means nothing.
  5. He described himself as semi-retired, which was generous.
  6. Describe how the machine sounded when it stopped.
See also
explain something to someone, mention something, report on/of, comment on

discuss something#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈskʌs ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ · dih-SKUSS SUM-thing · no preposition to reduce: discuss it /dəˈskʌs ɪt/
Русский
обсуждать что-л. — англ. без предлога (✗ discuss about the budget)
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct noun phrase, a gerund (discuss selling the building), or a wh-clause (discuss what to do next). It does not take about (✗ discuss about the budget) and never a that-clause (✗ discuss that we should sell).
Pattern
discuss [noun phrase / verb-ing / wh-clause] · discuss [topic] with [person] · under discussion · discuss [noun phrase] at length
Variants
talk about — the everyday equivalent, and it does take about; talk to and talk with are both American, with implying more of an exchange
Contrast
✗ Discuss about is the single most persistent preposition error in advanced English, and it comes from the noun, which requires one: a discussion about the budget. The verb is bare — we discussed the budget — and the person you discussed it with comes after with: I discussed it with the treasurer — and the object should not be left stranded after the with phrase, as in the graceless I discussed with the treasurer the budget. See discussion about/of for the noun, and note that the near-synonyms split: talk about, speak about, but discuss Ø and debate Ø.

To talk something through.

  1. We discussed the budget for two hours and settled nothing.
  2. I'd like to discuss the easement with you before you sign.
  3. The board discussed selling the annex and voted it down.
  4. Let's discuss what happens if the grant doesn't come through.
  5. That was discussed at length in the March meeting.
  6. She refuses to discuss it with anyone but her attorney.
See also
discussion about/of, argument with/about, comment on, mention something

emphasize something#

Pronunciation
/ˈɛmfəsaɪz ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ · EM-fuh-syze SUM-thing · no preposition to reduce: emphasize it /ˈɛmfəsaɪz ɪt/
Русский
подчёркивать что-л., акцентировать — без предлога (✗ emphasize on)
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
a direct noun phrase or a that-clause (emphasized that the deadline was firm). It does not take on (✗ emphasize on the deadline). The noun requires on: emphasis on the deadline.
Pattern
emphasize [noun phrase] · emphasize that [clause] · place emphasis on [noun phrase] · stress [noun phrase] · stress that [clause]
Variants
stress — behaves identically: stress the risk, ✗ stress on the risk, but stress on the second syllable as a noun
Contrast
✗ Emphasize on and ✗ stress on are errors produced entirely by the nouns, both of which take on: emphasis on safety, stress on the second syllable. The verbs are bare: emphasize safety, stress the risk. This is one of the most visible non-native markers in academic English, because the construction appears in almost every abstract. See emphasis on/stress on. Note the American spelling -ize; British writing allows -ise.

To give special importance to something.

  1. The instructor emphasized the deadline three times.
  2. I want to emphasize that nothing has been decided.
  3. The report emphasizes rural access over headline speed.
  4. She stressed the risk of signing before the survey came back.
  5. The training emphasizes de-escalation rather than restraint.
  6. He emphasized the word "voluntary" in a way that made it sound otherwise.
See also
emphasis on/stress on, attention to, focus on, importance of/to

enter something#

Pronunciation
/ˈɛntɚ ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ · EN-ter SUM-thing · no preposition to reduce: enter it /ˈɛntɚ ɪt/
Русский
входить куда-л. — англ. без предлога (✗ enter into the room в прямом смысле)
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct noun phrase — a room, a building, a country, a contest, or data. In the literal sense it does not take into (✗ enter into the room). Enter into is a separate idiom used with agreements, negotiations, and abstractions.
Pattern
enter [place / contest] · enter [data] · enter into [an agreement / negotiations] · entry into [place / market] · entrance to [building]
Contrast
✗ Enter into the room is the error; enter the room is the English. Enter into is reserved for things you take part in rather than places you walk into: enter into a contract, enter into negotiations, enter into an agreement, and the fixed that doesn't enter into it (is not relevant). The nouns take yet other prepositions — entry into the market, entrance to the building — so all three forms differ.

To go into a place, or to take part in something.

  1. Two officers entered the building through the loading dock.
  2. You have to enter the country on the same passport you booked with.
  3. She entered the contest on a dare and won.
  4. Enter your policy number in the second field.
  5. The city entered into a twenty-year agreement with the utility.
  6. How much it costs doesn't enter into it.
See also
access to, introduction to, agreement with/on, approach something

explain something to someone#

Pronunciation
/ɪkˈspleɪn ˈsʌmθɪŋ tu ˈsʌmwʌn/ · ik-SPLAYN SUM-thing too SUM-wun · to weakens to /tə/ and links: explain it to me /əkˈspleɪn ɪt tə mi/
Русский
объяснять что-л. кому-л. — порядок жёсткий (✗ explain me the rule)
Type
verb + direct object (+ to + person)
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct noun phrase, a that-clause (explain that the form was late), or a wh-clause (explain why it failed). The recipient must be introduced by to: ✗ explain me the rule. It does not take about (✗ explain about the rule, though explain about it is heard in casual speech).
Pattern
explain [noun phrase] to [person] · explain to [person] that [clause] · explain [wh-clause] · explain [noun phrase] · an explanation of [thing] to [person]
Contrast
✗ Explain me the rule is the error, and it is nearly universal among speakers of Spanish, French, Italian, Russian, and German, all of which allow the double-object frame. English does not for this verb: the person takes to, and the thing explained comes first — explain the rule to me. When the explanation is a clause, to me moves ahead of it: explain to me why the form was rejected, where explain why the form was rejected to me is grammatical but clumsy. The same rule governs describe, suggest, announce, mention, recommend, and report. Contrast tell, give, show, and send, which do take the double object: tell me the rule, where tell the rule to me is stiff but not wrong.

To make something clear to someone.

  1. Can you explain the appeal process to me in plain English?
  2. She explained to the board that the money had already been spent.
  3. Nobody has explained why the second inspection was canceled.
  4. Explain it to your brother the way you just explained it to me.
  5. He tried to explain the rules of cricket on a four-hour drive.
  6. The letter never explains what triggered the review.
See also
describe something, mention something, information about/on, reason for

join something#

Pronunciation
/dʒɔɪn ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ · joyn SUM-thing · no preposition to reduce: join it /dʒɔɪn ɪt/
Русский
вступать в что-л., присоединяться к — англ. без предлога (✗ join to the club)
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct noun phrase — an organization, a line, a group, or a person. It does not take to or in when it means becoming a member (✗ join to the club). Join in means take part in an ongoing activity; join X to Y means physically connect two things.
Pattern
join [organization / line / person] · join in [activity] · join X to Y · join up (enlist) · join [someone] for [meal / event]
Contrast
✗ Join to the union and ✗ join with the club are errors; the verb is bare — join the union. Three related frames are not: join in the singing (participate), join the two pipes together (connect), and join us for dinner (accompany). The noun is a different word class entirely (a joint, a junction), so there is no noun-preposition pair here — the trap is purely the pull of to from equivalents in other languages.

To become a member of something, or to come together with someone.

  1. She joined the volunteer fire department at nineteen.
  2. Join the line behind the woman with the folding chair.
  3. He joined the Navy two weeks after graduating.
  4. Won't you join us for dinner on Saturday?
  5. The kids eventually joined in the singing.
  6. The plumber joined the new line to the existing stack.
See also
contact someone, attend something, agreement with/on, contribution to

lack something#

Pronunciation
/læk ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ · lak SUM-thing · no preposition to reduce: lack it /læk ɪt/
Русский
не иметь чего-л., испытывать нехватку — глагол без of (✗ he lacks of experience)
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
a direct noun phrase, usually uncountable or plural (lacks funding, lacks options). It does not take of (✗ lacks of funding). The noun requires of: a lack of funding. The adjective requires in: lacking in detail.
Pattern
lack [noun phrase] · a lack of [noun phrase] · be lacking in [quality] · lack for [nothing] · for lack of [noun phrase]
Contrast
Three words in one family, three constructions, and no overlap. Verb: the report lacks detail — bare. Noun: a lack of detail. Adjective: the report is lacking in detail. ✗ The report lacks of detail is the error, and it is the standard transfer from French manquer de, Spanish carecer de, and Italian mancare di. The one place for belongs is the fixed litotes he lacked for nothing (he had everything he needed). See lack of.

To be without something needed.

  1. The proposal lacks any detail about who maintains the trail.
  2. He doesn't lack confidence; he lacks a permit.
  3. The building lacks a second exit, which is why it can't be used as a classroom.
  4. What the argument lacks is a single verifiable number.
  5. The estate was large and the children lacked for nothing.
  6. The whole plan is lacking in anything resembling a budget.
See also
lack of, need for, supply of, difficulty with/in

marry someone#

Pronunciation
/ˈmɛri ˈsʌmwʌn/ · MAIR-ee SUM-wun · no preposition to reduce: marry him /ˈmɛri ɪm/ in fast speech
Русский
жениться на ком-л., выходить замуж за — без предлога (✗ marry with her)
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct noun phrase naming the spouse. It does not take with or to (✗ marry with Tom, ✗ marry to Tom). The participial adjective does take to: married to Tom. The noun takes to as well: her marriage to Tom.
Pattern
marry [person] · get married to [person] · be married to [person] · marry into [a family] · marry off [a child] · the officiant married them
Variants
get married to — the everyday American form; marry alone is slightly more formal, and wed is headline English
Contrast
✗ Married with her is the most recognizable preposition error in English, carried in from Spanish casarse con, Italian sposarsi con, and Portuguese casar com. The English sequence is: marry Ø (she married Tom), married to (she is married to Tom), marriage to (her marriage to Tom). Married with children is correct only because with there means "having." Note that marry is also transitive in the officiant's sense (the judge married them) and takes into for joining a family (he married into money). See marriage to.

To take someone as a spouse, or to perform the ceremony.

  1. She married Tom in her mother's backyard in 1998.
  2. They got married to each other twice, ten years apart.
  3. He's been married to the same woman since he was twenty-two.
  4. A justice of the peace married them on a Tuesday morning.
  5. She married into a family that has run the hardware store for a century.
  6. Married with two kids and a thirty-year mortgage, he wasn't going anywhere.
See also
marriage to, relationship with/between, love for/of, connection with/to/between

mention something#

Pronunciation
/ˈmɛnʃən ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ · MEN-shun SUM-thing · no preposition to reduce: mention it /ˈmɛnʃən ɪt/
Русский
упоминать о чём-л. — англ. без предлога (✗ mention about it)
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct noun phrase, a gerund (mentioned seeing him), a that-clause (mentioned that the road was closed), or a wh-clause. It does not take about (✗ mention about the meeting). The recipient takes to: ✗ mention me the meeting.
Pattern
mention [noun phrase / verb-ing / that-clause] · mention [noun phrase] to [person] · not to mention [noun phrase] · make mention of [noun phrase] · don't mention it
Contrast
✗ Mention about it and ✗ mention me it are the two errors. The verb is bare and the recipient takes to: mention it to her. The noun, by contrast, requires of: he made no mention of the fee, there is a mention of it in the minutes — one more verb-bare / noun-prepositional pair. Not to mention is a fixed connector meaning "and also," and don't mention it is a response to thanks.

To refer to something briefly.

  1. He never mentioned the second appraisal.
  2. Did she mention the road closure to you?
  3. The realtor mentioned that the furnace was original to the house.
  4. Somebody mentioned seeing him at the hardware store on Sunday.
  5. The lease says nothing about pets, not to mention parking.
  6. There's no mention of the easement anywhere in the file.
See also
comment on, reference to, discuss something, describe something

phone someone#

Pronunciation
/foʊn ˈsʌmwʌn/ · fohn SUM-wun · no preposition to reduce: call her /kɔl ɚ/ in fast speech
Русский
звонить кому-л. — англ. без предлога (✗ phone to me)
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct noun phrase naming the person or office called. It does not take to (✗ phone to your mother, ✗ call to the office). Call on and call for exist as separate idioms with different meanings.
Pattern
phone / call [person] · call [person] about [matter] · give [someone] a call · call on [someone] (visit or ask to speak) · call for [something] (demand) · a call to [number / person]
Variants
call — the American default; phone and ring are more British, and ring someone up is not American English
Contrast
✗ Call to your mother is the error, produced by the noun, which does take to: a call to her mother. The verb is bare: call your mother. Two lookalikes mean other things: call on the mayor is to pay a visit or to invite her to speak, and call for an investigation is to demand one. Note that American English says call, uses phone mainly as a noun, and does not use ring in this sense at all.

To telephone a person or place.

  1. Call your mother before she calls the state police.
  2. I phoned the clinic three times and got a recording.
  3. Call the office about the missing invoice, not me.
  4. She gave him a call on the way home.
  5. The board called on the auditor to explain the discrepancy.
  6. There were four calls to the same number that night.
See also
contact someone, letter to/from, request for, reply to

protest something#

Pronunciation
/prəˈtɛst ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ · pruh-TEST SUM-thing · no preposition to reduce: protest it /prəˈtɛst ɪt/ — and note the stress shift, verb pro-TEST against noun PRO-test /ˈproʊtɛst/
Русский
протестовать против чего-л. — амер. без предлога; брит. protest against
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition in American English
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct noun phrase in American English (protest the ruling, protest the tuition increase), and a that-clause in the separate "insist" sense (he protested that he had never seen the file). British English keeps a preposition for the demonstration sense — protest against the ruling, protest at the delay — so protest against is not an error, simply British. The noun takes a preposition in both dialects: a protest against the ruling, a protest over pay.
Pattern
protest [noun phrase] · protest that [clause] · protest against / at [noun phrase] — British · a protest against / over [noun phrase] · in protest of [noun phrase] · under protest
Variants
protest Ø — American · protest against — British and Commonwealth for the demonstration sense · protest at — British, chiefly with a decision (protested at the delay)
Contrast
A genuine transatlantic split, not an error on either side. Americans protest the war; the British protest against the war — an American editor cuts the against, a British one puts it back. The noun is prepositional everywhere (a protest against the war), which is exactly where the against keeps creeping back into the American verb. Two American frames keep a preposition and are fixed: in protest of the ruling and under protest (unwillingly). Keep the "insist" sense separate: it takes a clause and no preposition in both dialects — she protested that the fee had already been paid, ✗ she protested about that the fee had been paid. Compare oppose, which is bare in both dialects while its noun takes to.

To object publicly to something, or to declare something in the face of doubt.

  1. Students protested the tuition hike for three straight weeks.
  2. Two hundred people protested outside the courthouse on Friday morning.
  3. He protested that he had never seen the memo before that morning.
  4. She signed the settlement under protest and filed an appeal the same day.
  5. The London papers reported that the crowd had protested against the closures.
  6. The union filed a formal protest against the new scheduling rule.
See also
objection to, opposition to, resistance to, complaint about, against

reach something#

Pronunciation
/ritʃ ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ · reech SUM-thing · no preposition to reduce: reach it /ritʃ ɪt/
Русский
достигать чего-л., добираться до; дозвониться до кого-л. — без предлога (✗ reach to)
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct noun phrase — a place, a person, a total, or an agreement. It does not take to (✗ reach to the summit, ✗ reach to a decision). Reach for means to stretch toward something, and reach out to is a separate idiom meaning to make contact.
Pattern
reach [place / person / total] · reach [an agreement / a decision / a verdict] · reach for [object] · reach out to [person] · out of reach · within reach of [noun phrase]
Contrast
✗ Reach to the summit is the error; the verb is bare — they reached the summit at noon. Reach for is a different action (he reached for the salt — he stretched toward it, whether or not he got it), and reach out to is American business idiom for making contact, now so common that it has crowded out plain contact. The nouns take of: out of reach, within reach of the freeway. Note the fixed collocations reach an agreement, reach a decision, reach a verdict, all bare.

To arrive at something, or to get in touch with someone.

  1. We reached the summit a little after noon.
  2. I couldn't reach her all weekend; her phone was off.
  3. The fund reached its target three days before the deadline.
  4. The two sides finally reached an agreement on the driveway.
  5. He reached for the flashlight and knocked over the lamp.
  6. The top shelf is out of reach for anybody under six feet.
See also
access to, contact someone, agreement with/on, limit to/on

request something#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈkwɛst ˈsʌmθɪŋ/ · rih-KWEST SUM-thing · no preposition to reduce: request it /rəˈkwɛst ɪt/
Русский
просить, запрашивать что-л. — глагол без предлога (✗ request for a copy)
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition
Register
formal
Complement
a direct noun phrase, a to-infinitive (requested to see the file), or a that-clause with the subjunctive (requested that the fee be waived). It does not take for (✗ request for a copy). The noun requires for: a request for a copy.
Pattern
request [noun phrase] · request to [verb] · request that [clause with subjunctive] · request [noun phrase] from [person] · a request for [noun phrase] · as requested
Variants
ask for — the everyday equivalent, and it does take for; the pair ask for / request Ø is the thing to memorize
Contrast
✗ Request for a copy as a verb is the error, and it is reinforced from both sides: the noun takes for (a request for a copy) and the plain synonym takes for (ask for a copy). Only the verb request is bare. Note the American subjunctive: requested that he be present, not ✗ that he is present. See request for.

To ask for something formally.

  1. We requested the inspection records in writing.
  2. She requested to see the original signature page.
  3. The court requested that all exhibits be filed by Friday.
  4. He requested a transfer to the night shift and got it.
  5. Copies may be requested from the clerk's office.
  6. The documents were produced as requested.
See also
request for, demand for, order for, permission for

resemble someone#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈzɛmbəl ˈsʌmwʌn/ · rih-ZEM-bul SUM-wun · no preposition to reduce: resemble him /rəˈzɛmbəl ɪm/
Русский
быть похожим на кого-л. — англ. без предлога (✗ resembles to his father)
Type
verb + direct object — zero preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
a direct noun phrase. It does not take to or with (✗ resembles to his father). The noun requires to: a resemblance to his father. The verb is not used in the progressive: ✗ he is resembling his father.
Pattern
resemble [person / thing] · bear a resemblance to [person] · a family resemblance · look like [person]
Variants
look like — the everyday equivalent, also bare (he looks like his father), and much commoner in speech
Contrast
✗ Resembles to his father is the error, imported from the noun (a resemblance to his father) and from Romance equivalents that are prepositional. The verb is bare: he resembles his father. Two further restrictions: it is a stative verb, so ✗ he is resembling his father is wrong, and it is somewhat formal — American speech prefers looks like, takes after, or has his father's eyes.

To look or be like someone or something.

  1. He resembles his father more every year.
  2. The finished porch resembled the drawing only in outline.
  3. Nothing in the final bill resembles what was introduced in January.
  4. She bears a striking resemblance to her grandmother at the same age.
  5. The two accounts resemble each other closely enough to be suspicious.
  6. There's an unmistakable family resemblance around the eyes.
See also
similarity to/between, comparison with/to, difference between/in, link between/to
